========================================================================
  MATHEMATICS, STATISTICS, COMPUTER SCIENCE AND DATA SCIENCE
  COMPREHENSIVE FORMULA AND CONCEPT REFERENCE
  (Built on the TU Dortmund MSc Data Science prerequisite syllabus:
   48 modules in Parts A-D)
========================================================================

PURPOSE
This file is a long-term learning and revision handbook. It is a formula
sheet, a mathematical reference, a concept explanation handbook and a
problem-solving reference in one. It is NOT a one-page cheat sheet.
It is written for someone REBUILDING foundations, so nothing is assumed
just because you "have seen it before".


------------------------------------------------------------------------
HOW TO READ THIS DOCUMENT
------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. Section numbers follow the syllabus. "MODULE n" is the syllabus
   module n. Sub-sections are numbered n.1, n.2, ...
2. Every formula or rule gets an ID "Fn.k" (n = module number) and, where
   it is a real formula, the ten-part template below.
3. Where a topic has NO formula (e.g. complexity classes, OOP) the
   concepts, rules, procedures and relationships are explained instead.
4. Warnings are marked  !! WARNING !!  and  !! COMMON MISTAKE !!
5. Material that goes beyond the syllabus but is needed to understand it
   is marked [SUPPLEMENTARY].
6. Items where the syllabus is ambiguous are marked [SYLLABUS NOTE].


------------------------------------------------------------------------
THE FORMULA TEMPLATE (A-J) USED THROUGHOUT
------------------------------------------------------------------------
 A. Formula name
 B. Formula
 C. Meaning of symbols
 D. Conceptual explanation (what, why, what problem it solves)
 E. Conditions and assumptions (when valid / invalid; misuse)
 F. Derivation (where it improves understanding)
 G. Worked examples (simple + moderately challenging)
 H. Interpretation of the result
 I. Common mistakes
 J. Related formulas (and when to prefer one over another)

Very elementary rules use the same lettered parts but in a compressed
form. Nothing is omitted; elementary parts are just short.


------------------------------------------------------------------------
NOTATION CONVENTIONS (used consistently everywhere)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Plain-text mathematics:
  x^2          x squared              x^(n-1)    x to the power n-1
  sqrt(x)      square root            |x|        absolute value
  a/b          a divided by b         (a+b)/(c+d) use brackets!
  *            multiplication (often omitted: 2x means 2*x)
  sum[i=1..n] x_i      sum of x_1 + x_2 + ... + x_n
  prod[i=1..n] x_i     product x_1 * x_2 * ... * x_n
  lim[x -> a] f(x)     limit of f(x) as x approaches a
  f'(x), df/dx         derivative;   d/dx = "derivative with respect to x"
  int[a..b] f(x) dx    definite integral of f from a to b
  partial f / partial x   partial derivative (written  df/dx  with
                       the word "partial" when ambiguity is possible)
  A^T                  transpose of A      A^(-1)  inverse of A
  det(A)               determinant         tr(A)   trace
  x_i  subscript i     x^(i)   superscript in brackets = i-th example
  E[X], Var(X), Cov(X,Y)   expectation, variance, covariance
  P(A)  probability of A;  P(A|B)  probability of A given B
  ~     "is distributed as"  (X ~ N(mu, sigma^2))
  mu, sigma, sigma^2   POPULATION mean, standard deviation, variance
  x_bar, s, s^2        SAMPLE mean, standard deviation, variance
  beta_hat (b-hat)     an ESTIMATE of beta computed from data
  in, subset, union (U), intersection (n), empty set {}
  =>  implies;   <=>  if and only if
  ln = natural log (base e);  log = base 10 unless a base is written;
  log_b(x) = log to base b.   In statistics/ML texts "log" usually means
  ln. This document writes ln explicitly whenever it matters.
  Vectors are column vectors by default; written (v1, v2, v3)^T or as
  [v1; v2; v3] (semicolons separate rows).
  Matrix rows are written [a b c ; d e f] (semicolon = new row).

Variance convention: "Var" with denominator n is the POPULATION version,
"s^2" with denominator n-1 is the SAMPLE version. Always check which one a
software library uses (NumPy np.var default: ddof=0 -> population;
pandas .var default: ddof=1 -> sample).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
SYLLABUS NOTES (ambiguities and how they were handled)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
[SYLLABUS NOTE 1] The syllabus is derived from the TU Dortmund
self-assessment. It lists topics, not depth. Where a bullet is only a
name (e.g. "Nelder-Mead method"), this document gives the idea, the
algorithm steps and its role, at a level suitable for prerequisite
understanding, not a full research treatment.

[SYLLABUS NOTE 2] "Order and degree" of a differential equation (Module
15): the ORDER is unambiguous (highest derivative). The DEGREE has a
convention (power of the highest derivative once the equation is a
polynomial in derivatives). Many modern texts do not use "degree". Both
are explained and the convention is stated.

[SYLLABUS NOTE 3] "Variance aggregation" and "Why medians cannot
generally be reconstructed" (Module 26) are given with exact formulas
and a counter-example.

[SYLLABUS NOTE 4] "Distance from a point to the origin" under
parametric curves (Module 14) is treated as the Euclidean norm of the
position vector r(t).

[SYLLABUS NOTE 5] The syllabus lists Python as the recommended
programming language for statistical computing (R is an alternative in
the original assessment). This document uses Python.

[SYLLABUS NOTE 6] Module 48 lists "Kernel methods", "SVM", "RBF kernel",
"LDA". These are covered with formulas and intuition, as prerequisites,
not full optimization theory.

[SYLLABUS NOTE 7] The syllabus text contains the study advice of the
earlier chat (time allocation, schedule). That is a study plan, not
mathematical content, and is not reproduced here except as the
supplementary pointers in Appendix D.

[SYLLABUS NOTE 8] Where a concept has two common conventions (sample
variance denominators, degrees of freedom, "log", row vs column vectors,
Python 0-based indexing) the convention used is stated at the point of
use.

A NOTE ON LEARNING LEVELS (from the syllabus)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Level 1 Familiarity: recognize the concept, purpose and where used.
Level 2 Competence : solve problems independently, derive key results,
                     state assumptions.
Level 3 Depth      : proofs, counterexamples, limitations, connections.
Aim: Level 2 across the core; Level 3 in selected foundational topics.
Each worked example in this file is meant to be redone by hand without
looking, then checked.

========================================================================
A1. CALCULUS
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 1: MATHEMATICAL FOUNDATIONS
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.1 THE REAL NUMBER SYSTEM AND INTERVALS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Numbers come in nested families. Calculus works on the REAL numbers,
which you can picture as every point on an infinitely long line with
no gaps.

THE FAMILIES
  N  natural numbers        1, 2, 3, ...   (some texts include 0)
  Z  integers               ..., -2, -1, 0, 1, 2, ...
  Q  rational numbers       fractions p/q, p and q integers, q != 0
                            (e.g. 3/4, -7, 0.125, 0.333...)
  R  real numbers           all points on the number line. Includes the
                            irrational numbers: sqrt(2), pi, e, which
                            cannot be written as p/q.
  Nesting: N subset Z subset Q subset R.
  Decimal view: rational numbers have decimals that terminate or repeat
  (1/3 = 0.333...). Irrational numbers have non-terminating,
  non-repeating decimals.

WHY "NO GAPS" MATTERS (completeness)
Between any two rationals there are other rationals, yet the rationals
still "miss" points like sqrt(2). The reals contain those points. This
is what lets limits exist (e.g. a sequence of rationals approaching
sqrt(2) has a limit in R, not in Q).

INTERVAL NOTATION
An interval is a connected set of real numbers.

  Notation     Set-builder form       Meaning
  (a, b)       {x : a < x < b}       open: endpoints NOT included
  [a, b]       {x : a <= x <= b}     closed: endpoints included
  [a, b)       {x : a <= x < b}      half-open (a in, b out)
  (a, b]       {x : a < x <= b}      half-open (a out, b in)
  (a, inf)     {x : x > a}           unbounded, open
  [a, inf)     {x : x >= a}          unbounded, closed at a
  (-inf, b)    {x : x < b}
  (-inf, inf)  R                     the whole line

  - Round bracket "(" or ")" = endpoint excluded.
    Square bracket "[" or "]" = endpoint included.
  - Infinity is NOT a number; infinity is always written with a round
    bracket because it can never be reached.
  - Set operations on intervals:
      union (U):         (1,3) U (2,5) = (1,5)
      intersection (n):  (1,3) n (2,5) = (2,3)
      [0,1] U (1,2)      = [0,2)       (the point 1 is covered once)
      (-inf,2) n [0,inf) = [0,2)

  WORKED EXAMPLE (simple): Write {x : -3 < x <= 4} as an interval:
      (-3, 4].
  WORKED EXAMPLE (moderate): Find (-inf, 5) n ([2, 8) U (-4, -1]).
      Step 1: the union [2,8) U (-4,-1] is two separate pieces:
              (-4,-1] and [2,8).
      Step 2: intersect each piece with (-inf,5):
              (-4,-1] n (-inf,5) = (-4,-1]
              [2,8)  n (-inf,5) = [2,5)
      Answer: (-4, -1] U [2, 5).

  !! COMMON MISTAKE !! Writing [2, inf]. Always write [2, inf).
  !! COMMON MISTAKE !! Writing (3, 1) for "from 3 down to 1"; the
  smaller number goes first: (1, 3).

DATA SCIENCE LINK: intervals are the language of confidence intervals,
domains of probability density functions, clipping ranges and
normalization ranges such as [0, 1].


------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.2 ABSOLUTE VALUES AND INEQUALITIES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F1.1  ABSOLUTE VALUE (definition)

A. NAME: Absolute value (modulus).

B. FORMULA
     |x| =  x     if x >= 0
     |x| = -x     if x <  0
   Equivalent: |x| = sqrt(x^2).

C. SYMBOLS
   x     any real number.
   |x|   the absolute value of x (a non-negative number).
   sqrt  the non-negative square root.

D. CONCEPT
   |x| is the DISTANCE from x to 0 on the number line, ignoring
   direction. Likewise |x - a| is the distance between x and a. So
   |x - 3| = 5 means "x is 5 units from 3": x = 8 or x = -2.

E. CONDITIONS
   Defined for all real numbers. Beware: sqrt(x^2) = |x|, NOT x.
   (e.g. sqrt((-3)^2) = sqrt(9) = 3 = |-3|.)

F. DERIVATION
   sqrt(x^2) is by definition the NON-NEGATIVE number whose square is
   x^2. If x >= 0 that number is x; if x < 0 it is -x. That is the
   two-case definition.

G. EXAMPLES
   Simple:   |-7| = 7, |4| = 4, |0| = 0, |3 - 8| = |-5| = 5.
   Moderate: Evaluate |x - 2| + |x + 1| at x = 0.5.
             |0.5 - 2| = |-1.5| = 1.5; |0.5 + 1| = 1.5; total = 3.

H. INTERPRETATION: |a - b| is the distance between a and b. In data
   science, absolute error |y - y_hat| is exactly this distance between
   truth and prediction (the basis of MAE).

I. COMMON MISTAKES
   - |a + b| is NOT |a| + |b| in general (see triangle inequality).
   - |-x| = |x|, but "-|x|" is always <= 0. Do not confuse them.

J. RELATED: triangle inequality (F1.3), L1 norm in linear algebra
   (sum of absolute values), MAE metric (Module 46).


F1.2  ABSOLUTE-VALUE INEQUALITY RULES

A. NAME: Absolute-value inequality rules (for a > 0).
B. FORMULAS
     |x| < a    <=>   -a < x < a            (a single interval)
     |x| <= a   <=>   -a <= x <= a
     |x| > a    <=>   x < -a   OR   x > a   (two separate rays)
     |x| >= a   <=>   x <= -a  OR   x >= a
   Shifted: |x - c| < a  <=>  c - a < x < c + a.

C. SYMBOLS: a is a positive constant (a "tolerance" or radius); c is
   the center; <=> means "if and only if".

D. CONCEPT: "distance from c is less than a" means x lies within a of c:
   the open interval (c-a, c+a). "Distance greater than a" means x is
   outside that interval: two pieces.

E. CONDITIONS: a > 0. If a < 0: |x| < a has NO solution (a distance
   cannot be negative); |x| > a is true for ALL x. If a = 0, |x| < 0 has
   no solution, |x| <= 0 has only x = 0.

F. DERIVATION (for |x| < a): case x >= 0 gives x < a. Case x < 0 gives
   -x < a, i.e. x > -a. Combining: -a < x < a.

G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: |x| < 3  ->  (-3, 3).
   Moderate 1: |2x - 3| < 7.
        -7 < 2x - 3 < 7  -> add 3: -4 < 2x < 10 -> divide by 2:
        -2 < x < 5.   Solution (-2, 5).
   Moderate 2: |x - 1| >= 3.
        x - 1 <= -3  OR  x - 1 >= 3  ->  x <= -2  OR  x >= 4.
        Solution (-inf, -2] U [4, inf).
   Moderate 3 (negative multiplier): |4 - 3x| <= 8.
        -8 <= 4 - 3x <= 8 -> subtract 4: -12 <= -3x <= 4 ->
        divide by -3 and FLIP both inequalities: 4 >= x >= -4/3.
        Solution [-4/3, 4].

H. INTERPRETATION: |x - c| < a describes "x is within tolerance a of c".
   A 95% confidence interval  estimate +/- margin  is exactly the
   statement |theta - estimate| < margin.

I. COMMON MISTAKES
   - Forgetting to FLIP the inequality when multiplying/dividing by a
     negative number.
   - Writing |x| > 3 as -3 > x > 3 (impossible). Correct: x < -3 OR x > 3.
   - Treating "OR" as "AND" (intersection vs union).

J. RELATED: epsilon-delta definition of limits (Module 2) is written
   with exactly these inequalities.

F1.3  TRIANGLE INEQUALITY
B. FORMULA:  |a + b| <= |a| + |b|     and     | |a| - |b| | <= |a - b|.
C. SYMBOLS: a, b real numbers (also holds for vectors with norms).
D. CONCEPT: going directly from 0 to a+b can never be longer than
   going 0 -> a -> a+b. Equality when a and b have the same sign
   (or one is 0).
E. CONDITIONS: all real a, b.
G. EXAMPLE: a = 3, b = -5: |a+b| = 2 <= |a|+|b| = 8 (strict).
              a = 3, b = 5: 8 <= 8 (equality).
H. INTERPRETATION: a detour is never shorter than the direct path; it is
   used to bound errors, e.g. |e1 + e2| <= |e1| + |e2|, and to prove
   limit laws.
I. MISTAKE: assuming equality. J. Related: Cauchy-Schwarz and vector
   norms (Module 10), error bounds in numerical analysis.
J. RELATED: for vectors |u + v| <= |u| + |v| (F6.3, F10.1); the second
   form is the reverse triangle inequality.


------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.3 ALGEBRAIC EXPRESSIONS AND SIMPLIFICATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA: Algebra is a set of rules for rewriting an expression into
an equivalent but more useful form without changing its value. Every
rule below is a statement "left side = right side for all allowed
values".

THE FIELD RULES (the foundation of all algebra)
  For all real a, b, c:
    Commutative:   a + b = b + a ;   a*b = b*a
    Associative:   (a+b)+c = a+(b+c) ;  (a*b)*c = a*(b*c)
    Distributive:  a*(b + c) = a*b + a*c
    Identities:    a + 0 = a ;  a * 1 = a
    Inverses:      a + (-a) = 0 ;  a * (1/a) = 1 for a != 0
  Consequences: a*0 = 0;  (-a)(-b) = ab;  (-1)a = -a;
  division by zero is NOT defined.

  Matrix multiplication is NOT commutative (Module 6): this is one of
  the places where "obvious" real-number rules fail.

ORDER OF OPERATIONS: brackets; exponents; multiplication/division
(left to right); addition/subtraction (left to right).
  -3^2 = -(3^2) = -9 (the minus is applied last), but (-3)^2 = 9.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
F1.4  EXPONENT (INDEX) LAWS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
A. NAME: Laws of exponents.

B. FORMULAS (a, b != 0 where division/negative powers appear)
     a^m * a^n = a^(m+n)
     a^m / a^n = a^(m-n)
     (a^m)^n   = a^(m*n)
     (a*b)^n   = a^n * b^n
     (a/b)^n   = a^n / b^n
     a^0       = 1           (a != 0)
     a^(-n)    = 1 / a^n
     a^(1/n)   = n-th root of a
     a^(m/n)   = (n-th root of a)^m = n-th root of (a^m)

C. SYMBOLS: a, b = bases (real numbers); m, n = exponents (integers or
   rationals here; extended to reals later). "n-th root of a" is the
   number that, raised to n, gives a.

D. CONCEPT: a^n means "multiply a by itself n times". The laws just
   count factors: a^3 * a^2 = (a*a*a)*(a*a) = a^5. Negative exponents
   mean "divide instead"; the fractional exponent 1/n is DEFINED so the
   first law still works: (a^(1/n))^n = a^1.

E. CONDITIONS
   - a^0 = 1 requires a != 0 (0^0 is an indeterminate form in limits;
     in many programming/combinatorial contexts it is DEFINED as 1;
     Python: 0**0 = 1).
   - For even n, the n-th root of a negative number is not real.
   - (a^m)^n = a^(mn) is NOT a^(m^n).
   - (a + b)^n != a^n + b^n.

F. DERIVATION (why a^0 = 1): a^n / a^n = 1 for a != 0. By the quotient
   law it equals a^(n-n) = a^0. So a^0 must be 1 for the law to hold.
   Why a^(-n) = 1/a^n: a^0 / a^n = 1/a^n and by the quotient law
   = a^(0-n) = a^(-n).

G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: 2^3 * 2^4 = 2^7 = 128. 5^(-2) = 1/25 = 0.04.
           27^(2/3) = (cube root of 27)^2 = 3^2 = 9.
   Moderate: Simplify (4x^3 y^(-2))^2 / (2x y^3)^3.
        Numerator: 4^2 x^6 y^(-4) = 16 x^6 y^(-4).
        Denominator: 2^3 x^3 y^9 = 8 x^3 y^9.
        Ratio: (16/8) x^(6-3) y^(-4-9) = 2 x^3 y^(-13) = 2x^3 / y^13.

H. INTERPRETATION: exponent rules convert multiplication into addition
   of exponents. This is the seed idea of logarithms (F1.12).

I. COMMON MISTAKES
   - (a+b)^2 = a^2 + b^2 (WRONG; correct: a^2 + 2ab + b^2).
   - 2^3 * 3^3 = 6^6 (WRONG). Different bases do not combine: 2^3*3^3 =
     (2*3)^3 = 216.
   - a^m + a^n = a^(m+n) (WRONG).
   - sqrt(a + b) = sqrt(a) + sqrt(b) (WRONG).

J. RELATED: logarithm laws (F1.12), exponential function (F1.11).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
F1.5  ROOTS AND RATIONALIZING
------------------------------------------------------------------------
B. FORMULAS
     sqrt(a*b) = sqrt(a) * sqrt(b)          (a, b >= 0)
     sqrt(a/b) = sqrt(a) / sqrt(b)          (a >= 0, b > 0)
     sqrt(a^2) = |a|
     1 / sqrt(a)  =  sqrt(a) / a            (a > 0)   [rationalizing]
     1 / (sqrt(a) - sqrt(b)) = (sqrt(a) + sqrt(b)) / (a - b)
                                             (a != b, a, b >= 0)
C. SYMBOLS: sqrt(.) principal (non-negative) square root.
D. CONCEPT: rationalizing removes roots from a denominator using the
   conjugate: multiplying (sqrt(a) - sqrt(b)) by (sqrt(a) + sqrt(b))
   gives a - b by the difference of squares. It is also the key
   algebraic technique for limits like 0/0 forms with roots (Module 2).
E. CONDITIONS: sqrt(ab) = sqrt(a) sqrt(b) requires a, b >= 0.
   sqrt((-4)(-9)) = sqrt(36) = 6, but sqrt(-4) sqrt(-9) is not defined
   in the reals.
G. EXAMPLE: 6 / (sqrt(5) - sqrt(2)) = 6 (sqrt(5) + sqrt(2)) / (5 - 2)
   = 2 (sqrt(5) + sqrt(2)).
H. INTERPRETATION: rationalizing turns a hard-to-read form into one
   where cancellation shows: 6/(sqrt5 - sqrt2) = 2(sqrt5 + sqrt2) =
   7.30; it is the algebra behind many limits.
I. MISTAKE: assuming sqrt(x^2 + y^2) = x + y.
J. RELATED: difference of squares (F1.6); limit technique 2 in section
   2.5.


------------------------------------------------------------------------
F1.6  SPECIAL PRODUCTS AND FACTORIZATION IDENTITIES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
A. NAME: Standard algebraic identities.
B. FORMULAS (all hold for all real a, b)
     (a + b)^2 = a^2 + 2ab + b^2
     (a - b)^2 = a^2 - 2ab + b^2
     a^2 - b^2 = (a - b)(a + b)                 [difference of squares]
     (a + b)^3 = a^3 + 3a^2 b + 3a b^2 + b^3
     (a - b)^3 = a^3 - 3a^2 b + 3a b^2 - b^3
     a^3 + b^3 = (a + b)(a^2 - ab + b^2)         [sum of cubes]
     a^3 - b^3 = (a - b)(a^2 + ab + b^2)         [difference of cubes]
     a^n - b^n = (a - b)(a^(n-1) + a^(n-2) b + ... + b^(n-1))
C. SYMBOLS: a, b any real numbers (or algebraic expressions).
D. CONCEPT: these are the distributive law applied repeatedly.
   Geometric picture of (a+b)^2: a square of side a+b is split into an
   a-by-a square, a b-by-b square and two a-by-b rectangles -> the
   middle term 2ab.
E. CONDITIONS: the identities hold for all real numbers and for any
   algebraic expressions in place of a and b; a^2 + b^2 has no real
   factorization; a^n + b^n factors over the reals only for odd n: a^n +
   b^n = (a + b)(a^(n-1) - a^(n-2) b + ... + b^(n-1)).
F. DERIVATION: (a+b)^2 = (a+b)(a+b) = a*a + a*b + b*a + b*b
   = a^2 + 2ab + b^2.  (a-b)(a+b) = a^2 + ab - ba - b^2 = a^2 - b^2.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: 101^2 = (100+1)^2 = 10000 + 200 + 1 = 10201.
           99*101 = (100-1)(100+1) = 10000 - 1 = 9999.
   Moderate: Factorize 8x^3 - 27: = (2x)^3 - 3^3
           = (2x - 3)(4x^2 + 6x + 9).
           Check by expanding: 2x*4x^2 = 8x^3; 2x*6x = 12x^2;
           2x*9 = 18x; -3*4x^2 = -12x^2; -3*6x = -18x; -27.
           Sum: 8x^3 - 27. OK.
H. INTERPRETATION: factorizing reveals zeros of expressions and allows
   cancellation of common factors in limits (Module 2).
I. MISTAKES: forgetting the middle term; sign errors in a^3 + b^3
   (the second factor is a^2 - ab + b^2, with MINUS ab).
   a^2 + b^2 does NOT factorize over the reals.
J. RELATED: binomial theorem (F1.7), quadratic formula (below).

F1.7  BINOMIAL THEOREM
B. FORMULA
     (a + b)^n = sum[k=0..n] C(n,k) a^(n-k) b^k
     C(n,k) = n! / (k! (n-k)!)
   Pascal's triangle: C(n,k) = C(n-1,k-1) + C(n-1,k).
C. SYMBOLS: n non-negative integer; k = 0,1,...,n; C(n,k) "n choose k"
   (also written with n over k in brackets); n! = n(n-1)...2*1,
   0! = 1.
D. CONCEPT: expanding (a+b)^n means choosing, from each of n brackets,
   either a or b. The coefficient of a^(n-k) b^k is the number of ways
   to choose which k brackets supply a b: C(n,k).
E. CONDITIONS: integer n >= 0 for a finite expansion. For non-integer n
   there is an infinite series version valid for |x| < 1 (Module 5).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: (x+1)^3 = x^3 + 3x^2 + 3x + 1.
   Moderate: (x + 2)^4: C(4,k) = 1,4,6,4,1. Terms:
        x^4 + 4*2 x^3 + 6*4 x^2 + 4*8 x + 16
        = x^4 + 8x^3 + 24x^2 + 32x + 16.
        Check x=1: 3^4 = 81 and 1+8+24+32+16 = 81. OK.
H. INTERPRETATION: the same coefficients count combinations (Module 33)
   and underlie the binomial distribution (Module 35).
I. MISTAKES: forgetting that b^k carries its own coefficient (e.g. 2^k
   above); sign alternation for (a-b)^n.
J. RELATED: combinations (F33.x), Taylor series (Module 5).

F1.8  QUADRATIC FORMULA AND COMPLETING THE SQUARE
A. NAME: Quadratic formula.
B. FORMULA: solutions of  a x^2 + b x + c = 0  (a != 0) are
     x = ( -b +/- sqrt(b^2 - 4ac) ) / (2a)
   Discriminant D = b^2 - 4ac.
   Vieta: sum of roots = -b/a ; product of roots = c/a.
C. SYMBOLS: a, b, c are real coefficients; D tells how many real roots.
   "+/-" means two solutions: one with +, one with -.
D. CONCEPT: a parabola y = a x^2 + b x + c crosses the x-axis where y=0.
   D > 0: two distinct real roots; D = 0: one repeated root;
   D < 0: no real roots (complex pair).
E. CONDITIONS: a != 0. If a = 0 the equation is linear.
F. DERIVATION (completing the square)
     a x^2 + b x + c = 0
     divide by a:           x^2 + (b/a) x + c/a = 0
     move constant:         x^2 + (b/a) x = -c/a
     add (b/(2a))^2:        (x + b/(2a))^2 = b^2/(4a^2) - c/a
                                           = (b^2 - 4ac)/(4a^2)
     take roots:            x + b/(2a) = +/- sqrt(b^2-4ac)/(2a)
     isolate x:             the formula.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: x^2 - 5x + 6 = 0: a=1,b=-5,c=6; D = 25-24 = 1;
           x = (5 +/- 1)/2 = 3 or 2. Check sum 5 = -b/a, product 6 = c/a.
   Moderate: 2x^2 + 3x - 5 = 0: D = 9 + 40 = 49; x = (-3 +/- 7)/4
           = 1 or -5/2. Factorization: (x - 1)(2x + 5). OK.
           No real roots example: x^2 + 2x + 5 = 0: D = 4 - 20 = -16 < 0.
H. INTERPRETATION: D < 0 means the parabola never touches the axis; in
   optimization (Module 12) the sign pattern of a similar quantity
   classifies critical points.
I. MISTAKES: sign of -b when b is negative (-(-5) = +5); dividing only
   part of the numerator by 2a; losing the factor 4 in 4ac.
J. RELATED: completing the square also gives vertex form; polynomial
   factorization; eigenvalues of 2x2 matrices solve a quadratic (F9.x).


------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.4 FUNCTIONS: DOMAIN, CODOMAIN AND RANGE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA: A function is a rule that assigns to each allowed input
EXACTLY ONE output. A machine: put x in, get f(x) out; the same x
always gives the same result.

FORMAL: f : X -> Y  means f assigns to each x in X exactly one y = f(x)
in Y.
  Domain   X  : the set of allowed inputs.
  Codomain Y  : the set in which outputs are declared to live.
  Range (image) f(X) = { f(x) : x in X } : the outputs that actually
                      occur. Range is a SUBSET of codomain.
  Example: f(x) = x^2 as f : R -> R. Domain R, codomain R, range
  [0, inf) (squares are never negative).

[SYLLABUS NOTE] Some textbooks use "range" for what is here called
"codomain". This document uses range = image (actual outputs).

HOW TO FIND A DOMAIN (checklist)
  1. Denominator must not be zero.
  2. Expression under an even root must be >= 0.
  3. Argument of a logarithm must be > 0.
  4. Other restrictions from the context (e.g. a probability must be in
     [0,1]; a count is a non-negative integer).

THE VERTICAL LINE TEST: a curve is the graph of a function iff no
vertical line meets it more than once.

ONE-TO-ONE (injective): different inputs give different outputs.
  f(a) = f(b) => a = b.  Horizontal line test: no horizontal line meets
  the graph more than once. f(x) = x^2 on R is NOT one-to-one
  (f(2) = f(-2)); on [0, inf) it is.
ONTO (surjective): range = codomain.
BIJECTIVE: both. Only bijections have inverses on the whole codomain.

EXAMPLES
  Simple: f(x) = 1/(x - 3). Domain: x != 3, i.e. (-inf,3) U (3,inf).
          Range: all y != 0.
  Moderate 1: f(x) = sqrt(x - 2)/(x - 5).
          Need x - 2 >= 0 -> x >= 2 and x != 5.
          Domain [2, 5) U (5, inf).
  Moderate 2: g(x) = ln(4 - x^2). Need 4 - x^2 > 0 -> x^2 < 4
          -> |x| < 2. Domain (-2, 2). Range: since 0 < 4 - x^2 <= 4,
          ln takes values (-inf, ln 4]. Range (-inf, ln 4].
  Moderate 3: h(x) = 2x^2 - 8x + 3 on R: complete the square:
          2(x - 2)^2 - 5. Minimum -5 at x = 2. Range [-5, inf).

!! COMMON MISTAKE !! Canceling a factor and forgetting the removed
point: f(x) = (x^2 - 1)/(x - 1) equals x + 1 only for x != 1. Its
domain excludes 1 (the graph has a "hole").

DATA SCIENCE LINK: a model is a function f : features -> predictions.
Its domain is the feature space; knowing the domain tells you where the
model's output is meaningful (extrapolation outside the training
domain is dangerous). Sigmoid: domain R, range (0,1) - which is why it
can be read as a probability.


------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.5 COMPOSITION AND INVERSE FUNCTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F1.9  COMPOSITION OF FUNCTIONS
B. FORMULA:   (f o g)(x) = f( g(x) ).
C. SYMBOLS: g is applied FIRST, then f. "o" is the composition symbol.
D. CONCEPT: chaining machines: output of g becomes input of f. Neural
   networks are long compositions: f_L(...f_2(f_1(x))...). The chain
   rule (Module 3) differentiates compositions.
E. CONDITIONS: g(x) must lie in the domain of f. Order matters:
   f o g is generally different from g o f.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: f(x) = x^2 + 1, g(x) = 3x - 2.
      (f o g)(x) = (3x-2)^2 + 1 = 9x^2 - 12x + 5.
      (g o f)(x) = 3(x^2+1) - 2 = 3x^2 + 1.   Different!
   Moderate: f(x) = sqrt(x), g(x) = x - 4. (f o g)(x) = sqrt(x - 4),
      domain x >= 4. (g o f)(x) = sqrt(x) - 4, domain x >= 0.
H. INTERPRETATION: the domain of f o g is the set of x in dom(g)
   such that g(x) is in dom(f).
I. MISTAKE: reading (f o g) as "f times g" or applying them in the
   wrong order.
J. RELATED: the chain rule (F3.7) differentiates compositions; inverse
   functions undo composition (F1.10); neural networks are compositions
   of layers.

F1.10  INVERSE FUNCTION
A. NAME: Inverse function.
B. FORMULA: f^(-1) is the function with
       f^(-1)( f(x) ) = x   for all x in dom(f),
       f( f^(-1)(y) ) = y   for all y in range(f).
   Procedure: write y = f(x); solve for x in terms of y; swap names.
C. SYMBOLS: f^(-1) means "inverse function", NOT 1/f.
D. CONCEPT: the inverse UNDOES f. Graph of f^(-1) is the graph of f
   reflected in the line y = x.
E. CONDITIONS: f must be one-to-one on the domain considered. If not,
   restrict the domain (sqrt is the inverse of x^2 on [0, inf)).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: f(x) = 3x + 2 -> y = 3x + 2 -> x = (y - 2)/3 ->
           f^(-1)(x) = (x - 2)/3.
   Moderate: f(x) = (2x + 3)/(x - 1) (x != 1).
        y(x - 1) = 2x + 3 -> yx - y = 2x + 3 -> x(y - 2) = y + 3
        -> x = (y + 3)/(y - 2). So f^(-1)(x) = (x + 3)/(x - 2).
        Check: f(f^(-1)(x)) = x (verified algebraically: numerator
        2(x+3)+3(x-2) = 5x; denominator (x+3)-(x-2) = 5; ratio x).
   Key pairs: exp and ln are inverses: ln(e^x) = x (all x),
        e^(ln x) = x (x > 0).
H. INTERPRETATION: the inverse of a model's transformation recovers
   the original scale, e.g. exp undoes a log transform of a skewed
   target variable (careful: this introduces bias for means).
I. MISTAKES: f^(-1)(x) = 1/f(x) (WRONG). Forgetting domain restrictions.
J. RELATED: logarithm as inverse of exponential; logit (inverse of
   sigmoid) in logistic regression.


------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.6 EXPONENTIAL AND LOGARITHMIC FUNCTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F1.11  EXPONENTIAL FUNCTION
A. NAME: Exponential function with base b.
B. FORMULA: f(x) = b^x, b > 0, b != 1. Natural exponential: e^x.
C. SYMBOLS: b = base (constant); x = the exponent (the variable).
D. CONCEPT: constant PROPORTIONAL growth: each unit step multiplies by
   b. Growth if b > 1, decay if 0 < b < 1. Differs from a power
   function x^n where the variable is the BASE.
E. CONDITIONS: b > 0 (so that b^x is defined for all real x).
   Domain R, range (0, inf), always positive, one-to-one.
   b^(x+y) = b^x b^y; b^(-x) = 1/b^x.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: 2^5 = 32; (1/2)^3 = 1/8.
   Moderate: Bacteria double every 3 hours, start 500. After t hours:
        N(t) = 500 * 2^(t/3). At t = 12: 500 * 2^4 = 8000.
H. INTERPRETATION: exponential growth eventually outpaces any
   polynomial; also the source of exponential-time algorithms
   (Module 17).
I. MISTAKE: x^2 (power) vs 2^x (exponential): derivative rules differ.
J. RELATED: the inverse is the logarithm (F1.12); base e gives d/dx e^x
   = e^x (F3.8); exponential growth ODE y' = k y (F16.3).

F1.12  LOGARITHM
A. NAME: Logarithm to base b.
B. FORMULA
     y = log_b(x)   <=>   b^y = x        (b > 0, b != 1, x > 0)
   Laws (b > 0, b != 1; x, y > 0):
     log_b(xy)    = log_b(x) + log_b(y)
     log_b(x/y)   = log_b(x) - log_b(y)
     log_b(x^k)   = k log_b(x)
     log_b(1) = 0 ;   log_b(b) = 1
     b^(log_b x)  = x ;   log_b(b^x) = x
     Change of base:  log_b(x) = ln(x) / ln(b) = log_c(x)/log_c(b)
C. SYMBOLS: log_b(x) is the exponent to which b must be raised to get
   x. ln = log base e.
D. CONCEPT: logs convert MULTIPLICATION into ADDITION and POWERS into
   MULTIPLICATION. They answer "how many times do I multiply by b to
   reach x?". Hence they compress huge ranges (pH, decibels, orders of
   magnitude) and turn products of probabilities into sums.
E. CONDITIONS: x > 0 only. Logs of 0 or negative numbers are undefined
   (real logs). Domain (0, inf), range R.
   Laws do NOT apply to log(x + y) (no rule!).
F. DERIVATION (product law): let m = log_b x, n = log_b y, so
   x = b^m, y = b^n. Then xy = b^m b^n = b^(m+n), hence
   log_b(xy) = m + n. Change of base: let y = log_b x, so b^y = x.
   Take ln of both sides: y ln b = ln x -> y = ln x / ln b.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: log_2(8) = 3 (2^3 = 8). log_10(0.001) = -3.
           log_2(32) - log_2(4) = 5 - 2 = 3 = log_2(8).
   Moderate 1: Solve 3^(2x-1) = 20.
        Take ln: (2x - 1) ln 3 = ln 20
        2x - 1 = ln 20 / ln 3 = 2.99573 / 1.09861 = 2.72682
        x = (2.72682 + 1)/2 = 1.8634 (4 d.p.).
   Moderate 2: log_5(20) = ln 20 / ln 5 = 2.99573/1.60944 = 1.8614.
   Moderate 3: Solve ln(x) + ln(x - 3) = ln 10 (x > 3).
        ln(x(x-3)) = ln 10 -> x^2 - 3x - 10 = 0 -> (x-5)(x+2)=0.
        x = 5 or -2. Only x = 5 satisfies the domain x > 3.
        (REJECT x = -2: ln(-2) is undefined.)
H. INTERPRETATION: log likelihood turns the product of n independent
   densities into a sum (Module 38). Log-scale plots show
   multiplicative change as equal steps.
I. MISTAKES
   - log(x + y) = log x + log y (WRONG).  - (log x)^2 vs log(x^2).
   - log x / log y = log(x - y) (WRONG; it is a change of base only).
   - Forgetting to check the domain after solving log equations.
J. RELATED: exponent laws (F1.4); derivative of ln x = 1/x (Module 3);
   entropy and cross-entropy use log (Modules 44, 48).


------------------------------------------------------------------------
1.7 THE NATURAL LOGARITHM AND EULER'S NUMBER e
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F1.13  EULER'S NUMBER e
A. NAME: Euler's number (the base of natural logarithms).
B. FORMULA
     e = lim[n -> inf] (1 + 1/n)^n = 2.718281828...
     e = sum[k=0..inf] 1/k! = 1 + 1 + 1/2 + 1/6 + 1/24 + ...
     e^x = lim[n -> inf] (1 + x/n)^n
C. SYMBOLS: n = number of compounding periods (a whole number going to
   infinity); k! = k factorial.
D. CONCEPT: Suppose 1 unit of money earns 100% interest per year.
   Compounding once: 2. Twice: (1.5)^2 = 2.25. n times: (1 + 1/n)^n.
   As n grows this approaches a limit, e. So e is the result of
   CONTINUOUS growth at 100% per unit time. It is the unique base for
   which the exponential function equals its own derivative:
   d/dx e^x = e^x (Module 3). That makes calculus formulas simplest
   in base e: the reason "natural".
E. CONDITIONS: the limit exists and is finite.
G. EXAMPLES
   Numerical: n = 1: 2; n = 10: 2.5937; n = 100: 2.7048;
              n = 1000: 2.7169; n -> inf: 2.71828...
   Compound interest: invest 1000 at 6% for 3 years.
     Annual compounding: 1000 (1.06)^3 = 1191.02.
     Continuous compounding: 1000 e^(0.06*3) = 1000 e^0.18 = 1197.22.
   Decay: N(t) = N0 e^(-lambda t); half-life T: e^(-lambda T) = 1/2 ->
          T = ln 2 / lambda.
H. INTERPRETATION: continuous compounding is always slightly above
   discrete; growth rate parameter in e^(rt) is the instantaneous
   relative growth rate r.
I. MISTAKES: treating e as a variable; e^x vs x^e; thinking ln means
   log base 10.
J. RELATED: natural log ln(x) = log_e(x), inverse of e^x. Normal
   distribution density contains e^(-z^2/2) (Module 35); exponential
   distribution (Module 35); softmax uses e^x; ODE growth/decay
   (Module 16).

F1.14  NATURAL LOGARITHM PROPERTIES
B. ln(x) = log_e(x); ln(e) = 1; ln(1) = 0; ln(e^x) = x; e^(ln x) = x;
   ln(a^b) = b ln a; ln(a/b) = ln a - ln b.
   Useful approximation: for small h, ln(1 + h) approx h (error is of
   order h^2; Module 5 Taylor series). Also e^h approx 1 + h.
C. SYMBOLS: x a real number (x > 0 for ln x); h a small real number; b a
   positive base; e = 2.71828...; ln the natural logarithm.
D. CONCEPT: ln converts products to sums and is the inverse of e^x; near
   0 the curves ln(1 + h) and e^h are almost straight lines tangent at 0
   (first-order Taylor, F5.6), so ln(1 + h) is about h and e^h about 1 +
   h.
E. CONDITIONS: ln x needs x > 0; the approximations are accurate only
   for small |h| (error about h^2/2); for |h| above about 0.3 use the
   exact values.
G. EXAMPLE: ln(1.02) approx 0.02 (true value 0.0198026...). So a 2%
   increase corresponds to a log-change of about 0.0198: log returns
   approximate percentage returns for small changes.
   Doubling time at 5% growth: ln 2 / ln 1.05 = 0.693147/0.048790
   = 14.21 periods (the "rule of 70": about 70/5 = 14).
H. INTERPRETATION: log returns add over time while simple returns
   multiply: +10% then -10% gives 0.99 (-1%), and the log returns
   +0.0953 and -0.1054 sum to -0.0101 = ln 0.99.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using ln(1 + h) approx h for large h (ln 2 = 0.693,
   not 1); confusing ln with log base 10 in software (np.log is the
   natural log, np.log10 is base 10).
J. RELATED: Taylor series of ln(1 + x) (F5.6); logarithm laws (F1.12);
   log-likelihood (F38.2).


========================================================================
MODULE 2: LIMITS AND CONTINUITY
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
2.1 CONCEPT OF A LIMIT
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
A limit answers: "What value is f(x) getting closer and closer to as x
gets closer and closer to a number a?" It is about the TREND near a, not
the value AT a. The function does not even need to be defined at a.

NOTATION:  lim[x -> a] f(x) = L
  Read "the limit of f(x) as x approaches a equals L".
  Symbols: x -> a means x gets arbitrarily close to a (but we never
  require x = a). L is a real number (the limit value).

EXAMPLE FOR INTUITION: f(x) = (x^2 - 1)/(x - 1) is undefined at x = 1
(0/0). For x != 1, f(x) = x + 1. Values: f(0.9) = 1.9; f(0.99) = 1.99;
f(1.01) = 2.01; f(1.1) = 2.1. So lim[x -> 1] f(x) = 2 even though
f(1) does not exist.

F2.1  FORMAL (EPSILON-DELTA) DEFINITION  [Level 3 material]
B. lim[x -> a] f(x) = L  means: for every epsilon > 0 there exists a
   delta > 0 such that  0 < |x - a| < delta  implies  |f(x) - L| < epsilon.
C. SYMBOLS: epsilon = how close to L we demand f(x) to be (an output
   tolerance); delta = how close x must be to a to guarantee it (an
   input tolerance). "0 < |x-a|" excludes x = a itself.
D. CONCEPT: It is a challenge-response game. Someone gives you any
   output tolerance epsilon, however tiny; you must reply with an input
   window delta that keeps f within epsilon of L.
E. CONDITIONS: epsilon > 0 is arbitrary; delta may depend on epsilon and
   on the point a but not on x; f must be defined near a (except
   possibly at a).
G. EXAMPLE: Prove lim[x -> 3] (2x + 1) = 7. |f(x) - 7| = |2x - 6|
   = 2|x - 3|. Given epsilon, choose delta = epsilon/2. If
   0 < |x - 3| < delta, then |f(x) - 7| = 2|x-3| < 2 delta = epsilon.
H. INTERPRETATION: it makes 'gets arbitrarily close' precise; every
   limit proof is a recipe for choosing delta from epsilon.
I. MISTAKE: letting delta depend on x; delta may depend only on epsilon
   (and a).
J. RELATED: sequence limits (F5.1) use the same idea with N instead of
   delta; continuity (F2.6) is the case L = f(a).

F2.2  BASIC LIMIT LAWS
B. If lim f = L and lim g = M (both finite, as x -> a) and c constant:
     lim [f + g] = L + M            lim [f - g] = L - M
     lim [c f]   = c L              lim [f * g] = L M
     lim [f / g] = L / M            (only if M != 0)
     lim [f(x)]^n = L^n             lim sqrt(f) = sqrt(L)  (L >= 0)
   Substitution rule for continuous functions: if f is continuous at a,
   lim[x -> a] f(x) = f(a).
C. SYMBOLS: L, M real numbers. D. CONCEPT: limits "pass through"
   arithmetic as long as every individual limit exists.
D. CONCEPT: limits respect arithmetic: the limit of a sum, product or
   quotient is the sum, product or quotient of the limits, so most
   limits of nice functions are found by substitution.
E. CONDITIONS: ALL the separate limits must exist. For a quotient the
   denominator's limit must be non-zero; if both limits are 0 you have
   an indeterminate form (2.5) and must do more work.
G. EXAMPLE: lim[x -> 2] (3x^2 - 5x + 1) = 3*4 - 10 + 1 = 3 (polynomial,
   continuous, so just substitute).
H. INTERPRETATION: justifies 'plugging in' for continuous functions; a
   failure (0/0) signals an indeterminate form (section 2.4).
I. MISTAKE: splitting a limit when one piece does not exist, e.g.
   lim (sin(1/x) * x) cannot be handled by splitting
   lim sin(1/x) (does not exist). (Squeeze theorem needed; F2.5.)
J. RELATED: epsilon-delta (F2.1), continuity (F2.6), L'Hopital (F2.5).


------------------------------------------------------------------------
2.2 ONE-SIDED (LEFT-HAND AND RIGHT-HAND) LIMITS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
B. NOTATION
     lim[x -> a-] f(x)   left-hand limit  (x approaches a from below)
     lim[x -> a+] f(x)   right-hand limit (x approaches a from above)
   THEOREM: lim[x -> a] f(x) = L   <=>   both one-sided limits exist and
   both equal L.
D. CONCEPT: on the number line you can sneak up on a from two sides. If
   the two sides disagree, there is no (two-sided) limit.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: f(x) = |x|/x (x != 0). For x > 0, f = 1; for x < 0, f = -1.
      Left limit at 0 is -1; right limit is +1; they differ, so
      lim[x -> 0] |x|/x does NOT exist.
   Moderate (piecewise): f(x) = x + 1 for x < 2; f(x) = 5 - x for x >= 2.
      Left: lim[x -> 2-] (x + 1) = 3. Right: lim[x -> 2+] (5 - x) = 3.
      Both equal 3, so the limit is 3. Also f(2) = 5 - 2 = 3, therefore
      f is continuous at 2.
H. INTERPRETATION: one-sided limits detect jumps (e.g. a step
   function; the empirical CDF jumps at each data point).
I. MISTAKES: reading f(a) off as the limit; ignoring that the two sides
   must match.
J. RELATED: continuity (2.7), CDFs (right-continuous, Module 35).


------------------------------------------------------------------------
2.3 LIMITS AT INFINITY AND INFINITE LIMITS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
LIMITS AT INFINITY: lim[x -> inf] f(x) = L means f(x) approaches L as x
grows without bound. Gives HORIZONTAL asymptotes y = L.

INFINITE LIMITS: lim[x -> a] f(x) = inf means f(x) grows without bound
as x -> a. Gives VERTICAL asymptotes x = a. "inf" describes behavior;
the limit does not "exist" as a real number.

STANDARD LIMITS (n > 0 constant)
     lim[x -> inf] 1/x^n = 0
     lim[x -> inf] a^x = inf  (a > 1);   = 0 (0 < a < 1)
     lim[x -> -inf] e^x = 0;   lim[x -> inf] e^x = inf
     lim[x -> inf] ln x = inf;  lim[x -> 0+] ln x = -inf
     lim[x -> 0+] 1/x = +inf;  lim[x -> 0-] 1/x = -inf
     Growth ranking as x -> inf:  ln x  <<  x^p  <<  a^x  <<  x!
     (p > 0, a > 1;  "<<" = grows much more slowly than).

TECHNIQUE FOR RATIONAL FUNCTIONS at infinity: divide numerator and
denominator by the highest power of x in the denominator.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: lim[x -> inf] (3x^2 + 5)/(2x^2 - x) = divide by x^2:
           (3 + 5/x^2)/(2 - 1/x) -> 3/2.
   Moderate: lim[x -> inf] (sqrt(x^2 + 4x) - x). Multiply by conjugate:
           (x^2 + 4x - x^2)/(sqrt(x^2+4x) + x) = 4x/(sqrt(x^2+4x) + x).
           Divide by x (x>0, sqrt(x^2+4x)/x = sqrt(1 + 4/x)):
           4/(sqrt(1 + 4/x) + 1) -> 4/2 = 2.
   Degree rule: for polynomials P (degree p) and Q (degree q):
           p < q -> limit 0;  p = q -> ratio of leading coefficients;
           p > q -> +/- infinity.
I. MISTAKE: writing inf - inf = 0 (it is indeterminate; see 2.5).
J. RELATED: Big-O (Module 17) is a statement about growth as n -> inf.
   Sigmoid limits: sigma(x) -> 1 as x -> inf, -> 0 as x -> -inf.


------------------------------------------------------------------------
2.4 INDETERMINATE FORMS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Forms where knowing the limits of the pieces does NOT determine the
limit of the whole:
     0/0,  inf/inf,  0 * inf,  inf - inf,  1^inf,  0^0,  inf^0.
"Indeterminate" does not mean "undefined" - it means "more work needed":
the answer could be 0, a finite number, or infinity, depending on how
fast each piece goes to its limit.
NOT indeterminate: 1/0 (type a/0 with a != 0 gives +/- infinity or DNE),
inf + inf = inf, 0/inf = 0, inf * inf = inf.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
2.5 ALGEBRAIC TECHNIQUES FOR EVALUATING LIMITS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
TECHNIQUE 1: Factor and cancel (0/0 with polynomials).
   lim[x -> 3] (x^2 - 9)/(x - 3) = lim (x - 3)(x + 3)/(x - 3)
   = lim (x + 3) = 6.
TECHNIQUE 2: Conjugate (roots).
   lim[x -> 4] (sqrt(x) - 2)/(x - 4) = lim 1/(sqrt(x) + 2) = 1/4.
   (Multiply top and bottom by sqrt(x) + 2: top becomes x - 4.)
TECHNIQUE 3: Common denominator (fractions).
   lim[x -> 0] (1/(x+2) - 1/2)/x = lim [(2 - (x+2))/(2(x+2))]/x
   = lim -x/(2(x+2)x) = -1/4.
TECHNIQUE 4: Divide by highest power (at infinity) (see 2.3).
TECHNIQUE 5: Squeeze (Sandwich) theorem.
F2.3  SQUEEZE THEOREM
B. If g(x) <= f(x) <= h(x) near a and lim g = lim h = L, then lim f = L.
C. SYMBOLS: f the function of interest; g (lower) and h (upper) the
   bounding functions; a the point; L the common limit.
D. CONCEPT: f is trapped between two functions that squeeze to L.
G. EXAMPLE: lim[x -> 0] x sin(1/x). Since -1 <= sin(1/x) <= 1:
   -|x| <= x sin(1/x) <= |x|; both bounds -> 0, so the limit is 0.
E. NOTE: g <= f <= h must hold on a neighbourhood of a (not
   everywhere necessarily).
H. INTERPRETATION: trap a hard function between two easy ones; it proves
   lim sin(x)/x = 1 and lim x sin(1/x) = 0.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: bounds that do not converge to the same L; using the
   theorem when the inequality holds only at isolated points.
J. RELATED: standard limits (F2.4); comparison tests for series (F5.4).
TECHNIQUE 6: Known special limits
F2.4  IMPORTANT STANDARD LIMITS
     lim[x -> 0] sin(x)/x = 1            (x in RADIANS)
     lim[x -> 0] (1 - cos x)/x = 0
     lim[x -> 0] (e^x - 1)/x = 1
     lim[x -> 0] ln(1 + x)/x = 1
     lim[x -> inf] (1 + 1/x)^x = e
     lim[x -> 0] (1 + x)^(1/x) = e
   Use: the derivative of sin x and of e^x are built from these.
   !! WARNING !! sin(x)/x -> 1 only in radians.
C. SYMBOLS: x the variable (in radians for the trigonometric limits); e
   = 2.71828...; ln the natural logarithm.
D. CONCEPT: near 0, sin x is about x, e^x about 1 + x, and ln(1 + x)
   about x (the functions look like their tangent lines); these limits
   are exactly the derivatives of sin, exp and ln at 0.
E. CONDITIONS: x tends to 0 (or infinity for the last limit); angles in
   radians.
G. EXAMPLES: lim[x->0] sin(3x)/x = 3 lim sin(3x)/(3x) = 3. lim[x->0]
   (e^(2x) - 1)/x = 2. lim[x->inf] (1 + 2/x)^x = e^2.
H. INTERPRETATION: small-angle approximation sin x ~ x (at x = 0.1, sin
   x = 0.09983); for small relative changes ln(1 + h) ~ h (F1.14).
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using degrees; forgetting the constant when the
   argument is not x (sin(3x)/x tends to 3, not 1).
J. RELATED: derivations of d/dx sin x and d/dx e^x (F3.8); Taylor series
   (F5.6).
TECHNIQUE 7: L'Hopital's rule  [SUPPLEMENTARY: uses derivatives from
Module 3]
F2.5  L'HOPITAL'S RULE
B. If lim f(x)/g(x) is of the form 0/0 or inf/inf, and f, g are
   differentiable near a (g' != 0 near a), then
        lim f(x)/g(x) = lim f'(x)/g'(x)
   provided the RIGHT-hand limit exists (or is +/- infinity).
C. SYMBOLS: f' and g' are derivatives of top and bottom SEPARATELY (not
   the quotient rule).
D. CONCEPT: when f/g is 0/0 or inf/inf, the ratio is governed by how
   fast numerator and denominator change, so compare their derivatives
   (rates) instead.
E. CONDITIONS: only for 0/0 or inf/inf. Applying it otherwise gives
   wrong answers. Other indeterminate forms must be converted first
   (0*inf -> f/(1/g); 1^inf, 0^0, inf^0 via logarithms).
G. EXAMPLES: lim[x -> 0] (e^x - 1 - x)/x^2: 0/0 -> (e^x - 1)/(2x): 0/0
   again -> e^x/2 -> 1/2.
   lim[x -> inf] ln(x)/x: inf/inf -> (1/x)/1 -> 0 (so ln x grows slower
   than x).
H. INTERPRETATION: ln x grows slower than x (ln x / x tends to 0);
   polynomials grow slower than exponentials.
I. MISTAKE: applying the quotient rule to f/g inside L'Hopital's rule.
   Using it when the form is not 0/0 or inf/inf, e.g.
   lim[x -> 0] (x+1)/(x+2) = 1/2 (just substitute).
J. RELATED: indeterminate forms (section 2.4), growth ranking (section
   2.3), Taylor series as an alternative (F5.6).

WORKED EXAMPLE (challenging): lim[x -> 0] (sqrt(1 + x) - 1)/x.
  Conjugate: (1 + x - 1)/(x (sqrt(1+x) + 1)) = 1/(sqrt(1+x) + 1) -> 1/2.
  L'Hopital check: derivative of top 1/(2 sqrt(1+x)) -> 1/2. Same.
  Interpretation: this is the derivative of sqrt(1+x) at 0, i.e. f'(0).


------------------------------------------------------------------------
2.6 DISCONTINUITY TYPES (preview for 2.7)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Removable   : lim exists but f(a) differs or is undefined (a "hole").
                Fixable by redefining f(a).  Example (x^2-1)/(x-1).
  Jump        : left and right limits both exist but differ.
                Example floor function, sign(x), step function.
  Infinite    : f -> +/- infinity (vertical asymptote). 1/x at 0.
  Oscillating : no limit because of unbounded oscillation. sin(1/x)
                at 0.


------------------------------------------------------------------------
2.7 CONTINUITY AND ITS RELATION TO LIMITS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F2.6  CONTINUITY AT A POINT
A. NAME: Continuity of f at x = a.
B. FORMULA: f is continuous at a if ALL THREE hold:
     (1) f(a) is defined;
     (2) lim[x -> a] f(x) exists;
     (3) lim[x -> a] f(x) = f(a).
   Equivalent: lim[x -> a] f(x) = f(lim[x -> a] x)  ("you may pass the
   limit inside").
C. SYMBOLS: f(a) is the actual value at a.
D. CONCEPT: The graph can be drawn through a without lifting the pen.
   Small input changes cause small output changes.
E. CONDITIONS: continuity on an interval means continuity at each of its
   points (one-sided at endpoints). Polynomials, exp, sin, cos are
   continuous on R; ln on (0, inf); rational functions are continuous
   wherever the denominator is non-zero; sums, products, compositions
   and quotients (denominator != 0) of continuous functions are
   continuous.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: f(x) = x^2 is continuous at every a (limit = a^2 = f(a)).
   Moderate: Find k so that f(x) = (x^2 - 4)/(x - 2) for x != 2 and
        f(2) = k is continuous at 2.
        lim[x -> 2] (x+2) = 4. So need k = 4.
   Moderate 2: f(x) = { 3x + 1 (x < 1);  a x^2 - 2 (x >= 1) } continuous
        at 1? Left limit 4. Right value a - 2. Need a - 2 = 4 -> a = 6.
H. INTERPRETATION: the Intermediate Value Theorem and Extreme Value
   Theorem require continuity.
I. MISTAKES: assuming "limit exists" implies "continuous"; the limit
   might not equal f(a). Assuming the converse: continuity does NOT
   imply differentiability (|x| at 0, Module 3).
J. RELATED: differentiability implies continuity (Module 3).

F2.7  INTERMEDIATE VALUE THEOREM (IVT) AND EXTREME VALUE THEOREM
B. IVT: if f is continuous on [a, b] and N is any number between f(a)
   and f(b), there exists c in [a, b] with f(c) = N.
   EVT: if f is continuous on a CLOSED, BOUNDED interval [a, b], f
   attains an absolute maximum and an absolute minimum on [a, b].
C. SYMBOLS: f a continuous function; [a, b] a closed bounded interval; N
   a number between f(a) and f(b); c a point in the interval.
D. CONCEPT: a continuous function cannot skip values; and on a closed
   interval it cannot "approach" an extreme without reaching it.
G. EXAMPLE (IVT, root existence): f(x) = x^3 + x - 1 on [0, 1]:
   f(0) = -1 < 0 < f(1) = 1, f continuous, so a root exists in (0, 1).
   Bisection method uses this (Module 43 context).
E. Fails when f is not continuous or the interval is open/unbounded:
   f(x) = 1/x on (0,1] has no maximum.
H. INTERPRETATION: IVT justifies root existence and the bisection
   method; EVT guarantees that a continuous objective on a compact set
   has a minimizer.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: applying IVT to a discontinuous function (1/x on
   [-1, 1] changes sign but has no root) or EVT to an open interval.
J. Related: Newton-Raphson and bisection root finding; optimization
   existence arguments (continuous loss on a compact set has a minimum).

DATA SCIENCE LINK (Modules 1-2): sigmoid and softmax are continuous;
ReLU is continuous but not differentiable at 0; the 0/1 classification
loss is discontinuous (jump), which is why smooth surrogate losses are
used for gradient-based training.


========================================================================
MODULE 3: DIFFERENTIATION
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.1 DERIVATIVES FROM FIRST PRINCIPLES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
A derivative measures HOW FAST a quantity is changing at one exact
instant. Average speed over a trip is "distance / time". But a
speedometer shows speed AT A MOMENT. To get that, we take the average
rate over a shorter and shorter time window and see what number it
settles toward. That limiting number is the derivative.

MATHEMATICAL MEANING
For y = f(x), the average rate of change between x and x + h is
   (f(x + h) - f(x)) / h        (the "difference quotient")
This is the slope of the SECANT line through (x, f(x)) and
(x+h, f(x+h)). Shrink h toward 0: the secant line pivots into the
TANGENT line, whose slope is the instantaneous rate of change.

F3.1  DERIVATIVE (DEFINITION)
A. NAME: Derivative of f at x, from first principles.

B. FORMULA
     f'(x) = lim[h -> 0] ( f(x + h) - f(x) ) / h
   Equivalent at a specific point a:
     f'(a) = lim[x -> a] ( f(x) - f(a) ) / ( x - a )
   Other notations: df/dx, dy/dx, d/dx [f(x)], Df(x).

C. SYMBOLS
   f(x)    the function; f'(x) its derivative (also a function of x).
   h       a small non-zero change in the input.
   f(x+h) - f(x)   change in output ("rise").
   (...)/h   rise over run = slope of the secant.
   lim[h -> 0]   the value the quotient approaches as h shrinks.
   dy/dx   Leibniz notation: "infinitesimal change in y per
           infinitesimal change in x". It is a single symbol for the
           derivative, not literally a fraction (though it behaves like
           one in the chain rule).

D. CONCEPTUAL EXPLANATION
   What it represents: the slope of the tangent line; the
   instantaneous rate of change; the best LINEAR approximation of f near
   x. Units: (units of f) per (unit of x): meters per second, dollars
   per item, change in loss per unit change in a weight.
   Problem solved: slope of a curve at a single point cannot be found by
   "rise over run" using one point, since rise and run are both 0. The
   limit resolves this 0/0 by studying a family of secants.

E. CONDITIONS AND ASSUMPTIONS
   - The limit must EXIST and be finite. If it does not, f is not
     differentiable at that point.
   - f must be defined near x (both sides).
   - Differentiable => continuous. CONVERSE FALSE: f(x) = |x| is
     continuous at 0 but not differentiable (left slope -1, right slope
     +1: a corner). Similarly a vertical tangent (cube root at 0) or a
     cusp gives no derivative. ReLU(x) = max(0,x) is not
     differentiable at 0; frameworks use a sub-gradient (conventionally
     0 or 1 at that point).

F. DERIVATION OF f'(x) FOR f(x) = x^2
   f(x+h) - f(x) = (x+h)^2 - x^2 = 2xh + h^2.
   Divide by h: 2x + h. Let h -> 0: f'(x) = 2x.

G. WORKED EXAMPLES
   Simple: f(x) = x^2 at x = 3.
     (f(3+h) - f(3))/h = ((3+h)^2 - 9)/h = (6h + h^2)/h = 6 + h -> 6.
     Tangent slope at x = 3 is 6. Tangent line: y - 9 = 6(x - 3),
     i.e. y = 6x - 9.
   Moderate: f(x) = 1/x, x != 0.
     (1/(x+h) - 1/x)/h = ( (x - (x+h)) / (x(x+h)) ) / h
                       = -h / (x(x+h)h) = -1/(x(x+h)) -> -1/x^2.
     So f'(x) = -1/x^2. At x = 2, slope -1/4 (a decreasing curve).
   Moderate 2: f(x) = sqrt(x) (x > 0): multiply by the conjugate:
     (sqrt(x+h) - sqrt(x))/h = 1/(sqrt(x+h) + sqrt(x)) -> 1/(2 sqrt(x)).

H. INTERPRETATION
   f'(a) > 0: f is rising at a (the steeper, the larger). f'(a) < 0:
   falling. f'(a) = 0: flat tangent: a candidate for a maximum, minimum
   or plateau/saddle point.
   Position s(t): s'(t) = velocity; s''(t) = acceleration.

I. COMMON MISTAKES
   - Forgetting to cancel h before taking the limit (gives 0/0).
   - Using (f(x+h))^2 instead of f(x+h) squared correctly; expand
     carefully.
   - Treating dy/dx as a literal fraction in contexts where it is not
     (but chain rule "canceling" works as a mnemonic).
   - Confusing f'(a) (number) with f'(x) (function).
   - Believing continuous implies differentiable.

J. RELATED: the rules below (3.3) are shortcuts derived from this
   definition. The multivariable generalization is the gradient
   (Module 12).


------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.2 GEOMETRIC INTERPRETATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F3.2  TANGENT LINE AND LINEAR APPROXIMATION
A. NAME: Tangent line equation; linearization.
B. FORMULA
     Tangent at x = a:   y = f(a) + f'(a) (x - a)
     Linear approximation: f(x) approx f(a) + f'(a)(x - a), for x near a.
     Differential: dy = f'(x) dx.
C. SYMBOLS: (a, f(a)) point of tangency; f'(a) slope; (x - a) the step
   away from a.
D. CONCEPT: close enough to a, a smooth curve is almost a straight line.
   The tangent is the straight line that "hugs" the curve best at a.
E. CONDITIONS: f differentiable at a. Approximation is good only for
   small |x - a|; error is about (1/2) f''(a) (x-a)^2 (Taylor, Module 5).
F. DERIVATION: slope-point form of a line with slope f'(a) through
   (a, f(a)).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: f(x) = x^2, a = 3: tangent y = 9 + 6(x - 3).
   Moderate: Estimate sqrt(4.1). f(x) = sqrt(x), a = 4, f(4) = 2,
     f'(4) = 1/(2*2) = 0.25. sqrt(4.1) approx 2 + 0.25*0.1 = 2.025.
     True value 2.02485... (error about 0.00015, tiny).
H. INTERPRETATION: derivatives give the sensitivity: a change dx in the
   input changes the output by about f'(x) dx.
I. MISTAKE: using a linear approximation far from a.
J. RELATED: Newton-Raphson iteration x_new = x - f(x)/f'(x) (Module 43)
   is "follow the tangent to its x-intercept"; Taylor polynomials
   (Module 5); gradient descent (first-order local model).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.3 DIFFERENTIATION RULES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F3.3  CONSTANT, CONSTANT-MULTIPLE AND SUM RULES
B. d/dx [c] = 0
   d/dx [c f(x)] = c f'(x)
   d/dx [f(x) + g(x)] = f'(x) + g'(x)        (and for differences)
C. SYMBOLS: c a constant; f, g differentiable functions.
D. CONCEPT: differentiation is LINEAR: it acts term by term and pulls out
   constants.
E. CONDITIONS: f and g differentiable at x.
F. DERIVATION: from the limit laws (F2.2), since the difference quotient
   of f+g is the sum of the difference quotients.
G. EXAMPLE: d/dx [5x^3 - 4x + 7] = 15x^2 - 4.
H. INTERPRETATION: derivatives of sums and multiples are computed term
   by term: differentiation is a linear operator.
I. MISTAKE: the product and quotient are NOT term-by-term: (fg)' !=
   f'g'.
J. RELATED: linearity of integration (F4.3) and of expectation (F36.2).

F3.4  POWER RULE
A. NAME: Power rule.
B. FORMULA:   d/dx [ x^n ] = n x^(n-1)
C. SYMBOLS: n is any real constant (integer, negative, fraction); x the
   variable (x > 0 or such that x^n is defined/differentiable).
D. CONCEPT: the slope of x^n at x is n times the "one lower power". For
   n=2: the curve x^2 at x has slope 2x: steeper the further out.
   Why: when x grows by h, x^n changes by about n x^(n-1) h
   (one of the n factors changes, n ways to choose which).
E. CONDITIONS: the variable is in the BASE and the exponent is a
   CONSTANT. For a CONSTANT base and variable exponent (2^x) the
   power rule does NOT apply (use F3.8). For x^x neither applies (use
   logarithmic differentiation, F3.9).
F. DERIVATION for positive integer n (binomial theorem F1.7):
     (x + h)^n = x^n + n x^(n-1) h + C(n,2) x^(n-2) h^2 + ...
     ( (x+h)^n - x^n ) / h = n x^(n-1) + (terms with h) -> n x^(n-1).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: d/dx x^5 = 5x^4;  d/dx sqrt(x) = d/dx x^(1/2)
           = (1/2) x^(-1/2) = 1/(2 sqrt(x));  d/dx (1/x^3)
           = d/dx x^(-3) = -3 x^(-4) = -3/x^4.
   Moderate: f(x) = 4 x^(3/2) - 6/x + 2 sqrt(x) at x = 4.
        Rewrite: 4x^(3/2) - 6x^(-1) + 2x^(1/2).
        f'(x) = 6 x^(1/2) + 6 x^(-2) + x^(-1/2).
        At x = 4: 6*2 + 6/16 + 1/2 = 12 + 0.375 + 0.5 = 12.875.
H. INTERPRETATION: the sign and size of 12.875 say f is rising steeply
   at x = 4: increasing x by 0.01 raises f by about 0.129.
I. MISTAKES: bringing the exponent down but forgetting to subtract 1;
   differentiating 1/x^3 as 1/(3x^2); using the power rule on 2^x.
J. RELATED: chain rule extends it to (g(x))^n.

F3.5  PRODUCT RULE
B. FORMULA: d/dx [ f(x) g(x) ] = f'(x) g(x) + f(x) g'(x)
C. SYMBOLS: f, g differentiable; f' and g' their derivatives.
D. CONCEPT: a rectangle with sides f and g has area fg. When both sides
   change a little, the area change is (change in f)*g + f*(change in
   g) + a negligible corner term of order (change)^2. Dividing by dx
   gives the rule.
E. CONDITIONS: both differentiable. NOT (fg)' = f'g'.
F. DERIVATION:
     f(x+h)g(x+h) - f(x)g(x)
       = [f(x+h) - f(x)] g(x+h) + f(x) [g(x+h) - g(x)]
   Divide by h; as h -> 0, g(x+h) -> g(x) (continuity).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: d/dx [x^2 e^x] = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x = e^x (x^2 + 2x).
   Moderate: d/dx [ (3x + 1) ln x ] = 3 ln x + (3x + 1)/x
           = 3 ln x + 3 + 1/x.
H. INTERPRETATION: sensitivity of a product splits into the two
   contributions.
I. MISTAKE: (fg)' = f'g'. Forgetting one of the two terms.
J. RELATED: quotient rule is the product rule with 1/g.

F3.6  QUOTIENT RULE
B. FORMULA: d/dx [ f(x)/g(x) ] = ( f'(x) g(x) - f(x) g'(x) ) / ( g(x) )^2
C. SYMBOLS: f numerator, g denominator, g(x) != 0.
D. CONCEPT: product rule applied to f * g^(-1) with the chain rule.
   Mnemonic: "low d-high minus high d-low, over low squared".
E. CONDITIONS: g(x) != 0. ORDER of the numerator matters (minus sign).
F. DERIVATION: f/g = f * g^(-1);
   (f g^(-1))' = f' g^(-1) + f * (-g^(-2) g') = (f' g - f g')/g^2.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: d/dx [ x / (x^2 + 1) ] =
        ( 1*(x^2+1) - x*2x ) / (x^2+1)^2 = (1 - x^2)/(x^2 + 1)^2.
        Zero at x = +/-1: extrema of x/(x^2+1) at x = 1 (max 1/2) and
        x = -1 (min -1/2).
   Moderate: d/dx [ tan x ] = d/dx [ sin x / cos x ]
        = (cos x cos x - sin x (-sin x))/cos^2 x = 1/cos^2 x = sec^2 x.
H. INTERPRETATION: the sign of f'g - f g' tells whether the ratio rises
   or falls; for x/(x^2 + 1) it is zero at x = +/-1, the extrema.
I. MISTAKES: swapping the order in the numerator; forgetting to square
   the denominator; differentiating top and bottom separately.
J. RELATED: for 1/g: (1/g)' = -g'/g^2.

F3.7  CHAIN RULE
A. NAME: Chain rule (derivative of a composition).
B. FORMULA:   d/dx [ f( g(x) ) ] = f'( g(x) ) * g'(x)
   Leibniz form: if y = f(u), u = g(x):   dy/dx = (dy/du) * (du/dx).
C. SYMBOLS: g is the inner function; f the outer; f'(g(x)) means "the
   derivative of f evaluated at g(x)".
D. CONCEPT: rates multiply along a chain. If y changes 3 times as fast as
   u and u changes 2 times as fast as x, then y changes 6 times as fast
   as x. This is the engine of back-propagation in neural networks.
E. CONDITIONS: g differentiable at x and f differentiable at g(x).
F. DERIVATION (idea): f(g(x+h)) - f(g(x)) =
   [f(g(x+h)) - f(g(x))]/[g(x+h) - g(x)] * [g(x+h) - g(x)], then divide
   by h and use g(x+h) - g(x) -> 0. (A fully rigorous proof handles the
   case g(x+h) = g(x).)
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: d/dx (3x^2 + 1)^5 = 5 (3x^2 + 1)^4 * 6x = 30x (3x^2+1)^4.
   Moderate: d/dx e^(-x^2/2) = e^(-x^2/2) * (-x). (This is the shape of
        the normal density derivative.)
   Moderate 2 (three layers): d/dx sin( ln( x^2 + 1 ) )
        = cos(ln(x^2+1)) * 1/(x^2+1) * 2x.
   Sigmoid: s(x) = 1/(1 + e^(-x)) = (1 + e^(-x))^(-1).
        s'(x) = -(1 + e^(-x))^(-2) * (-e^(-x)) = e^(-x)/(1 + e^(-x))^2
        = s(x) (1 - s(x)).
        At x = 0: s = 1/2, s' = 1/4.
H. INTERPRETATION: sensitivities multiply as signals pass through
   layers. In deep networks a product of many small derivatives can
   shrink toward 0 (vanishing gradients) - e.g. sigmoid' <= 1/4.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting the inner derivative; evaluating f' at x instead
   of g(x); stopping after one layer of a nested function.
J. RELATED: multivariable chain rule (Module 12).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.4 DERIVATIVES OF EXPONENTIAL, LOGARITHMIC AND TRIGONOMETRIC FUNCTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F3.8  DERIVATIVE TABLE (memorize; each derived from the rules)
     d/dx e^x       = e^x
     d/dx a^x       = a^x ln a                    (a > 0)
     d/dx ln x      = 1/x                          (x > 0)
     d/dx ln|x|     = 1/x                          (x != 0)
     d/dx log_a x   = 1/(x ln a)
     d/dx sin x     = cos x
     d/dx cos x     = -sin x
     d/dx tan x     = sec^2 x = 1/cos^2 x
     d/dx arctan x  = 1/(1 + x^2)
     d/dx sqrt(x)   = 1/(2 sqrt(x))
     d/dx 1/x       = -1/x^2
   Chain-rule versions:
     d/dx e^(g(x)) = g'(x) e^(g(x));   d/dx ln(g(x)) = g'(x)/g(x).
C. SYMBOLS: trig functions use RADIANS.
D. CONCEPT: e^x is its own derivative: the growth rate of e^x equals its
   current size (definition of "natural" exponential growth).
E. CONDITIONS: ln x and 1/x need x > 0 (or x != 0); a^x needs a > 0;
   trigonometric derivatives assume radians; tan x needs cos x != 0.
F. DERIVATION of d/dx e^x: (e^(x+h) - e^x)/h = e^x (e^h - 1)/h -> e^x * 1
   using lim (e^h - 1)/h = 1 (F2.4).
   d/dx ln x: if y = ln x then e^y = x; differentiate implicitly:
   e^y y' = 1 -> y' = 1/e^y = 1/x.
   d/dx a^x: a^x = e^(x ln a), chain rule gives ln a * e^(x ln a).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: d/dx 5e^(3x) = 15 e^(3x).  d/dx ln(2x) = 2/(2x) = 1/x.
   Moderate: d/dx [ ln(x^2 + 4x) ] = (2x + 4)/(x^2 + 4x) at x = 2:
        (8)/(12) = 2/3.
   Moderate 2: d/dx [x * ln x - x] = ln x + 1 - 1 = ln x. (So
        x ln x - x is an antiderivative of ln x; Module 4.)
H. INTERPRETATION: e^x is the only function (up to a constant multiple)
   equal to its own derivative; ln x has slope 1/x (slope 1 at x = 1).
I. MISTAKES: d/dx e^x = x e^(x-1) (WRONG: power rule misuse);
   d/dx 2^x = x 2^(x-1) (WRONG; correct 2^x ln 2);
   d/dx ln(x^2) = 1/x^2 (WRONG; correct 2/x). Degrees instead of
   radians in trig derivatives.
J. RELATED: logistic regression derivative; log-likelihood gradients
   (Module 38); softmax.

F3.9  LOGARITHMIC DIFFERENTIATION
B. If y = f(x)^(g(x)) or a long product/quotient: take ln of both sides,
   differentiate implicitly:
       ln y = g(x) ln f(x)    =>   y'/y = ( g(x) ln f(x) )'
       y' = y * ( g(x) ln f(x) )'.
C. SYMBOLS: y the function; f(x) > 0 the base and g(x) the exponent;
   y'/y = (ln y)' is the logarithmic derivative (relative rate of
   change).
D. CONCEPT: logs turn powers and products into sums that are easy to
   differentiate.
E. CONDITIONS: f(x) > 0 (or use |f|).
G. EXAMPLES: y = x^x (x > 0). ln y = x ln x; y'/y = ln x + 1;
   y' = x^x (ln x + 1). At x = 1: y = 1, y' = 1*(0 + 1) = 1.
   Moderate: y = x^2 (x+1)^3 / (2x - 1)^4. ln y = 2 ln x + 3 ln(x+1)
   - 4 ln(2x-1);  y'/y = 2/x + 3/(x+1) - 8/(2x-1).
H. INTERPRETATION: y'/y is the relative (percentage) rate of change, so
   products become sums of relative rates.
I. MISTAKE: using the power rule (x^x)' = x * x^(x-1).
J. RELATED: log-likelihood differentiates sums, not products, for the
   same reason (Module 38).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.5 IMPLICIT DIFFERENTIATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F3.10  IMPLICIT DIFFERENTIATION
A. NAME: Implicit differentiation.
B. PROCEDURE: for a relation F(x, y) = 0 that defines y as a
   (local) function of x:
     1. Differentiate both sides with respect to x, treating y as y(x)
        so that d/dx [y^n] = n y^(n-1) * y'  (chain rule).
     2. Collect all terms with y' on one side.
     3. Solve for y'.
C. SYMBOLS: y' = dy/dx.
D. CONCEPT: some curves (circles) cannot be written y = f(x) globally,
   but they still have a well-defined slope at most points.
E. CONDITIONS: slope formula usually has y in the denominator; it fails
   (vertical tangent) where that denominator is 0.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: x^2 + y^2 = 25. 2x + 2y y' = 0 -> y' = -x/y.
        At (3, 4): y' = -3/4. Tangent: y - 4 = -(3/4)(x - 3).
        The slope is perpendicular to the radius (slope 4/3): product
        -1. OK.
   Moderate: Folium x^3 + y^3 = 6xy at (3, 3). (Check: 27 + 27 = 54 =
        6*9. OK.)
        3x^2 + 3y^2 y' = 6y + 6x y'
        y' (3y^2 - 6x) = 6y - 3x^2
        y' = (2y - x^2)/(y^2 - 2x).
        At (3,3): (6 - 9)/(9 - 6) = -1.
H. INTERPRETATION: the slope -x/y of x^2 + y^2 = 25 is the negative
   reciprocal of the radius slope y/x: the tangent is perpendicular to
   the radius.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting y' when differentiating a y term; dividing by
   zero at points of vertical tangent.
J. RELATED: related rates; implicit function theorem (supplementary);
   level curves (Module 12): the gradient is normal to F(x,y) = c.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.6 FIRST AND SECOND DERIVATIVES; INCREASING AND DECREASING FUNCTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F3.11  MONOTONICITY TEST (FIRST-DERIVATIVE TEST FOR INCREASE/DECREASE)
B. On an interval I:
     f'(x) > 0 for all x in I  =>  f is increasing on I.
     f'(x) < 0 for all x in I  =>  f is decreasing on I.
     f'(x) = 0 for all x in I  =>  f is constant on I.
C. SYMBOLS: "increasing" means x1 < x2 implies f(x1) < f(x2).
D. CONCEPT: positive slope = going uphill.
E. CONDITIONS: f continuous on I and differentiable inside. (The test
   gives a sufficient, not necessary, condition: f(x) = x^3 is
   increasing even though f'(0) = 0.)
G. EXAMPLES: f(x) = x^3 - 3x: f'(x) = 3(x^2 - 1) is positive for |x| > 1
   (increasing on (-inf, -1) and (1, inf)) and negative on (-1, 1)
   (decreasing). f(x) = x^3 is increasing although f'(0) = 0.
H. INTERPRETATION: the sign of the derivative gives the direction of
   change; a loss decreases along directions where its derivative is
   negative.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: requiring f' > 0 everywhere for 'increasing'
   (isolated zeros are fine); testing the sign at one point instead of
   on the whole interval.
J. RELATED: critical points (F3.12), gradient descent (F43.2).

F3.12  CRITICAL POINTS AND THE FIRST-DERIVATIVE TEST
B. A CRITICAL POINT of f is a point c in the domain where f'(c) = 0 or
   f'(c) does not exist. Candidates for local extrema occur only at
   critical points (Fermat's theorem: if f has a local extremum at an
   interior point c where f is differentiable, then f'(c) = 0).
   First-derivative test at a critical point c:
     f' changes + to - at c  => local maximum.
     f' changes - to + at c  => local minimum.
     f' does not change sign => no local extremum (e.g. x^3 at 0).
C. SYMBOLS: c a critical point; f'(c) the derivative there; the sign
   pattern of f' just left and right of c decides the type.
D. CONCEPT: at a smooth peak or valley the tangent is horizontal
   (Fermat); a sign change of f' from + to - marks a peak, from - to + a
   valley.
E. !! WARNING !! f'(c) = 0 does NOT guarantee an extremum.
G. EXAMPLES: f(x) = x^3 - 3x + 1: f' = 3(x - 1)(x + 1): + to - at x = -1
   (local max, f = 3), - to + at x = 1 (local min, f = -1). f(x) = x^3:
   f'(0) = 0 but no sign change, so no extremum. f(x) = |x|: f'(0) does
   not exist; x = 0 is the global minimum.
H. INTERPRETATION: critical points are the only candidates for interior
   extrema.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: assuming f'(c) = 0 means an extremum; forgetting
   points where f' does not exist; ignoring endpoints.
J. RELATED: second-derivative test (F3.14), closed-interval method
   (F3.13), gradient equal to zero (F43.2).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.7 LOCAL AND GLOBAL (ABSOLUTE) MAXIMA AND MINIMA
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
  Local (relative) maximum at c: f(c) >= f(x) for all x NEAR c.
  Global (absolute) maximum at c: f(c) >= f(x) for ALL x in the domain.
  Similarly for minima.
  A global extremum is also a local one, but not conversely.

F3.13  CLOSED-INTERVAL METHOD (for global extrema on [a, b])
B. For f continuous on [a, b]:
     1. Find all critical points in (a, b).
     2. Evaluate f at the critical points AND at the endpoints a and b.
     3. Largest value = global max; smallest = global min.
C. SYMBOLS: [a, b] the interval; c the critical points inside it; f(a),
   f(b) the endpoint values.
D. CONCEPT: a continuous function on a closed interval must attain its
   extremes (EVT), and they occur at critical points or endpoints, so
   only finitely many values need comparing.
E. CONDITIONS: continuity on a closed bounded interval guarantees
   existence (EVT, F2.7). On an open or unbounded domain extremes may not
   exist; study limits at the ends.
G. EXAMPLE: f(x) = x^3 - 3x + 1 on [-2, 3].
     f'(x) = 3x^2 - 3 = 0 -> x = -1 or 1.
     f(-2) = -8 + 6 + 1 = -1;  f(-1) = -1 + 3 + 1 = 3;
     f(1) = 1 - 3 + 1 = -1;    f(3) = 27 - 9 + 1 = 19.
     Global max = 19 at x = 3; global min = -1 at x = -2 and x = 1.
     Local max at x = -1 (value 3), local min at x = 1 (value -1).
G2 (APPLIED): fence 200 m to enclose a rectangle. Width x, length
     100 - x. Area A(x) = x(100 - x) on [0, 100]. A'(x) = 100 - 2x = 0
     -> x = 50. A(0) = 0, A(50) = 2500, A(100) = 0. Maximum 2500 m^2
     (a square).
H. INTERPRETATION: the largest candidate value is the global maximum,
   the smallest the global minimum.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting endpoints; not checking that the critical point
   is inside the interval.
J. RELATED: extreme value theorem (F2.7), optimization over bounded sets
   (Module 43).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.8 CONCAVITY AND INFLECTION POINTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F3.14  SECOND DERIVATIVE, CONCAVITY, SECOND-DERIVATIVE TEST
B. FORMULA/RULES
     f''(x) = d/dx [ f'(x) ]   (second derivative; also d^2 f/dx^2)
     f'' > 0 on I  => f is CONCAVE UP (convex, "cup", slope increasing)
     f'' < 0 on I  => f is CONCAVE DOWN ("cap", slope decreasing)
     Inflection point: a point where concavity changes (f'' changes
     sign); at such a point f'' = 0 or does not exist (necessary, not
     sufficient).
     SECOND-DERIVATIVE TEST: if f'(c) = 0 then
       f''(c) > 0 => local minimum at c
       f''(c) < 0 => local maximum at c
       f''(c) = 0 => test inconclusive (use the first-derivative test).
C. SYMBOLS: f'' is the rate of change of the slope (curvature,
   acceleration).
D. CONCEPT: concave up: the curve lies above its tangents and bends
   upward like a bowl: critical points there are minima. This
   reasoning generalizes to the Hessian in many variables (Module 12).
E. CONDITIONS: f'' must exist near c; the test is inconclusive when
   f''(c) = 0 (x^4 has a minimum, x^3 none, -x^4 a maximum).
G. EXAMPLES
   Using f(x) = x^3 - 3x + 1: f''(x) = 6x. f''(-1) = -6 < 0 (max);
   f''(1) = 6 > 0 (min); f'' changes sign at x = 0: inflection point
   (0, 1).
   Moderate: f(x) = x^4: f'(0) = 0, f''(0) = 0 (inconclusive); but f'
   changes from - to +, so a minimum. For f(x) = x^3, f' = 3x^2 does
   not change sign: no extremum.
   Moderate 2: f(x) = x e^(-x). f' = e^(-x)(1 - x) = 0 at x = 1.
   f'' = e^(-x)(x - 2); f''(1) = -e^(-1) < 0: local (and global) max
   with f(1) = 1/e = 0.3679. Inflection at x = 2.
H. INTERPRETATION: a loss function that is concave up everywhere
   (convex) has no spurious local minima: any critical point is a global
   minimum (e.g. squared-error loss for linear regression).
I. MISTAKES: "f''(c) = 0 means inflection point" (not necessarily, see
   x^4); mixing up "concave up" with "increasing".
J. RELATED: Hessian (Module 12); Newton's method uses f'' (Module 43).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.9 INTERPRETING DERIVATIVE GRAPHS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Reading f from graphs of f' and f'' (a common exam skill):

  Feature of the graph of f'           Meaning for f
  f' above the x-axis                  f increasing
  f' below the x-axis                  f decreasing
  f' crosses axis from + to -          local max of f
  f' crosses axis from - to +          local min of f
  f' touches axis, no sign change      no extremum (flat spot)
  f' increasing                        f concave up (f'' > 0)
  f' decreasing                        f concave down (f'' < 0)
  f' has a local max or min            f has an inflection point
  f' large in magnitude                f changing rapidly (steep)
  f' undefined (jump, spike)           corner or cusp of f

Reading f' from the graph of f: slope of the tangent at each point.
Reading f from f': area under f' gives change in f (Module 4).

WORKED EXAMPLE: f' is the line f'(x) = 2 - x on R. f' > 0 for x < 2 (f
increasing), f' < 0 for x > 2 (f decreasing); f' crosses + to - at 2
(local max); f' is decreasing everywhere so f'' = -1 < 0 (concave down).
One such f is f(x) = 2x - x^2/2 + C.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
3.10 PRACTICAL APPLICATIONS (CALCULUS TO DATA SCIENCE)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
1. Gradient descent: update w <- w - eta f'(w) moves downhill (Module
   43).
2. Maximum likelihood: set the derivative of the log-likelihood to 0
   (Module 38). Example below.
3. Sensitivity analysis: f'(x) dx approximates the effect of small
   changes.
4. Related rates & marginal analysis (economics: marginal cost = C'(x)).
5. Activation functions: sigmoid s' = s(1-s); tanh' = 1 - tanh^2;
   ReLU' = 0 or 1 (undefined at 0).

WORKED EXAMPLE (MLE preview, derivative = 0): n Bernoulli(p) trials give
k successes. Log-likelihood l(p) = k ln p + (n - k) ln(1 - p).
   l'(p) = k/p - (n - k)/(1 - p) = 0
   => k(1 - p) = (n - k) p => k = n p => p_hat = k/n.
   l''(p) = -k/p^2 - (n-k)/(1-p)^2 < 0, so it is a maximum. For n = 20,
   k = 7: p_hat = 0.35.

COMPARISON TABLE: DIFFERENTIATION vs INTEGRATION (preview; see Module 4)
  Aspect            Differentiation        Integration
  Question          How fast is f          How much accumulates / what
                    changing here?         function has this rate?
  Output            slope/rate function    area/total/antiderivative
  Operation type    local (at a point)     global (over an interval)
  Notation          f'(x), dy/dx           int f(x) dx, int[a..b]
  Inverse of        integration            differentiation (FTC)
  Always has a      mostly mechanical      no closed form for many
  formula?          (rules)                functions (e.g. e^(-x^2))
  Data science use  gradients, optimization  probabilities from
                                           densities, expectations


========================================================================
MODULE 4: INTEGRATION
========================================================================
