      of the left table; RIGHT keeps all of the right; FULL keeps both.
C. SYMBOLS: R, S relations (tables); sigma[cond] selection; pi[attrs]
   projection; x the Cartesian product; join[theta] theta join; |R| the
   number of rows.
D. CONCEPT: each operator takes tables and returns a table, so operators
   compose into queries.
E. CONDITIONS: set operators need union-compatible relations; natural
   join needs common attribute names; the pure model has no duplicate
   rows, SQL does.
G. EXAMPLES: see the worked examples in this section
   (sigma[city='Pune'](Customers), pi[name](...), the inner join with 3
   rows).
H. INTERPRETATION: SQL SELECT-FROM-WHERE corresponds to pi, x and sigma;
   optimizers rearrange these operators.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: confusing selection (rows) with projection
   (columns); ignoring NULLs in joins.
J. RELATED: join algorithms (F25.1), aggregation (section 25.4), pandas
   filtering (Module 42).
WORKED EXAMPLES (tables of 24.1)
   sigma[city='Pune'](Customers) = {(1, Asha, Pune), (3, Meera, Pune)}.
   pi[city](Customers) = {Pune, Delhi, Mumbai}  (Pune once: duplicates
      removed; SQL SELECT DISTINCT city).
   pi[name](sigma[city='Pune'](Customers)) = {Asha, Meera}
      ("names of Pune customers": select first, then project).
   Customers x Orders: 4 x 4 = 16 rows.
   Customers join[id = cid] Orders (inner equi-join): pairs where id = cid:
      (1, Asha, Pune, 101, 1, 250); (1, Asha, Pune, 102, 1, 100);
      (3, Meera, Pune, 103, 3, 400). 3 rows.
      Customers 2 and 4 have no orders; order 104 has no customer.
CARDINALITY RULES OF THUMB
   FK -> PK join (each order matches exactly one customer, FK non-NULL):
      |Orders join Customers| = |Orders|.
   General equi-join size: between 0 and |R| * |S|.
   Joining on a non-unique key on BOTH sides multiplies rows (many-to-many).
EQUIVALENCES USED BY OPTIMIZERS
   sigma[c1 AND c2](R) = sigma[c1](sigma[c2](R));
   selection can be PUSHED below a join if it only uses one side's columns;
   projection can be pushed down (drop unneeded columns early);
   join is commutative and associative (join ORDER affects cost, not result).
COMMON MISTAKES: confusing sigma (rows) with pi (columns); applying union to
incompatible relations; forgetting that a pure projection removes
duplicates but SQL does not unless DISTINCT is given; assuming join
preserves row count; joining on non-key columns inadvertently.
NORMALIZATION [SUPPLEMENTARY]: to avoid redundancy and update anomalies,
split tables so that every non-key attribute depends on "the key, the whole
key, and nothing but the key" (1NF atomic values, 2NF no partial
dependency on part of a composite key, 3NF no transitive dependency).
Functional dependency X -> Y: X determines Y (student_id -> email).


========================================================================
MODULE 25: DATABASE QUERY PROCESSING
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
25.1 SELECTION, PROJECTION AND SET INTERSECTION IN PRACTICE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
SQL / pandas EQUIVALENTS
   SELECT name FROM Customers WHERE city = 'Pune';
   customers.loc[customers.city == 'Pune', 'name']
   SELECT DISTINCT city FROM Customers;      customers['city'].drop_duplicates()
LOGICAL ORDER OF EVALUATION of an SQL query (differs from the written order):
   FROM / JOIN  ->  WHERE  ->  GROUP BY  ->  HAVING  ->  SELECT  ->  DISTINCT
   ->  ORDER BY  ->  LIMIT.
   Consequences: WHERE filters rows BEFORE grouping and cannot use aggregates;
   HAVING filters groups AFTER aggregation; a column alias defined in SELECT
   is not visible in WHERE.
SET INTERSECTION (INTERSECT): rows appearing in BOTH inputs.
   Implementation: put one input in a hash set (O(|R|)), probe with the
   other (O(|S|)): expected O(|R| + |S|). Alternative: sort both and merge:
   O(|R| log |R| + |S| log |S|).
   EXAMPLE: R = {a, b, c, d}, S = {c, d, e}: R n S = {c, d}.
   Equivalent to an equi-join on all attributes (then projection).
   Python: set(R) & set(S).
COMPLEXITY (n rows):
   full table scan for selection: O(n);
   with an index on the filter column: B-tree O(log n + k) for k matches;
   hash index O(1) for equality only.
   projection: O(n) (O(n) extra for hashing if duplicates are removed).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
25.2 JOINS AND JOIN ALGORITHMS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
JOIN TYPES (data of 24.1: Customers C has 4 rows; Orders O has 4 rows)
   INNER JOIN C.id = O.cid: 3 rows (Asha-101, Asha-102, Meera-103).
   LEFT JOIN (keep all customers): the 3 matches + Ravi (NULL order)
        + John (NULL order) = 5 rows.
   RIGHT JOIN (keep all orders): 3 matches + order 104 with NULL customer
        = 4 rows.
   FULL OUTER JOIN: 3 matches + Ravi + John + order 104 = 6 rows.
   CROSS JOIN: 4 x 4 = 16 rows.
   SEMI-JOIN (customers that HAVE orders, no order columns): Asha, Meera.
   ANTI-JOIN (customers with NO orders): Ravi, John.
   SQL: SELECT c.name, o.amount FROM Customers c LEFT JOIN Orders o
        ON c.id = o.cid;
   pandas: customers.merge(orders, left_on='id', right_on='cid', how='left')
   (how = 'inner', 'left', 'right', 'outer', 'cross').
THE ROW-COUNT PITFALL: if the join key is not unique on the right side,
   each left row is repeated once per match: aggregations computed after
   the join are inflated (sum of customer-level fields double counts).
   Use merge(..., validate='one_to_many') etc.
F25.1  JOIN ALGORITHMS AND THEIR COST (R with r rows, S with s rows)
   NESTED-LOOP JOIN: for each row of R, scan all of S and compare.
        Time O(r * s); space O(1). Simple; fine for tiny tables.
   INDEX NESTED-LOOP: for each row of R, look up S through an index on the join
        key: O(r log s) (B-tree) or O(r) expected (hash).
   HASH JOIN (equi-joins only): BUILD a hash table on the smaller input
        (say S), then PROBE with each row of R.
        Time O(r + s) expected; space O(min(r, s)) (or spills to disk by
        partitioning).
   SORT-MERGE JOIN: sort both on the join key, then merge with two pointers.
        Time O(r log r + s log s + output); useful if the inputs are already
        sorted or if sorted output is needed; handles inequality/range
        joins better than hash.
   Choice: hash join is typically best for large unsorted equi-joins;
   nested-loop with an index for selective lookups; sort-merge when sorted.
   OUTPUT SIZE is a separate cost: it can be as large as r * s.
   SELECTIVITY ESTIMATE (optimizer): for an equi-join on column a,
        |R join S| approx (r * s) / max(V(R,a), V(S,a)),
        where V(R,a) is the number of distinct values of a in R.
   EXAMPLE: r = 10,000 orders, s = 500 customers, join on customer id
        (500 distinct in S; assume up to 500 in R): estimate
        10,000 * 500 / 500 = 10,000 rows (every order matches one customer).
C. SYMBOLS: r, s the numbers of rows of R and S; V(R, a) the number of
   distinct values of attribute a; |R join S| the result size.
D. CONCEPT: nested loops compare all pairs; hashing and sorting reduce
   the matching work to near-linear.
E. CONDITIONS: hash join needs equality conditions; sort-merge handles
   ranges; indexes help selective joins.
G. EXAMPLE: r = 10,000 orders and s = 500 customers: nested loop
   5,000,000 comparisons, hash join about 10,500 operations.
H. INTERPRETATION: the choice of join algorithm can change running time
   by orders of magnitude.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: joining on non-unique keys (row explosion); ignoring
   skewed key values.
J. RELATED: complexity (Module 17), hash tables (section 18.5), sorting
   (Module 20).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
25.3 SORTING, QUERY EXECUTION AND COMPLEXITY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
SORTING (ORDER BY): O(n log n) with comparison sorts (Module 20). When the
data exceeds memory the database uses EXTERNAL MERGE SORT: sort chunks that
fit in memory, write sorted "runs" to disk, merge runs (several at a
time). An index on the sort column can avoid sorting entirely.
QUERY EXECUTION PIPELINE
   1. PARSE the SQL text into a syntax tree and check names/types.
   2. LOGICAL PLAN: a relational-algebra tree.
   3. OPTIMIZE: rewrite (push selections/projections down; reorder joins),
      choose physical algorithms and indexes using COST ESTIMATES based on
      table statistics (row counts, distinct values, histograms).
   4. PHYSICAL PLAN: concrete operators (index scan, hash join, sort).
   5. EXECUTE (often as a pipeline of iterators) and return results.
   Inspect plans with EXPLAIN / EXPLAIN ANALYZE.
OPTIMIZATION EXAMPLE: pi[name](sigma[city='Pune'](Customers join Orders))
   is better executed as: filter Customers by city first, then join.
   Pushing the selection down shrinks the join input.
QUERY COMPLEXITY SUMMARY (n rows)
   Scan/selection/projection  O(n)         Index lookup  O(log n) or O(1)
   Sort (ORDER BY)            O(n log n)   Hash aggregation O(n)
   Hash join                  O(r + s)     Nested-loop join O(r s)
   Sort-merge join            O(r log r + s log s)
   Cartesian product          O(r s) output
   DISTINCT, INTERSECT, EXCEPT  O(n) hash or O(n log n) sort
EDGE CASES: NULL keys never match in an equi-join; empty tables; duplicate
keys; skewed keys (one value holding most rows overloads one worker in a
distributed join); type mismatches (int vs string key) which silently
yield no matches.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
25.4 AGGREGATION AND DISTRIBUTED AGGREGATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
AGGREGATE FUNCTIONS: COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX (and STDDEV, VARIANCE, ...)
collapse many rows to one value; GROUP BY computes one value per group.
NULL RULES: aggregates ignore NULLs, except COUNT(*) which counts rows;
COUNT(col) counts non-NULL values; AVG(col) = SUM(col)/COUNT(col) over
non-NULL values; SUM of an empty set is NULL (COUNT is 0).
WHERE vs HAVING: WHERE filters rows before grouping; HAVING filters groups.
WORKED EXAMPLE (Orders table of 24.1): 
   SELECT cid, COUNT(*) n, SUM(amount) total, AVG(amount) mean
   FROM Orders GROUP BY cid;
      cid 1: n = 2, total = 350, mean = 175
      cid 3: n = 1, total = 400, mean = 400
      cid 5: n = 1, total = 75,  mean = 75
   ... HAVING SUM(amount) > 300 keeps cid 1 and cid 3.
   Grouping by customer city after the inner join: Pune: 250 + 100 + 400
   = 750 (3 orders, mean 250).
ALGORITHMS: HASH AGGREGATION (one pass, hash table keyed by group, O(n));
SORT-BASED (sort by group key then scan, O(n log n)).
pandas: orders.groupby('cid')['amount'].agg(['count', 'sum', 'mean']).
DISTRIBUTED AGGREGATION (idea; details with formulas in Module 26)
   Data is split into PARTITIONS stored on different machines. Instead of
   shipping all rows to one place:
     1. each partition computes a small PARTIAL aggregate locally;
     2. partial results are SHUFFLED/sent and COMBINED;
     3. a final step produces the global answer.
   (MapReduce/Spark: map -> combine (local pre-aggregation) -> shuffle ->
   reduce.) This reduces network traffic from O(n) rows to O(groups x
   partitions) partial values.
   Works exactly for aggregates with a CONSTANT-SIZE, MERGEABLE partial
   state: sum, count, min, max (partial = the value itself), average
   (partial = (sum, count)), variance (partial = (count, mean, M2) or
   (count, sum, sum of squares)). It does NOT work with partial medians
   (Module 26).
EXAMPLE: total sales per region over 3 servers holding rows of all regions:
   each server outputs {region: partial_sum}; the combiner adds the
   partial sums per region. The AVERAGE must carry (sum, count) pairs.


========================================================================
MODULE 26: STATISTICAL AGGREGATION IN DISTRIBUTED SYSTEMS
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.1 SETUP AND CLASSIFICATION OF AGGREGATES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
SETTING: a dataset of N values is split across k partitions (machines,
files, shards). Partition i holds n_i values x_i1, ..., x_i,n_i, with
N = n_1 + ... + n_k. Each partition may send only a SMALL SUMMARY. The
question: what summary suffices to compute the global statistic EXACTLY?
SUFFICIENT SUMMARY INFORMATION: a (constant-size) set of values per partition
from which the global statistic can be computed exactly, with no access to
the raw data.
CLASSIFICATION (Gray et al.)
   DISTRIBUTIVE: global value = same function of partial values.
        sum, count, min, max.
   ALGEBRAIC: computed from a fixed number of distributive aggregates.
        mean (sum, count), variance/standard deviation (count, sum, sum
        of squares), covariance, correlation, regression coefficients
        (sums of x, y, xy, x^2, y^2).
   HOLISTIC: no constant-size exact summary exists.
        median, exact quantiles, mode, exact distinct count, rank.
        (Approximations via sketches: t-digest, GK, HyperLogLog, count-min.)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.2 SUMS, COUNTS, MINIMUM AND MAXIMUM
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F26.1  DISTRIBUTIVE AGGREGATES
B. N = sum[i] n_i;  S = sum[i] S_i  (S_i = sum of partition i);
   global min = min[i] (min_i);  global max = max[i] (max_i).
C. SYMBOLS: n_i count and S_i sum on partition i; min_i local minimum.
D. CONCEPT: addition and comparison are associative and commutative, so
   partial results can be combined in any order and in any grouping (a
   tree of combiners is fine).
E. CONDITIONS: empty partitions must send the identity element (count 0,
   sum 0, min = +infinity, max = -infinity); NULLs excluded consistently.
G. EXAMPLE: partitions A = [2, 4, 6], B = [8, 10]:
   n = (3, 2), S = (12, 18), min = (2, 8), max = (6, 10).
   Global: N = 5, S = 30, min = 2, max = 10.
H. INTERPRETATION: these aggregates can be computed with a tree of
   combiners, so adding machines scales.
I. MISTAKE: summing floating-point partials in different orders gives
   slightly different results (floating-point addition is not exactly
   associative); numerical reproducibility is not exact.
J. RELATED: algebraic aggregates (F26.2 to F26.4), MapReduce combiners
   (section 25.4).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.3 WEIGHTED AVERAGES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F26.2  GLOBAL MEAN FROM PARTITION MEANS
A. NAME: Weighted average (combining means).
B. FORMULA
     mu = (sum_i S_i) / (sum_i n_i) = sum_i (n_i / N) m_i
   General weighted average: x_w = sum_j w_j x_j / sum_j w_j.
C. SYMBOLS: m_i = S_i / n_i local mean; n_i / N the WEIGHT (share of data
   in partition i); w_j weights.
D. CONCEPT: a partition with more data should influence the global mean
   proportionally more. The mean of the means ignores the sizes.
E. CONDITIONS: weights non-negative with positive sum; the local means must
   be over the SAME quantity. Weights are sample sizes for means; for
   other statistics the weights may differ.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: A = [2, 4, 6] (n = 3, m = 4), B = [8, 10] (n = 2, m = 9).
      Correct: (3*4 + 2*9)/5 = 30/5 = 6.   (All data: 30/5 = 6 OK.)
      WRONG: mean of means = (4 + 9)/2 = 6.5.
   Moderate (rates): variant A: 50 conversions out of 1000 visits (5%);
      variant B: 2 out of 10 (20%). Average of rates = 12.5% (wrong);
      overall rate = 52/1010 = 5.15% (visit-weighted).
H. INTERPRETATION: an "average of averages" is only right when all groups
   have equal size. Related: SIMPSON'S PARADOX - trends in groups can
   reverse in the aggregate because of different group sizes.
I. MISTAKES: averaging averages; averaging percentages without weights;
   weighting by the wrong quantity (use the denominator of each rate).
J. RELATED: weighted mean (F29.2), distributive aggregates (F26.1),
   Simpson's paradox (section 40.8).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.4 VARIANCE AGGREGATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F26.3  COMBINING VARIANCES (SUM-OF-SQUARES FORM)
A. NAME: Variance from distributed summaries.
B. FORMULAS (partition i sends n_i, S_i = sum x, Q_i = sum x^2)
     N = sum n_i,  S = sum S_i,  Q = sum Q_i,  mu = S/N
     population variance: sigma^2 = Q/N - mu^2
     sample variance:     s^2 = (Q - N mu^2)/(N - 1) = (Q - S^2/N)/(N - 1)
C. SYMBOLS: Q_i sum of squares on partition i; mu global mean.
D. CONCEPT: variance = E[X^2] - (E[X])^2: both expectations are averages of
   distributive quantities (x and x^2).
E. CONDITIONS & NUMERICAL WARNING: this form suffers CATASTROPHIC
   CANCELLATION when the mean is large relative to the spread (Q/N and
   mu^2 are huge and nearly equal; subtracting loses precision and can
   even produce a negative variance). Use the stable form below for real
   systems.
F. DERIVATION: sum (x - mu)^2 = sum x^2 - 2 mu sum x + N mu^2 = Q - 2 mu S +
   N mu^2 = Q - N mu^2.
G. EXAMPLE: A = [2,4,6], B = [8,10]:
   n = (3, 2); S = (12, 18); Q = (4 + 16 + 36, 64 + 100) = (56, 164).
   N = 5, S = 30, Q = 220, mu = 6.
   population variance = 220/5 - 36 = 44 - 36 = 8.
   sample variance = (220 - 5*36)/4 = 40/4 = 10.
H. INTERPRETATION: shows that variance is computable from sums, but it
   can lose precision (see the stable form F26.4).
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using the formula with large means (cancellation);
   mixing n and n - 1.
J. RELATED: stable form (F26.4), population and sample variance (F31.3,
   F31.4).

F26.4  COMBINING VARIANCES (STABLE, PARALLEL / CHAN ET AL. FORM)
B. Each partition sends (n_i, m_i, M2_i) with m_i the local mean and
   M2_i = sum_j (x_ij - m_i)^2 (sum of squared deviations from its own mean).
   Merge two summaries A and B:
        n = n_A + n_B
        delta = m_B - m_A
        m = m_A + delta * n_B / n
        M2 = M2_A + M2_B + delta^2 * n_A * n_B / n
   Final: population variance = M2/n; sample variance = M2/(n - 1).
   Merging is associative: fold over all partitions in any order/tree.
C. SYMBOLS: M2 "sum of squares about the mean"; delta difference of the means.
D. CONCEPT (law of total variance in sum form): total scatter = within-group
   scatter (M2_A + M2_B) + between-group scatter (the delta^2 term: how
   far apart the group means are, weighted by sizes). Equivalent
   population form: sigma^2 = (1/N) sum n_i sigma_i^2 + (1/N) sum n_i
   (m_i - mu)^2 ("mean of the variances" + "variance of the means").
E. CONDITIONS: exact (up to floating point); numerically stable.
G. EXAMPLE (same data): A = [2,4,6]: n = 3, m = 4, M2 = 4 + 0 + 4 = 8.
   B = [8,10]: n = 2, m = 9, M2 = 1 + 1 = 2.
   delta = 9 - 4 = 5, n = 5.
   m = 4 + 5 * 2/5 = 6;
   M2 = 8 + 2 + 25 * 3 * 2/5 = 10 + 30 = 40.
   sample variance 40/4 = 10, population variance 40/5 = 8: matches the
   direct calculation (deviations -4, -2, 0, 2, 4: squares 16 + 4 + 0 + 4
   + 16 = 40).
   WRONG shortcuts: the average of the two sample variances
   (A: 8/2 = 4, B: 2/1 = 2; average 3) or the pooled
   within-variance alone (10/(5 - 2) = 3.33) ignores the between-group
   term and is far below the true 10.
H. INTERPRETATION: the between-group term can dominate when partitions
   have different means (e.g. data sharded by time or region).
I. MISTAKES: using sample vs population denominators inconsistently across
   partitions (n_i - 1 vs n_i); sending only local variances and means
   (variance AND n_i are needed, plus the mean: with sample variances
   v_i, use M2_i = (n_i - 1) v_i); using the sum-of-squares formula on
   large, tightly clustered values.
J. RELATED: sample variance and Bessel's correction (Module 31, 37);
   Welford's one-pass online algorithm is the n_B = 1 case of the same
   merge; covariance merge: C = C_A + C_B + delta_x delta_y n_A n_B/n,
   so correlation and regression can also be aggregated exactly.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
26.5 WHY MEDIANS CANNOT GENERALLY BE RECONSTRUCTED FROM LOCAL MEDIANS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
CLAIM: The global median is NOT a function of the local medians and
sizes (nor of the local means, minima and maxima).
COUNTER-EXAMPLE (two different datasets, same local summaries)
   Dataset 1: A = [1, 2, 3], B = [4, 5, 6].
        local medians (2, 5), sizes (3, 3); combined sorted
        [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6] -> median (3 + 4)/2 = 3.5.
   Dataset 2: A' = [1, 2, 10], B' = [4, 5, 6].
        local medians (2, 5), sizes (3, 3) - IDENTICAL summaries;
        combined sorted [1, 2, 4, 5, 6, 10] -> median (4 + 5)/2 = 4.5.
   Since two datasets with identical (median, size) pairs have different
   global medians (3.5 vs 4.5), no formula can recover the global median
   from those pairs.
   Further: the median of medians (2 and 5 -> 3.5) coincides with the true
   value in dataset 1 only by luck; in dataset 2 it errs (true 4.5).
WHY: the median depends on the RANK of values across ALL partitions: how
many values on each machine lie below a candidate value. Local medians
discard the information about the shape of each distribution.
WHAT DOES WORK
   - Exact: iterate on the VALUE axis. For a candidate threshold t each
     partition returns count(x <= t) (a distributive aggregate); the
     coordinator binary-searches t until the global rank N/2 is reached
     (a few rounds of communication), or ships histograms/quantile
     sketches.
   - Approximate with guarantees: t-digest, Greenwald-Khanna, KLL sketches,
     approx_percentile in Spark/SQL. Error is a bounded rank or
     relative error.
   - If an approximation is acceptable, the median of medians works only as
     a rough guide (and, with unequal sizes, can be badly biased).
   - Related holistic statistics (mode, exact distinct count, percentiles)
     have the same problem.
COMMON MISTAKE: computing groupby().median() on pre-aggregated data (median
of daily medians) and reporting it as the overall median.

SUMMARY TABLE: WHAT EACH PARTITION MUST SEND
  Global statistic         Sufficient local summary             Exact?
  count, sum               n_i, S_i                              yes
  min, max                 min_i, max_i                          yes
  mean                     n_i, S_i (or n_i, m_i)               yes
  variance, std            n_i, m_i, M2_i (or n_i, S_i, Q_i)    yes
  covariance, correlation  n_i, means, co-moments                yes
  proportion/rate          numerator and denominator counts      yes
  median / quantile        (no constant-size exact summary)      no; sketches
  distinct count, mode     (no constant-size exact summary)      no; HyperLogLog
  Rule: send the numerator and denominator (or the moments) - never the
  already-divided result.

DATA SCIENCE LINK (Module 26): Spark/Dask/BigQuery aggregations, federated
analytics and learning (each client sends gradient sums and counts),
batch normalization statistics across devices, online learning
(Welford), A/B-test metrics across shards, and pitfalls such as Simpson's
paradox when reporting averages of averages.


========================================================================
B4. THEORETICAL COMPUTER SCIENCE
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 27: COMPUTABILITY AND FORMAL LANGUAGES
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
27.1 ALGORITHMS AND COMPUTABILITY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Not every well-posed question can be answered by a computer program, no
matter how much time or memory we have. Computability theory studies
exactly what CAN and CANNOT be computed. This is different from
efficiency (complexity theory, Module 17), which asks how FAST something
can be computed.

MODELS OF COMPUTATION
   TURING MACHINE (informal): an infinite tape of cells, a head that reads
   and writes one symbol and moves left/right, and a finite set of states
   with transition rules (state, symbol) -> (new state, written symbol,
   move). It models a computer with unlimited memory.
   CHURCH-TURING THESIS: anything that can be computed by any
   "effective procedure" (what we intuitively call an algorithm) can be
   computed by a Turing machine. It is a thesis (a statement about the
   informal notion of algorithm), not a theorem; all known models
   (lambda calculus, RAM machines, real programming languages with
   unbounded memory) are equivalent in power.
DECISION PROBLEM: a yes/no question about inputs, e.g. "Is n prime?",
   "Does this string match the pattern?", "Does program P halt on input x?".
   Any computation can be phrased as deciding membership in a set (a
   language, 27.4).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
27.2 DECIDABLE AND UNDECIDABLE PROBLEMS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   DECIDABLE: there is an algorithm that, for EVERY input, HALTS and gives
      the correct yes/no answer.
   SEMI-DECIDABLE (recognizable): an algorithm halts with "yes" on every
      yes-instance, but may run forever on a no-instance.
   UNDECIDABLE: no algorithm decides it for all inputs. (It may still be
      solvable for many specific inputs.)
EXAMPLES
   Decidable: Is n prime? Is a list sorted? Does a DFA accept string w? Do
      two DFAs accept the same language? Is a given Sudoku solution valid?
   Undecidable: the Halting Problem; "do two arbitrary programs compute the
      same function?"; "does this program ever print 42?"; "does the
      program contain a bug (infinite loop, crash) for some input?";
      Hilbert's Entscheidungsproblem (is a first-order statement valid?).
   Semi-decidable but not decidable: the halting problem (run P on x; if
      it halts say yes - but if it never halts you cannot be sure).
UNDECIDABLE vs INTRACTABLE: undecidable = NO algorithm exists; intractable =
   an algorithm exists but needs exponential time (e.g. brute-force
   traveling salesman). Different phenomena, often confused.

F27.1  THE HALTING PROBLEM
A. NAME: Halting problem (Turing, 1936).
B. STATEMENT: there is NO algorithm H(P, x) that, given the code of any
   program P and any input x, always halts and correctly answers whether
   P halts on x.
C. SYMBOLS: P program text; x input; H the hypothetical decider.
D. CONCEPT: a program that could analyze any other program and predict
   termination would be able to analyze itself in a paradoxical way.
F. PROOF (by contradiction, diagonal argument)
     1. Suppose a total decider H(P, x) exists: it returns "halts" or
        "loops" and always terminates.
     2. Build a new program D(P): if H(P, P) = "halts" then loop forever; else
        halt immediately.
     3. Run D on its own code, D(D).
        - If H(D, D) says "halts", D loops forever: so D(D) does NOT halt.
        - If H(D, D) says "loops", D halts: so D(D) DOES halt.
        Either way H is wrong about D(D). Contradiction. Therefore H
        cannot exist.
E. CONDITIONS: the result concerns the GENERAL problem over ALL programs and
   inputs. Individual programs can often be analyzed (simple loops
   with a decreasing counter provably halt). The theorem says no single
   method works for all.
G. EXAMPLES: a loop 'while x != 1' that applies x -> x/2 (even) or 3x +
   1 (odd) (the Collatz iteration): nobody has proved that it halts for
   every start value, which shows why general termination analysis is
   hard; 'for i in range(10)' obviously halts.
H. INTERPRETATION / CONSEQUENCES: perfect automatic bug-finders, perfect
   virus detectors, and complete program-equivalence checkers cannot
   exist; tools use approximations (may give false alarms or miss
   problems). RICE'S THEOREM [SUPPLEMENTARY]: every non-trivial question
   about what a program COMPUTES (its behavior, not its text) is
   undecidable.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: reading it as "we can never tell whether a program
   halts" (we can for many); confusing undecidable with "takes very long";
   thinking more powerful hardware or more memory would solve it.
J. RELATED: Rice's theorem (same section), decidability table (section
   27.6), complexity versus computability.
DATA SCIENCE LINK: why automated verification of arbitrary code or ML
pipelines is necessarily incomplete; why static analysers and linters
are heuristic; why timeouts are used in practice.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
27.3 FORMAL LANGUAGES: ALPHABETS AND STRINGS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   ALPHABET (Sigma): a finite, non-empty set of symbols.
      Sigma = {0, 1} (binary); Sigma = {a, b}; Sigma = ASCII characters.
   STRING (word) over Sigma: a finite sequence of symbols from Sigma.
      "0110" is a string over {0, 1}.
   EMPTY STRING (epsilon, written eps): the string with no symbols; length 0.
   LENGTH |w|: number of symbols. |0110| = 4; |eps| = 0.
   CONCATENATION uv: u followed by v. |uv| = |u| + |v|; eps is the identity:
      eps w = w eps = w. Concatenation is associative but NOT commutative.
   POWER w^k: w repeated k times; w^0 = eps. (ab)^3 = ababab.
   Sigma^k: all strings of length k; |Sigma^k| = |Sigma|^k.
   Sigma* ("Sigma star"): ALL finite strings over Sigma, including eps.
      Sigma* = Sigma^0 U Sigma^1 U Sigma^2 U ...; it is infinite (countably).
   LANGUAGE: any SET of strings L, a subset of Sigma*. The set can be finite
      or infinite.
EXAMPLES (Sigma = {a, b})
   Number of strings of length at most 2: 1 + 2 + 4 = 7:
      eps, a, b, aa, ab, ba, bb.
   L1 = {strings ending in b}; L2 = {a^n b^n : n >= 0} = {eps, ab, aabb,
   aaabbb, ...}; L3 = {strings with an even number of a's}.
OPERATIONS ON LANGUAGES
   Union L U M; intersection L n M; complement ~L = Sigma* - L;
   concatenation LM = {uv : u in L, v in M};
   Kleene star L* = {eps} U L U LL U LLL U ... (zero or more concatenations
   of words of L); L+ = LL* (one or more).
   Example: L = {a, bb}: L* contains eps, a, bb, aa, abb, bba, bbbb, ...
COUNTING FACT: there are countably many strings but UNCOUNTABLY many
   languages (subsets of Sigma*), and only countably many programs; hence
   most languages are not recognizable by any program. (This is another
   route to undecidability.)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
27.4 FINITE AUTOMATA
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F27.2  DETERMINISTIC FINITE AUTOMATON (DFA)
A. NAME: DFA.
B. DEFINITION: a DFA is a 5-tuple M = (Q, Sigma, delta, q0, F):
     Q       finite set of STATES
     Sigma   input alphabet
     delta   transition function  delta : Q x Sigma -> Q  (exactly one next
             state for each state and symbol)
     q0      start state (q0 in Q)
     F       set of ACCEPTING (final) states (F subset of Q)
   Computation on w = w1 w2 ... wn: start in q0; for each symbol move to
   delta(current, symbol). M ACCEPTS w if the final state is in F.
   L(M) = the set of strings M accepts (the language of M).
C. SYMBOLS: Q states; delta the rule book; F the "yes" states.
D. CONCEPT: a machine with a fixed, finite amount of memory (its current
   state) that reads the input once, left to right, no going back. The
   state records everything it needs to remember about the prefix read
   so far.
E. CONDITIONS: DFA recognition takes O(|w|) time and O(1) memory. Finite
   memory is the key limitation (27.6). A "dead/trap" state is used for
   impossible continuations if delta is to be total.
G. EXAMPLES
   (1) Even number of 1s over {0, 1}. States E (start, accepting), O.
        delta: E-0->E, E-1->O, O-0->O, O-1->E.
        Input 1101: E -1-> O -1-> E -0-> E -1-> O: ends in O (not
        accepting): REJECT (three 1s, odd). Input 1001: E,O,O,O,E: ends in
        E: ACCEPT (two 1s).
   (2) Strings over {a, b} ending in "ab". States: q0 (start; no useful
        progress), q1 (last symbol was a), q2 (last two symbols were ab;
        accepting).
        delta: q0-a->q1, q0-b->q0; q1-a->q1, q1-b->q2; q2-a->q1, q2-b->q0.
        "aab": q0 -a-> q1 -a-> q1 -b-> q2: ACCEPT.
        "abb": q0 -a-> q1 -b-> q2 -b-> q0: REJECT.
   (3) Binary numbers divisible by 3. States = remainders {0, 1, 2}
        (start 0, accepting 0). Reading bit b changes the value v to 2v + b,
        so delta(r, b) = (2r + b) mod 3.
        "110" (decimal 6): r = 0 -1-> 1 -1-> (2*1 + 1) mod 3 = 0 -0-> 0:
        ACCEPT. "101" (5): 0 -> 1 -> (2*1 + 0) = 2 -> (2*2 + 1) mod 3 = 2:
        REJECT.
H. INTERPRETATION: DFAs are the model behind tokenizers, lexers,
   protocol parsers, and regular-expression engines.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: leaving some (state, symbol) pairs undefined in a DFA;
   forgetting that the empty string is accepted iff q0 is in F; not
   making the machine remember enough (state meaning must be clear).
J. RELATED: NFA (F27.3), regular expressions (F27.4), tokenizers and
   parsers.

F27.3  NONDETERMINISTIC FINITE AUTOMATON (NFA)
B. Like a DFA but delta : Q x (Sigma U {eps}) -> subsets of Q: several (or no)
   next states, and eps-moves. An NFA ACCEPTS w if SOME sequence of choices
   leads to an accepting state.
C. SYMBOLS: delta now maps (state, symbol or eps) to a SET of states;
   the other components are as for a DFA.
D. CONCEPT: "guess" the right path (or explore all in parallel). Easier to
   design: e.g. NFA for "contains aab as a substring" is tiny.
E. THEOREM: NFAs and DFAs recognize exactly the same languages. SUBSET
   CONSTRUCTION converts an n-state NFA into a DFA with up to 2^n states.
   The blow-up can really occur.
G. EXAMPLE: an NFA for strings over {a, b} containing the substring aab:
   states q0 (loops on a, b), q1, q2, q3 (accepting, loops on a, b); the
   'guess that the substring starts here' moves q0 -a-> q1 -a-> q2 -b->
   q3. It accepts baab.
H. INTERPRETATION: nondeterminism is a design convenience; every NFA can
   be simulated by tracking the set of possible states.
I. MISTAKE: thinking nondeterminism adds computing power to finite automata
   (it adds convenience, not power; it does for other machine types).
J. RELATED: subset construction, regular expressions (F27.4), DFA
   (F27.2).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
27.5 REGULAR LANGUAGES AND REGULAR EXPRESSIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITION: a language is REGULAR if some DFA (equivalently NFA, or regular
expression) recognizes it.
F27.4  REGULAR EXPRESSIONS (theoretical syntax)
B. Built from: the empty set; eps; each symbol a in Sigma; and the operations
     union       R | S  (also written R + S)
     concatenation   RS
     Kleene star R*   (zero or more repetitions)
   Shorthands: R+ = RR* (one or more); R? = (R | eps) (optional);
   [abc] = a | b | c.
   Precedence: star highest, then concatenation, then union. Use brackets.
C. SYMBOLS: | "or"; * "zero or more".
D. CONCEPT: a compact algebra for describing patterns; Kleene's theorem:
   a language is regular <=> it is described by a regular expression
   <=> recognized by a finite automaton.
E. CONDITIONS: theoretical regular expressions have only union,
   concatenation and star; practical engines add features
   (backreferences, lookahead) that exceed regular languages.
G. EXAMPLES (Sigma = {0, 1} or {a, b})
   0*10*          exactly one 1 (strings of 0s with a single 1 somewhere).
   (a|b)*ab       strings ending in ab.   (matches aab, ab, bbab; not abb)
   a*b*           any number of a's followed by any number of b's.
   (0|1)*00       binary strings ending in 00.
   (00)*          an even number of 0s (including none).
   a(a|b)*b       strings that start with a and end with b (length >= 2).
   Matching strings: for (a|b)*ab, "bab" yes, "ba" no.
H. INTERPRETATION: regular expressions describe exactly the patterns a
   finite automaton can recognize.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: unescaped special characters; using regular
   expressions for nested structure.
J. RELATED: DFAs and NFAs (F27.2, F27.3), pumping lemma (F27.5), Python
   re module.
PYTHON re (practical regular expressions)
   import re
   re.fullmatch(r'(a|b)*ab', 'aab')     # match object (whole string must match)
   re.search(r'\d{3}-\d{4}', text)      # first occurrence anywhere
   re.findall(r'[A-Za-z]+', text)       # all words
   Notes: re.match anchors only at the START (not the end); fullmatch needs the
   whole string; "." matches any character except newline; quantifiers are
   GREEDY by default (add ? for lazy). Python regexes include features
   (backreferences like (a+)\1) that go beyond theoretical regular
   languages.
CLOSURE PROPERTIES: regular languages are closed under union, intersection,
complement, concatenation, Kleene star, and reversal. (Complement of a
DFA: swap accepting and non-accepting states; intersection: product
construction.)
DECISION PROPERTIES: membership, emptiness, and equivalence are decidable for
regular languages.
COMMON MISTAKES: forgetting anchors/fullmatch; unescaped special characters
(. * + ? ( ) [ ] \ |); using regex for nested structures like HTML.
DATA SCIENCE LINK: text cleaning, tokenization, log parsing, validating
formats (emails, dates), feature extraction from strings, pandas
str.contains/extract/replace.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
27.6 RECOGNITION OF REGULAR AND NON-REGULAR LANGUAGES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
IDEA: a DFA has only FINITELY many states, so it can remember only a bounded
amount about what it has read. Languages needing UNBOUNDED memory
(counting without limit, matching nested structure) are not regular.
NON-REGULAR EXAMPLES
   {a^n b^n : n >= 0}: needs to count the a's exactly.
   Balanced parentheses; {ww : w in {a,b}*}; palindromes;
   {strings with equally many a's and b's}; {a^p : p prime}.
REGULAR EXAMPLES (finite memory suffices)
   "even number of a's", "ends in ab", "divisible by 3 in binary",
   "contains the substring abb", "at most 5 a's" (finite counting is fine),
   {a^n b^m : n, m >= 0} (no relation required between n and m).
F27.5  PUMPING LEMMA FOR REGULAR LANGUAGES
A. NAME: Pumping lemma.
B. STATEMENT: if L is regular, there is a number p >= 1 (the pumping length)
   such that EVERY string w in L with |w| >= p can be written w = x y z with
        (1) |y| >= 1,   (2) |x y| <= p,   (3) x y^i z is in L for all i >= 0.
C. SYMBOLS: p pumping length (can be taken as the number of DFA states);
   x, y, z the three pieces; y is the "pumpable" section.
D. CONCEPT: a string longer than the number of states must revisit a state
   (pigeonhole); the stretch between the two visits can be repeated any
   number of times (or deleted), and the DFA still ends in the same state.
E. CONDITIONS: it is a NECESSARY condition for regularity. It can be used
   only to PROVE NON-regularity (via contrapositive). Passing the test
   does NOT prove a language regular.
   HOW TO USE (a game): (1) assume L regular with pumping length p;
   (2) YOU choose a clever string w in L with |w| >= p; (3) for ANY split
   x y z satisfying (1) and (2), (4) find i with x y^i z not in L;
   (5) contradiction.
G. EXAMPLE: L = {a^n b^n : n >= 0} is not regular.
     Assume regular with pumping length p. Choose w = a^p b^p (in L,
     length 2p >= p). Since |xy| <= p, both x and y lie inside the leading
     block of a's: y = a^k with k >= 1. Pump up (i = 2): x y^2 z = a^(p+k) b^p,
     which has more a's than b's, so it is not in L. Contradiction;
     L is not regular.
   Another: palindromes: w = a^p b a^p; y is within the first p a's, so
     pumping gives more a's on the left only: not a palindrome.
H. INTERPRETATION: a language that needs unbounded counting cannot be
   regular.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: choosing a w that depends on a particular split;
   forgetting condition |xy| <= p; trying to use it to show a language IS
   regular; choosing w too short (< p).
J. RELATED: Myhill-Nerode theorem (a language is regular iff it has finitely
   many distinguishable prefix classes) gives an exact test.

THE CHOMSKY HIERARCHY (context)
  Type  Language class        Machine                       Example
  3     Regular               finite automaton (DFA/NFA)    ends in ab; email format-ish
  2     Context-free          pushdown automaton (stack)    a^n b^n, balanced parentheses,
                                                            programming-language syntax
  1     Context-sensitive     linear bounded automaton      a^n b^n c^n
  0     Recursively enumerable Turing machine               halting problem (recognizable)
  Each class strictly contains the one below. A stack lets a machine
  count and match nesting (a^n b^n): CFG S -> a S b | eps.
  Regular languages: membership decidable in O(|w|). Context-free: O(|w|^3)
  (CYK) generally.
SUMMARY TABLE: DECIDABILITY FOR FORMAL LANGUAGE QUESTIONS
  Question                           Regular  Context-free  Turing-recognizable
  Does machine accept w?             yes      yes           semi-decidable only
  Is the language empty?             yes      yes           no
  Are two machines equivalent?       yes      no            no


========================================================================
B5. COMPUTER ARCHITECTURE
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 28: PARALLEL COMPUTING FUNDAMENTALS
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.1 CPU AND MEMORY FUNDAMENTALS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA: Computing speed depends on how fast the processor executes
instructions AND on how fast data reaches it. Often the bottleneck is
memory, not arithmetic.
CPU COMPONENTS
   ALU (arithmetic logic unit): performs arithmetic and logic.
   Control unit: fetches and decodes instructions, directs execution.
   Registers: tiny, very fast storage inside the CPU.
   Clock: sets the rhythm; clock speed in GHz (billions of cycles per
   second). Performance is not just GHz: instructions per cycle, cores
   and memory behavior matter.
INSTRUCTION CYCLE: FETCH instruction from memory -> DECODE it -> EXECUTE
   (operate, maybe access memory) -> WRITE BACK the result.
MEMORY HIERARCHY (approximate orders of magnitude; they vary by hardware)
   Level            Typical size        Typical access time
   Registers        bytes               < 1 ns
   L1 cache         tens of KB          ~1 ns
   L2 cache         hundreds of KB-MB   ~3-10 ns
   L3 cache         tens of MB          ~10-40 ns
   Main memory RAM  GBs                 ~100 ns
   SSD              100s of GB-TB       ~100 microseconds
   Hard disk        TBs                 ~ms
   Network / disk are many orders of magnitude slower than registers.
   Rule: the closer to the CPU, the faster, smaller and costlier.
LOCALITY OF REFERENCE: caches work because programs tend to reuse recent
   data (TEMPORAL locality) and touch nearby addresses (SPATIAL
   locality). Memory is fetched in CACHE LINES (typically 64 bytes).
   EXAMPLE: NumPy arrays are row-major (C order) by default: elements of
   a row are adjacent. Summing along rows (a[i, :]) streams through
   memory; iterating down columns jumps by a whole row each step and
   can be several times slower for large arrays. (pandas/NumPy
   operations choose efficient axes; Fortran-ordered arrays reverse
   this.)
LATENCY vs BANDWIDTH: latency = time for ONE access; bandwidth = bytes
   per second sustained. COMPUTE-BOUND tasks are limited by arithmetic
   speed; MEMORY-BOUND tasks (most data-science operations, e.g. summing
   a huge array) are limited by memory bandwidth, so more cores may not
   help.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.2 CPU CORES, THREADS AND PROCESSES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
   CORE: an independent processing unit able to execute its own instruction
      stream. A multi-core CPU has several (4, 8, 16, ...).
   PROCESS: a running program with its own memory space.
   THREAD: a lightweight execution path inside a process; threads of one
      process SHARE memory.
   HYPER-THREADING / SMT: one core presents two logical threads that
      share its execution units; typical gain 0-30 percent, not 2x.
   GPU: thousands of simpler cores optimized for the same operation on
      many data items (SIMD-like, called SIMT); excellent for matrix
      operations and neural networks, poor for branchy sequential code;
      data must be copied between CPU and GPU memory.
   PYTHON AND THE GIL: CPython's Global Interpreter Lock lets only one
      thread execute Python bytecode at a time. Consequences:
        - threading does NOT speed up CPU-bound pure Python code;
        - threading DOES help I/O-bound work (waiting for network/disk);
        - multiprocessing (separate processes), joblib, or libraries that
          release the GIL (NumPy, many compiled extensions) achieve real
          parallelism. (Recent Python versions have an optional free-
          threaded build; check the version you use.)
   os.cpu_count() reports logical cores; scikit-learn n_jobs=-1 uses all.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.3 INSTRUCTIONS AND EXECUTION; FLYNN'S TAXONOMY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
INSTRUCTIONS: the CPU executes MACHINE INSTRUCTIONS from its instruction
set architecture (ISA): load/store, add, multiply, compare, branch.
High-level code (Python) is translated (compiled/interpreted) into
these. Interpreted Python executes many machine instructions per Python
operation, which is why vectorized library calls are much faster.
SPEED-UPS INSIDE ONE CORE
   PIPELINING: overlap the stages of consecutive instructions (like an
      assembly line).
   SUPERSCALAR execution: several instructions per cycle.
   BRANCH PREDICTION: guess which way an if/loop goes to keep the
      pipeline full; mispredictions cost tens of cycles (sorted data can
      make branchy code faster).
FLYNN'S TAXONOMY (classifies by instruction streams and data streams)
   Class  Meaning                              Example
   SISD   single instruction, single data      classic single-core scalar CPU
   SIMD   single instruction, multiple data    vector units (AVX), GPU lanes
   MISD   multiple instruction, single data    rare (fault-tolerant systems)
   MIMD   multiple instruction, multiple data  multi-core CPUs, clusters

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.4 SIMD (SINGLE INSTRUCTION, MULTIPLE DATA)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
IDEA: one instruction operates on a whole VECTOR of values at once.
   An AVX2 register is 256 bits: it holds 8 float32 or 4 float64 numbers,
   so one add instruction performs 8 (or 4) additions. AVX-512 doubles
   that.
   Scalar:  c[0] = a[0] + b[0]; c[1] = a[1] + b[1]; ...  (8 instructions)
   SIMD:    c[0..7] = a[0..7] + b[0..7]                   (1 instruction)
WHERE USED: NumPy, pandas (internally), BLAS/LAPACK linear algebra, image
   processing, GPUs.
CONDITIONS: the same operation on contiguous, independent data; branches
   and irregular access reduce efficiency. float32 gives twice the
   SIMD width of float64.
IDEAL SPEEDUP = vector width (e.g. up to 8 for float32 with AVX2); real
   speedups are smaller because of memory limits.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.5 MIMD (MULTIPLE INSTRUCTION, MULTIPLE DATA)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
IDEA: several processors independently run DIFFERENT instruction streams on
DIFFERENT data. Examples: multi-core CPU running different threads;
a cluster of machines each processing its partition (Spark, Dask, MPI).
Task parallelism (different tasks) and data parallelism (the same task
on different chunks) are both MIMD-capable.
COMPARISON: SIMD vs MIMD
  Aspect           SIMD                          MIMD
  Control          one instruction stream        independent instruction streams
  Data             vector of data per instr.     separate data per processor
  Granularity      fine: element operations      coarse: tasks/chunks
  Hardware         vector units, GPU             multi-core CPU, clusters
  Best for         uniform array arithmetic      irregular/different work
  Coordination     implicit (lockstep)           explicit (locks, messages)
  Example          np.add(a, b)                  Pool.map(train, folds)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.6 PARALLEL vs CONCURRENT EXECUTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   CONCURRENCY: managing MULTIPLE tasks in overlapping time periods; tasks
      make progress by interleaving, possibly on ONE core (a structure
      of the program).
   PARALLELISM: tasks literally executing AT THE SAME INSTANT on multiple
      cores (a property of the execution/hardware).
   Concurrent does not imply parallel; parallel programs are concurrent.
   Analogy: one cook switching between two dishes (concurrent) vs two
   cooks cooking simultaneously (parallel).
  Aspect           Concurrent                         Parallel
  Needs multiple cores?  No                           Yes
  Main goal        responsiveness, overlapping waits  speed (throughput)
  Typical use      async I/O, GUIs, web servers       numerical computing, training
  Python tool      asyncio, threading (I/O-bound)     multiprocessing, joblib, NumPy
  Hazards          race conditions, deadlocks         same, plus communication cost
HAZARDS: RACE CONDITION (result depends on timing when two threads modify
shared data without protection), DEADLOCK (tasks wait for each other
forever), starvation. Remedies: locks, immutable data, message passing,
avoiding shared mutable state.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.7 SHARED vs DISTRIBUTED MEMORY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
  Aspect            Shared memory                     Distributed memory
  Address space     one, visible to all cores         separate per node
  Communication     read/write shared variables       send/receive messages
  Programming       threads, OpenMP                   MPI, Spark, Dask
  Scaling           limited by one machine            to thousands of machines
  Main issue        synchronization, cache coherence  network latency/bandwidth,
                                                      data partitioning
  Failure           whole machine                     single nodes can fail
  Data movement     cheap (same RAM)                  expensive: move computation
                                                      to the data
Rule of thumb: minimize communication and keep data local.

F28.1  AMDAHL'S LAW
A. NAME: Amdahl's law (speed-up limit for a fixed problem size).
B. FORMULA
     S(p) = T(1)/T(p) = 1 / ( (1 - f) + f/p )
     maximum speed-up as p -> infinity:  S_max = 1/(1 - f).
     Efficiency E(p) = S(p)/p.
C. SYMBOLS: p number of processors; f the fraction (0 to 1) of the ORIGINAL
   running time that can be parallelized; (1 - f) the serial fraction;
   T(1) and T(p) running times on 1 and p processors.
D. CONCEPT: the serial part cannot be sped up, so it sets a ceiling no
   matter how many processors are added.
E. CONDITIONS: fixed problem size; ignores communication, load imbalance
   and overheads (real speed-ups are lower). If the problem grows with
   the machine, Gustafson's law S = (1 - f) + f p [SUPPLEMENTARY] is the
   more relevant model.
F. DERIVATION: normalize T(1) = 1. Serial part costs (1 - f) on any number
   of processors; the parallel part costs f/p. So T(p) = (1 - f) + f/p.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: f = 0.9 (90% parallelisable), p = 8:
        S = 1/(0.1 + 0.9/8) = 1/(0.1 + 0.1125) = 1/0.2125 = 4.71.
        Efficiency 4.71/8 = 58.8%. Even with infinite processors the
        maximum is 1/0.1 = 10.
   Moderate: f = 0.95, p = 16: S = 1/(0.05 + 0.059375) = 9.14;
        S_max = 20. Going from 16 to 64 processors: S(64) = 1/(0.05 +
        0.01484) = 15.4 (4x the processors, 1.7x more speed-up).
H. INTERPRETATION: reduce the SERIAL fraction first (I/O, data loading,
   merging results); adding cores has diminishing returns.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting that f is a fraction of the ORIGINAL time; assuming
   linear speed-up; ignoring overhead.
J. RELATED: Gustafson's law (scaled speed-up), tuning cost (section
   47.6), embarrassingly parallel tasks (section 28.8).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
28.8 VECTORIZED COMPUTATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITION: replace explicit Python loops by whole-array operations that
run in compiled, SIMD-optimized code.
BENEFITS: speed (often 10 to 100 times faster than pure Python loops),
shorter code, fewer bugs.
EXAMPLES (Python/NumPy)
   Loop version                      Vectorized version
   s = 0                             s = np.sum(x)
   for v in x: s += v
   y = [2*v + 1 for v in x]          y = 2*x + 1
   dist = [sqrt(sum((a-b)**2 ...))]  dist = np.sqrt(((A - b)**2).sum(axis=1))
   Standardizing columns of X:       Z = (X - X.mean(axis=0)) / X.std(axis=0)
   (BROADCASTING: the length-p mean vector is stretched across all n rows.)
   Matrix product for all predictions: y_hat = X @ w.
BROADCASTING RULES: align shapes from the right; dimensions are compatible if
equal or one of them is 1. (n, p) - (p,) works; (n, p) - (n,) fails
unless reshaped to (n, 1).
LIMITS AND PITFALLS: temporaries use extra memory; very large arrays may
not fit in RAM (use chunking/Dask); inherently sequential recurrences
(a[i] depends on a[i-1]) cannot be trivially vectorized; np.vectorize
and pandas .apply with Python functions are loops in disguise (no
speed-up).
ALGORITHMIC PARALLELISM IN MACHINE LEARNING
   Embarrassingly parallel (independent tasks): cross-validation folds,
      bootstrap samples, random-forest trees, hyper-parameter search
      candidates, Monte Carlo simulations. (n_jobs=-1)
   Data parallel: split a batch across GPUs, average gradients.
   Sequential stages: gradient boosting trees depend on earlier ones
      (parallelism only inside a tree), iterative optimization steps.
PYTHON SNIPPETS
   from joblib import Parallel, delayed
   results = Parallel(n_jobs=4)(delayed(fit_fold)(k) for k in range(5))
   from multiprocessing import Pool
   with Pool(4) as pool: out = pool.map(f, data)
COMMON MISTAKES: parallelizing tiny tasks (overhead exceeds gain); sharing
mutable state between processes; random seeds identical in every worker
(duplicated "random" results) - seed each worker differently; measuring
speed-up with too-small data; ignoring that threads do not help
CPU-bound pure Python.

DATA SCIENCE LINK (Modules 26 and 28): distributed aggregation needs
mergeable partial summaries; vectorization and BLAS make linear algebra
fast; GPU training relies on SIMD-style parallelism; Amdahl's law
explains why speeding up model training is limited by data loading and
other serial stages.


########################################################################
#                      PART C - STATISTICS                             #
########################################################################

CONVENTIONS FOR PART C
  Population quantities (parameters, usually unknown): mu, sigma^2, sigma,
  rho (correlation), N (population size).
  Sample quantities (statistics, computed from data): x_bar, s^2, s, r,
  n (sample size).
  Sorted data (order statistics): x_(1) <= x_(2) <= ... <= x_(n).
  Quartile convention: where quartiles are computed numerically this
  document uses the "linear interpolation" rule (NumPy default,
  method 7): Q(p) sits at position (n - 1)p + 1 in the sorted list (1-based),
  interpolating between neighbors. Other textbooks (median-of-halves,
  etc.) give slightly different Q1, Q3 for small samples. This is a
  genuine convention difference [SYLLABUS NOTE 8].

========================================================================
C1. DESCRIPTIVE STATISTICS
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 29: MEASURES OF CENTRAL TENDENCY
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
A measure of central tendency answers: "What is a typical or central
value of this data?" Mean, median and mode are three different answers to
that question, and they can disagree. Which is appropriate depends on
the shape of the data and what you want to know.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.1 ARITHMETIC MEAN
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F29.1  ARITHMETIC MEAN
A. NAME: Arithmetic mean (average).
B. FORMULAS
     Sample mean:        x_bar = (1/n) sum[i=1..n] x_i
     Population mean:   mu    = (1/N) sum[i=1..N] x_i
C. SYMBOLS: x_i the i-th observation; n sample size (N population size);
   sum[i=1..n] the sum of all n values.
D. CONCEPT: the "balance point" of the data: if each value were a weight on
   a seesaw, x_bar is where it balances. Equivalent: the value each item
   would have if the total were shared equally.
E. CONDITIONS: needs numeric (interval/ratio) data. It uses every value, so
   it is SENSITIVE TO OUTLIERS (breakdown point 0: a single extreme
   value can move it arbitrarily).
   KEY PROPERTIES
     (1) sum (x_i - x_bar) = 0  (deviations balance).
     (2) x_bar minimizes sum (x_i - c)^2 over c (least squares).
     (3) Linear: mean(a x + b) = a x_bar + b.
     (4) mean(x + y) = mean(x) + mean(y) for paired data.
F. DERIVATION of (2): g(c) = sum (x_i - c)^2; g'(c) = -2 sum (x_i - c) = 0
   gives c = x_bar; g'' = 2n > 0 (a minimum). Property (1) follows from
   sum x_i - n x_bar = 0.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9: sum = 40, n = 8, x_bar = 5.
      Deviations: -3, -1, -1, -1, 0, 0, 2, 4 (sum 0).
   Moderate: temperatures in Celsius 20, 22, 25 have mean 22.33. Convert to
      Fahrenheit F = 1.8 C + 32: mean = 1.8(22.33) + 32 = 72.2 (property 3).
H. INTERPRETATION: the mean is the natural summary for symmetric data and
   for totals (mean x n = total).
I. MISTAKES: averaging percentages or ratios without weights (Module 26);
   reporting the mean of heavily skewed data (income) as "typical";
   averaging ordinal codes (1-5 satisfaction) without noting the
   assumption.
J. RELATED: weighted mean (F29.2), median (F29.3), expectation E[X]
   (Module 36): the population mean is the expectation of a random value
   drawn uniformly from the population.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.2 WEIGHTED MEAN
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F29.2  WEIGHTED MEAN
A. NAME: Weighted arithmetic mean.
B. FORMULA:   x_w = ( sum w_i x_i ) / ( sum w_i )
   Grouped data (frequency table): x_bar = ( sum f_j x_j ) / ( sum f_j ),
   with f_j the frequency of value (or class midpoint) x_j.
C. SYMBOLS: w_i weights (importance, reliability, size), w_i >= 0, not all 0;
   f_j frequencies.
D. CONCEPT: some observations count more than others; each value is
   multiplied by its weight, and the total is divided by total weight so
   weights need not sum to 1. If all w_i are equal it reduces to the
   ordinary mean.
E. CONDITIONS: weights must be meaningful (credits, group sizes, inverse
   variances). Class midpoints in grouped data give an APPROXIMATION.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (GPA): grades 90 (3 credits), 80 (4 credits), 70 (2 credits):
      (90*3 + 80*4 + 70*2)/(3 + 4 + 2) = (270 + 320 + 140)/9 = 730/9
      = 81.11. (Unweighted mean would be 80.)
   Moderate (frequency table): values 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 9 with frequencies
      1, 2, 3, 2, 1, 1: sum f x = 2 + 6 + 12 + 10 + 6 + 9 = 45; sum f = 10;
      mean = 4.5.
   Combining groups: group means 4 (n = 3) and 9 (n = 2): (3*4 + 2*9)/5 = 6
      (Module 26).
H. INTERPRETATION: use weights equal to group sizes to recover the pooled
   mean; inverse-variance weights give the most precise combined
   estimate of a quantity measured with different precisions.
I. MISTAKES: weights that do not match the quantity (e.g. weighting
   rates by something other than their denominators); dividing by
   the number of items rather than the sum of weights.
J. RELATED: combining group means (F26.2), grouped data, expectation
   (F36.1).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.3 MEDIAN
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F29.3  MEDIAN
A. NAME: Median.
B. FORMULA: sort the data x_(1) <= ... <= x_(n).
     n odd:   median = x_((n+1)/2)
     n even:  median = ( x_(n/2) + x_(n/2 + 1) ) / 2
C. SYMBOLS: x_(k) the k-th smallest value (order statistic).
D. CONCEPT: the middle value: half the data lie at or below it, half at or
   above. It depends on the ORDER of values, not their magnitudes, so
   extreme values cannot drag it far (breakdown point 50%). It minimizes
   the sum of absolute deviations: sum |x_i - c| is smallest at c = median.
E. CONDITIONS: needs at least ordinal data. For even n, the average of the two
   middle values may not be an observed value (for discrete or categorical
   ordinal data some use the lower or upper median).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: 3, 9, 4, 1, 7 -> sorted 1, 3, 4, 7, 9 -> median 4.
           1, 2, 3, 4 -> (2 + 3)/2 = 2.5.
   Moderate (outlier): salaries (thousands) 30, 32, 35, 36, 38, 40, 300.
      Mean = 511/7 = 73.0 (pulled up by 300); median = 36 (the middle
      value of seven). The median describes the "typical" salary here.
      A 15% trimmed mean (drop the lowest and highest value) is
      (32 + 35 + 36 + 38 + 40)/5 = 36.2.
H. INTERPRETATION: a median salary of 36 means half of employees earn at
   most 36. Reports on incomes, house prices and waiting times
   usually use the median.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting to sort; mis-handling even n; assuming the median of
   combined groups equals a combination of group medians (Module 26.5).
J. RELATED: quartiles (Module 30/31), quantile of a distribution (Module
   35), L1 loss (the median minimizes mean absolute error).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.4 MODE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITION: the most frequent value (or the most frequent class). 
   Data may have NO mode (all values distinct), ONE mode (unimodal), or
   several (bimodal, multimodal). For continuous data the mode is the
   location of a peak of the density.
USES: the only measure for NOMINAL data (most common color, most common
product category, most common class label: the "majority-class"
baseline classifier).
EXAMPLES: 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6, 9: mode 4 (three times).
   1, 2, 2, 3, 3: modes 2 and 3 (bimodal in the data sense).
MISTAKES: reporting the mode of continuous raw data (usually every value is
unique; bin the data or estimate a density first); treating the mode as
unique; forgetting that the mode can be far from the center.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
29.5 RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN MEAN AND MEDIAN; OUTLIERS AND SKEWED DISTRIBUTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
SYMMETRIC unimodal: mean = median = mode.
RIGHT-SKEWED (positive skew; long tail to the right, e.g. income):
   typically mean > median > mode (the tail pulls the mean toward it).
LEFT-SKEWED (negative skew; long tail to the left, e.g. age at death in
   developed countries): typically mean < median < mode.
!! CAUTION !! These orderings are TYPICAL, not laws; counter-examples
exist (multimodal or discrete data).
ROBUSTNESS: mean (breakdown 0%), median (50%), trimmed mean (as chosen).
EXAMPLE: data 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6, 9 has mean 4.5 > median 4 = mode 4:
   right-skewed because of the 9. Pearson's 2nd skewness coefficient
   3 (mean - median)/s = 3 (0.5)/1.958 = 0.77 > 0.
TRANSFORMATIONS: for right-skewed positive data a log transform often makes
   the distribution more symmetric (mean and median closer).
OTHER MEANS [SUPPLEMENTARY]
   Geometric mean (growth factors): GM = (prod x_i)^(1/n). Growth of 10% then
      20%: factors 1.1 and 1.2: GM = sqrt(1.32) = 1.1489 -> average growth
      14.89% per period (arithmetic 15% overstates).
   Harmonic mean (rates over equal distances): HM = n / sum(1/x_i).
      Driving 120 km at 60 km/h and 120 km at 40 km/h: HM = 2/(1/60 + 1/40)
      = 48 km/h (total distance 240 km / total time 5 h = 48). The
      arithmetic mean 50 would be wrong.
   Inequality: HM <= GM <= AM (equal only if all values are equal).

COMPARISON TABLE: MEAN vs MEDIAN vs MODE
  Aspect              Mean                   Median                Mode
  Definition          sum / n                middle sorted value   most frequent value
  Data types          numeric                ordinal or numeric    any, incl. nominal
  Uses all values     yes                    no (order only)       no (frequency only)
  Outlier sensitivity high                   low (robust)          low
  Skewed data         pulled toward tail     stays near the bulk   at the peak
  Unique?             always                 always                may be none or many
  Algebra             easy (linear,          harder                hard
                      combinable)
  Minimizes           sum of squares         sum of absolute       (0-1 loss: number
                                             deviations            of misses)
  Typical use         symmetric data,        income, prices,       categories,
                      totals, modeling       skewed data           majority class
DATA SCIENCE LINK: the mean is the best constant predictor under squared
error, the median under absolute error, the mode under 0-1 loss: these
are the baselines (mean-baseline regressor, majority classifier).
Imputation: median is safer for skewed features. Robust scaling uses
median and IQR.


========================================================================
MODULE 30: DATA DISTRIBUTIONS AND VISUALIZATION
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.1 FREQUENCY DISTRIBUTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS (for data x_1..x_n)
   FREQUENCY f_j: number of observations equal to value x_j (or falling in
      class j).
   RELATIVE FREQUENCY f_j / n (a proportion; all sum to 1).
   CUMULATIVE FREQUENCY: running total of frequencies up to class j;
      cumulative relative frequency = running total / n.
WORKED EXAMPLE: data 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6, 9 (n = 10)
   Value  Freq  Rel. freq  Cumulative freq  Cumulative rel.
    2      1     0.1        1                0.1
    3      2     0.2        3                0.3
    4      3     0.3        6                0.6
    5      2     0.2        8                0.8
    6      1     0.1        9                0.9
    9      1     0.1        10               1.0
FOR CONTINUOUS DATA: group into non-overlapping classes (bins); state the
boundary convention (e.g. [a, b), left-closed), otherwise boundary values are
double counted or lost.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.2 HISTOGRAMS AND DENSITY HISTOGRAMS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
HISTOGRAM: bars over contiguous bins; bar height shows the COUNT (or
relative frequency) in the bin. Bars touch (the variable is continuous);
contrast with a bar chart (categories, gaps).
F30.1  DENSITY HISTOGRAM
A. NAME: Density histogram (area-normalized histogram).
B. FORMULA: height of the bar for bin j of width h_j:
        d_j = f_j / ( n h_j )
   so that  sum_j d_j h_j = 1  (total area 1).
C. SYMBOLS: f_j count in bin j; n sample size; h_j bin width.
D. CONCEPT: the AREA of a bar (not its height) represents the proportion of
   data in the bin: area_j = d_j h_j = f_j/n. Thus the histogram
   approximates a probability density function (Module 35): the area
   over [a, b] approximates P(a <= X < b).
E. CONDITIONS: with UNEQUAL bin widths a count histogram is misleading (a
   wide bin looks tall merely because it is wide); use density heights.
G. EXAMPLE: bins [2, 4), [4, 6), [6, 10) (widths 2, 2, 4) for the data above.
   Counts: [2,4): 2, 3, 3 -> 3;  [4,6): 4, 4, 4, 5, 5 -> 5;  [6,10): 6, 9 -> 2.
   Densities: 3/(10*2) = 0.15; 5/(10*2) = 0.25; 2/(10*4) = 0.05.
   Areas: 0.15*2 + 0.25*2 + 0.05*4 = 0.3 + 0.5 + 0.2 = 1.0. OK.
   Proportion in [4, 6) = 0.5 (area of that bar).
H. INTERPRETATION: heights are "proportion per unit of x" (density), not
   proportions. Density values can exceed 1 if the bins are narrow
   (e.g. data in [0, 0.1]).
BIN CHOICE (a bias-variance trade-off: too few bins hide structure, too
many show noise)
     Sturges: k = ceil(log2 n) + 1 bins.
     Scott:   h = 3.49 s n^(-1/3).
     Freedman-Diaconis: h = 2 IQR n^(-1/3) (robust to outliers).
   Always try several bin widths; shape impressions depend on them.
   (NumPy: bins='auto' combines Sturges and FD.) KERNEL DENSITY
   ESTIMATES smooth the histogram [SUPPLEMENTARY].
I. MISTAKES: unequal widths without density scaling; unlabeled axes (counts
   vs density); extreme outliers stretching the x-axis; histogram of
   categorical data.
J. RELATED: PDF (F35.3), ECDF (F30.2), kernel density estimates.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.3 EMPIRICAL DISTRIBUTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F30.2  EMPIRICAL CUMULATIVE DISTRIBUTION FUNCTION (ECDF)
A. NAME: Empirical distribution function.
B. FORMULA:   F_n(x) = (1/n) * #{ i : x_i <= x } = (1/n) sum 1[x_i <= x]
C. SYMBOLS: 1[condition] equals 1 if true, else 0; #{...} counts the
   observations at most x.
D. CONCEPT: the proportion of the sample at or below x. A step function that
   starts at 0, rises by 1/n at each data point (by k/n at a value
   repeated k times) and reaches 1. It is the CDF of the "distribution
   that puts probability 1/n on each observed value".
E. CONDITIONS: right-continuous, non-decreasing, between 0 and 1. By the
   Glivenko-Cantelli theorem [SUPPLEMENTARY] F_n converges uniformly to the
   true CDF F as n grows. Quantiles are read by inverting F_n.
G. EXAMPLE (data above): F_n(3) = 3/10 = 0.3; F_n(4) = 0.6; F_n(4.5) = 0.6
   (flat between jumps); F_n(9) = 1; F_n(1) = 0.
   Median: smallest x with F_n(x) >= 0.5 -> 4.
H. INTERPRETATION: "60% of observations are at most 4". The ECDF needs no
   binning, so it is the cleanest honest picture of a sample; the
   bootstrap resamples from F_n (Module 37); the KS statistic compares F_n
   with a theoretical CDF.
I. MISTAKES: reading values of the ECDF as densities; forgetting the
   jump-size rule for ties.
J. RELATED: CDF (Module 35), quantiles, histograms (ECDF = cumulative
   version).

QUARTILES AND THE FIVE-NUMBER SUMMARY
   Q1 (25th percentile), Q2 = median, Q3 (75th percentile). Minimum, Q1,
   median, Q3, maximum form the five-number summary, drawn as a BOX PLOT
   (box from Q1 to Q3, line at the median, whiskers to the most extreme
   values within the fences, points beyond as outliers).
   For the data above (linear-interpolation rule): Q1 = 3.25, Q2 = 4,
   Q3 = 5 (position 2.25 in 0-based sorted values: 3 + 0.25(4 - 3) = 3.25;
   position 6.75: both neighbors 5).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.4 DISTRIBUTION SHAPE AND SKEWNESS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
SHAPE VOCABULARY
   Modality: unimodal (one peak), bimodal (two peaks; often two
      sub-populations mixed), uniform (flat), multimodal.
   Symmetry: left side mirrors right (normal-like) vs skewed.
   Tails: heavy tails (more extremes than a normal), light tails.
   Spread and center.
F30.3  SKEWNESS COEFFICIENTS
A. NAME: Sample skewness.
B. FORMULAS
     Moment coefficient:  g1 = m3 / m2^(3/2),
        m2 = (1/n) sum (x_i - x_bar)^2,  m3 = (1/n) sum (x_i - x_bar)^3
     Adjusted (Fisher-Pearson, as in Excel/SciPy bias=False):
        G1 = sqrt(n(n - 1)) / (n - 2) * g1
     Pearson's second coefficient: Sk2 = 3 (x_bar - median) / s.
C. SYMBOLS: m2, m3 the second and third central moments; s sample SD.
D. CONCEPT: cubing the standardized deviations keeps their SIGN, so a
   long tail on one side makes that side dominate the sum. g1 > 0:
   right-skewed; g1 < 0: left-skewed; g1 approx 0: roughly symmetric.
   (Excess kurtosis g2 = m4/m2^2 - 3 measures tail heaviness relative to the
   normal: 0 for a normal.)
E. CONDITIONS: skewness is sensitive to outliers; needs adequate n
   (unstable for small samples); several definitions exist, so state
   which one.
G. EXAMPLE: data 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6, 9: g1 = 1.12 (calculated with
   the 1/n moment form), G1 = 1.33 (adjusted); both positive:
   right-skewed. Symmetric data 1, 2, 3, 4, 5: g1 = 0.
H. INTERPRETATION: rough guide |g1| < 0.5 approximately symmetric;
   0.5-1 moderately skewed; > 1 highly skewed (rule of thumb only).
I. MISTAKES: calling a distribution symmetric because the mean equals the
   median (not sufficient); confusing the direction ("right skew" means
   the TAIL is on the right, the bulk on the left).
J. RELATED: moments (F36.4), transformations (F36.5), normality checks
   (section 40.4).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: skewed features are often transformed (log,
square root, Box-Cox) before linear models; skewed targets motivate
log-transformed regression or robust losses.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.5 OUTLIERS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITION: an observation far from the bulk of the data. It may be an
error, a different population, or a genuine extreme: investigate before
removing.
RULES OF THUMB
   TUKEY FENCES (box plot rule): flag values below Q1 - 1.5 IQR or above
      Q3 + 1.5 IQR; "far out" beyond 3 IQR.
      Example (data above): IQR = 5 - 3.25 = 1.75. Fences:
      3.25 - 2.625 = 0.625 and 5 + 2.625 = 7.625. The value 9 lies
      above 7.625: flagged as an outlier.
   Z-SCORE: z = (x - x_bar)/s; |z| > 3 flags. Weakness: outliers inflate x_bar
      and s, masking themselves.
   MODIFIED Z-SCORE: M = 0.6745 (x - median)/MAD, MAD = median |x_i - median|;
      |M| > 3.5 flags (robust).
TREATMENT: verify the data; keep if genuine; use robust methods (median,
   IQR, Huber loss); winsorize (cap at percentiles); transform; analyze
   with and without; do not delete simply to improve a model fit.
EXAMPLE (impact): salaries 30, 32, 35, 36, 38, 40, 300: the mean jumps from
   35.2 (without 300) to 73.0 (with), the median from 35.5 to 36.
   (Without 300: six values 30, 32, 35, 36, 38, 40: mean 35.17, median 35.5.)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
30.6 INTERPRETING SCATTER PLOTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
A scatter plot shows each observation as a point (x_i, y_i). Describe:
   DIRECTION: upward (positive association), downward (negative), none.
   FORM: linear, curved (quadratic, exponential, saturating), clusters.
   STRENGTH: how tightly points follow the pattern.
   OUTLIERS and INFLUENTIAL POINTS: unusual points; high-leverage points far
      in x can pull a fitted line.
   SPREAD PATTERN: constant vertical spread (homoscedastic) or a funnel
      (heteroscedastic: variance grows with x).
   OVERPLOTTING: with many points use transparency, hexbin or 2D
      histograms; DISCRETE values create stripes.
WARNINGS: correlation coefficient hides shape; ANSCOMBE'S QUARTET: four
   datasets with the same means, variances, correlation (0.816) and
   regression line but completely different scatter plots (linear,
   curved, an outlier, a single leverage point). ALWAYS plot.
GROUPS: color by category; Simpson's paradox can reverse the overall trend
   relative to within-group trends.
DATA SCIENCE LINK: scatter plots reveal nonlinearity (add polynomial or
tree features), heteroscedasticity (weighted or log-transformed
regression), clusters (unsupervised methods), and leakage (suspiciously
perfect relationships).


========================================================================
MODULE 31: MEASURES OF DISPERSION
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Two datasets can have the same mean but be very different: test scores
all near 70 versus scores spread from 20 to 100. Measures of dispersion
(spread, variability) quantify how far values typically lie from each
other or from the center.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
31.1 RANGE AND INTERQUARTILE RANGE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F31.1  RANGE
B. Range = max - min = x_(n) - x_(1).
C. SYMBOLS: x_(1) the minimum and x_(n) the maximum of the sorted data.
D. Simplest spread measure; depends only on two extreme values, so it grows
   with sample size and is extremely outlier-sensitive.
E. CONDITIONS: needs numeric data; it grows with sample size and is
   dominated by outliers.
G. 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9: range = 9 - 2 = 7.
H. INTERPRETATION: the total spread of the data.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using the range for skewed data with outliers;
   comparing ranges across different sample sizes.
J. RELATED: IQR (F31.2), variance (F31.3), outlier rules (section 30.5).

F31.2  INTERQUARTILE RANGE (IQR)
B. IQR = Q3 - Q1 (the spread of the MIDDLE 50% of the data).
C. SYMBOLS: Q1 and Q3 the 25th and 75th percentiles (lower and upper
   quartile).
D. Robust: unaffected by the extreme 25% in each tail. Used by box plots
   and outlier fences; robust scale estimate: for normal data the SD is
   about IQR/1.349.
E. CONDITIONS: needs a stated quartile convention; robust to outliers
   (breakdown point 25%).
G. Data 2, 3, 3, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 6, 9: Q1 = 3.25, Q3 = 5: IQR = 1.75.
   Data 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9: Q1 = 4, Q3 = 5.5 (linear interpolation):
   IQR = 1.5.
H. INTERPRETATION: the middle half of the data lies within an interval
   of length IQR.
I. MISTAKES: mixing quartile conventions when comparing across software;
   reporting range for skewed data with outliers.
OTHER ROBUST SPREAD: median absolute deviation MAD = median |x_i - median|
   (for 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9: median 4.5, absolute deviations 2.5, 0.5, 0.5,
   0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 2.5, 4.5 -> MAD = 0.5).
J. RELATED: box plots, Tukey fences (section 30.5), robust scaling
   (F42.1), MAD.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
31.2 VARIANCE AND STANDARD DEVIATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F31.3  POPULATION VARIANCE AND STANDARD DEVIATION
