A. NAME: Population variance and standard deviation.
B. FORMULAS
     sigma^2 = (1/N) sum[i=1..N] (x_i - mu)^2
     sigma   = sqrt(sigma^2)
     Computational form: sigma^2 = (1/N) sum x_i^2 - mu^2
C. SYMBOLS: mu population mean; N population size; (x_i - mu) the deviation
   of observation i from the mean; sigma^2 the average squared deviation.
D. CONCEPT: the AVERAGE squared distance from the mean. Squaring makes all
   deviations positive and penalizes large deviations more. The
   standard deviation returns to the original units.
E. CONDITIONS: use when the data are the ENTIRE population of interest.
   Properties: sigma^2 >= 0 (= 0 iff all values equal); Var(a x + b) =
   a^2 Var(x) (shifting does not matter, scaling squares);
   units of variance are squared units; SD has the original units.
G. EXAMPLE: 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9 as a whole population: mu = 5.
   Deviations -3, -1, -1, -1, 0, 0, 2, 4; squares 9, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 4, 16;
   sum = 32. sigma^2 = 32/8 = 4; sigma = 2.
   Computational check: sum x^2 = 4 + 16 + 16 + 16 + 25 + 25 + 49 + 81 = 232;
   232/8 - 25 = 29 - 25 = 4. OK.
H. INTERPRETATION: sigma = 2 means a typical value lies about 2 units
   from the mean (for 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9).
I. MISTAKES: using this formula on a sample (underestimates, see below);
   forgetting the square root for SD; confusing variance units.
J. RELATED: sample variance (F31.4); the expectation form E[X^2] - mu^2
   (F36.3).

F31.4  SAMPLE VARIANCE AND SAMPLE STANDARD DEVIATION
A. NAME: Sample variance (unbiased) and sample standard deviation.
B. FORMULAS
     s^2 = (1/(n - 1)) sum[i=1..n] (x_i - x_bar)^2
     s   = sqrt(s^2)
     Computational form: s^2 = ( sum x_i^2 - n x_bar^2 ) / (n - 1)
C. SYMBOLS: x_bar the SAMPLE mean (not mu); n - 1 "degrees of freedom".
D. CONCEPT: an ESTIMATE of the unknown population variance sigma^2 from a
   sample. The divisor n - 1 corrects a systematic underestimate that
   appears because the deviations are measured from x_bar (which is
   calculated from the same data and sits closer to the data than the
   true mu does).
E. CONDITIONS: needs n >= 2. Assumes the sample is random from the
   population. s^2 is UNBIASED for sigma^2 (E[s^2] = sigma^2); s is NOT
   unbiased for sigma (slightly underestimates, by Jensen's inequality,
   because sqrt is concave).
F. DERIVATION OF THE n - 1 (WHY BESSEL'S CORRECTION)
   Let X_1..X_n be independent with mean mu and variance sigma^2, and
   x_bar their mean. Identity (add and subtract mu):
        sum (x_i - x_bar)^2 = sum (x_i - mu)^2 - n (x_bar - mu)^2.
   Take expectations:
        E[ sum (x_i - mu)^2 ] = n sigma^2,
        E[ n (x_bar - mu)^2 ] = n Var(x_bar) = n (sigma^2/n) = sigma^2.
   Hence E[ sum (x_i - x_bar)^2 ] = n sigma^2 - sigma^2 = (n - 1) sigma^2.
   So dividing by n - 1 gives an unbiased estimator; dividing by n gives
   expectation (n - 1)/n times sigma^2: too small.
   Degrees-of-freedom view: the deviations x_i - x_bar must sum to 0, so only
   n - 1 of them are free to vary; the n-th is determined.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: sample 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9 (n = 8): x_bar = 5; sum of squared
      deviations 32; s^2 = 32/7 = 4.571; s = 2.138.
      (Population version for the same numbers: 4 and 2.)
   Moderate (simulation evidence): draw 200,000 samples of size 5 from a
      normal with sigma^2 = 4. The average of the divide-by-n variance is
      about 3.2 (= (4/5)*4); the divide-by-(n - 1) version averages about
      4.0.
   Moderate 2: scores 70, 75, 80, 85, 90: x_bar = 80; deviations -10, -5, 0, 5,
      10; squares 100, 25, 0, 25, 100 = 250; s^2 = 250/4 = 62.5; s = 7.91.
H. INTERPRETATION: s = 7.91 means scores typically lie about 8 points from
   the mean. For roughly bell-shaped data about 68% of values lie within
   +/- 1 s and 95% within +/- 2 s (empirical rule). Chebyshev's
   inequality (any distribution): at least 1 - 1/k^2 of the data lie
   within k standard deviations (k = 2: at least 75%).
I. COMMON MISTAKES: dividing by n for a sample; dividing by n - 1 for a
   complete population; software defaults (NumPy np.var and np.std:
   ddof = 0; pandas .var/.std: ddof = 1; statistics.variance: n - 1,
   statistics.pvariance: n); computing the computational form with huge
   means (cancellation; Module 26); n = 1 (undefined).
J. RELATED: standard error (Module 37), unbiased estimation (Module 38),
   t-distribution df = n - 1 (Module 39), regression residual variance
   (divide by n - p, Module 40).

COMPARISON TABLE: POPULATION vs SAMPLE VARIANCE
  Aspect               Population variance sigma^2        Sample variance s^2
  Formula              (1/N) sum (x_i - mu)^2             (1/(n-1)) sum (x_i - x_bar)^2
  Center used          true mean mu (fixed, known)        sample mean x_bar (estimated)
  Purpose              DESCRIBE the whole population      ESTIMATE sigma^2 from a sample
  Divisor              N                                  n - 1 (degrees of freedom)
  Bias as estimator    n/a                                unbiased
  Dividing sample by n biased low: E = (n-1)/n sigma^2    corrected by n - 1
  Python default       np.var(x) (ddof=0)                 pd.Series.var() / np.var(x, ddof=1)
  Use when             you hold ALL the data of interest  data are a sample
  (Large n: the two nearly coincide.)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
31.3 WHY SQUARED DEVIATIONS ARE USED
------------------------------------------------------------------------
THE PROBLEM: raw deviations x_i - x_bar sum to ZERO (positive and negative
cancel), so their average is useless as a spread measure.
FIXES: (1) take absolute values: the mean absolute deviation
(1/n) sum |x_i - x_bar|; or (2) square them: variance.
WHY SQUARES ARE PREFERRED IN STATISTICS
   1. SMOOTH and differentiable everywhere (|x| has a kink at 0): easy
      calculus; closed-form minimizers (least squares).
   2. The mean is exactly the value minimizing the sum of squares (the
      median minimizes the sum of absolute values).
   3. ADDITIVITY: for independent variables Var(X + Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y)
      (SDs do not add). Variances decompose into explained and unexplained
      parts (ANOVA, R^2).
   4. Geometry: sum of squares is squared Euclidean distance; Pythagoras
      and orthogonal projections (Module 10) work with it.
   5. Natural for the NORMAL distribution (its density has the squared term
      in the exponent) and the Central Limit Theorem.
COSTS OF SQUARING: (a) units are squared (hence the SD); (b) outliers are
heavily penalized (a deviation of 10 counts 100x a deviation of 1), so
the variance is NOT robust; (c) alternative: MAD / mean absolute error
when robustness matters (L1 loss).
EXAMPLE: deviations 1, 1, 1, 1 versus 0, 0, 0, 4: both have mean absolute
deviation 1, but variance (taking squared deviations / n) is 1 versus 4:
squaring is sensitive to one large deviation.
OTHER SPREAD MEASURE: coefficient of variation CV = s / x_bar (ratio scale,
positive mean); unit-free relative spread. Example: heights SD 7 cm at mean
170 cm: CV = 4.1%; weights SD 12 kg at mean 70 kg: CV = 17%.
DATA SCIENCE LINK: standardization divides by s; MSE and RMSE are
variance-like; regularization penalties (L2) use squares; features with
zero variance carry no information (VarianceThreshold); the bias-variance
decomposition (Module 46) is a statement about squared error.


========================================================================
MODULE 32: CORRELATION
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Correlation measures how consistently two variables move together: when
one is above its average, is the other usually above (positive), usually
below (negative), or unrelated?

------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.1 COVARIANCE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F32.1  COVARIANCE
A. NAME: Covariance.
B. FORMULAS
     Population: Cov(X, Y) = E[ (X - mu_X)(Y - mu_Y) ] = E[XY] - mu_X mu_Y
     Sample: s_xy = (1/(n - 1)) sum (x_i - x_bar)(y_i - y_bar)
                  = ( sum x_i y_i - n x_bar y_bar ) / (n - 1)
C. SYMBOLS: x_i, y_i paired observations; x_bar, y_bar the sample means;
   mu_X, mu_Y population means; E[.] expectation (Module 36).
D. CONCEPT: multiply the two deviations for each pair. If both are above
   (or both below) their means the product is positive; if one is above
   and the other below, negative. The average product captures the
   joint tendency. Cov(X, X) = Var(X).
E. CONDITIONS: needs PAIRED data of equal length. Units are (unit of x) x
   (unit of y), so the magnitude is hard to interpret. Properties:
   Cov(aX + b, cY + d) = a c Cov(X, Y);
   Cov(X, Y) = Cov(Y, X); Var(X + Y) = Var X + Var Y + 2 Cov(X, Y);
   independent => Cov = 0 (converse FALSE).
G. EXAMPLE: x = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5; y = 2, 4, 5, 4, 5. x_bar = 3, y_bar = 4.
   dx: -2, -1, 0, 1, 2; dy: -2, 0, 1, 0, 1.
   Products: 4, 0, 0, 0, 2: sum 6. s_xy = 6/4 = 1.5 (positive).
   Doubling y plus 10 (y' = 2y + 10): s_xy' = 2*1.5 = 3.0 (covariance
   changes with units).
H. INTERPRETATION: the sign gives the direction of linear association; the
   size depends on units and variances (so cannot compare strength).
I. MISTAKES: reading magnitude as strength; dividing by n vs n - 1
   inconsistently (state the convention); concluding independence from
   zero covariance.
J. RELATED: covariance matrix (Module 10), correlation.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.2 PEARSON CORRELATION COEFFICIENT
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F32.2  PEARSON CORRELATION COEFFICIENT
A. NAME: Pearson product-moment correlation coefficient.
B. FORMULAS
     rho = Cov(X, Y) / (sigma_X sigma_Y)                      (population)
     r = s_xy / (s_x s_y)
       = sum (x_i - x_bar)(y_i - y_bar) / sqrt( sum (x_i - x_bar)^2 *
                                              sum (y_i - y_bar)^2 )
       = (1/(n - 1)) sum z_xi z_yi   (z = standardized values)
C. SYMBOLS: s_x, s_y sample standard deviations; z_xi = (x_i - x_bar)/s_x.
   The n or n - 1 factors cancel between numerator and denominator, so r
   is the same whichever convention is used consistently.
D. CONCEPT: covariance rescaled to be UNIT-FREE: the average product of
   standardized values. Geometrically, r is the COSINE of the angle
   between the centered data vectors (Module 10): r = cos(theta). Hence
   -1 <= r <= 1 by Cauchy-Schwarz.
E. CONDITIONS & PROPERTIES
     - Measures LINEAR association only.
     - r = +1 or -1 iff all points lie exactly on a straight line (positive or
       negative slope); r = 0 means NO LINEAR association (not
       independence).
     - Symmetric: r(X, Y) = r(Y, X).
     - Needs non-zero variance in both variables.
     - Sensitive to outliers and to restricted range (selecting only high
       values of x lowers r).
     - In simple linear regression r^2 = R^2, the share of variance of y
       explained by x; slope b1 = r s_y / s_x.
F. DERIVATION OF THE BOUNDS: the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
   |sum a_i b_i|^2 <= sum a_i^2 sum b_i^2, applied to a_i = x_i - x_bar,
   b_i = y_i - y_bar, gives |r| <= 1, with equality iff b is a multiple of a.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: x = 1..5, y = 2, 4, 5, 4, 5:
      sum dx dy = 6; sum dx^2 = 4 + 1 + 0 + 1 + 4 = 10;
      sum dy^2 = 4 + 0 + 1 + 0 + 1 = 6.
      r = 6/sqrt(10 * 6) = 6/7.746 = 0.7746.
      r^2 = 0.6: 60% of the variance of y is linearly explained by x.
   Moderate 1 (nonlinear): x = -2, -1, 0, 1, 2 and y = x^2 = 4, 1, 0, 1, 4:
      x_bar = 0, y_bar = 2; sum dx dy = (-2)(2) + (-1)(-1) + 0 + (1)(-1) + (2)(2)
      = -4 + 1 - 1 + 4 = 0, so r = 0 although y is a perfect function of x.
      (Correlation misses symmetric nonlinear dependence.)
   Moderate 2 (perfect line): y = 3 - 2x gives r = -1 exactly.
H. INTERPRETATION: sign = direction; |r| = strength of the LINEAR
   relationship. Rough guide (context-dependent!): |r| < 0.3 weak,
   0.3-0.7 moderate, > 0.7 strong. In noisy social data r = 0.3 can be
   notable; in physics r = 0.95 might be poor.
I. MISTAKES: interpreting r as a percentage of association (r = 0.5 is not
   "50% related": r^2 = 0.25); interpreting r = 0 as independence;
   computing r on data with an outlier/leverage point without looking at
   the scatter plot; correlating ratios sharing a common denominator
   (spurious correlation); using it for categorical or strongly
   nonlinear relations.
J. RELATED: Spearman's rank correlation [SUPPLEMENTARY] = Pearson r on the
   RANKS: robust to outliers and detects monotonic (not only linear)
   relations; for the example above it is 0.738. Kendall's tau is another
   rank correlation. Point-biserial and phi coefficients for binary data.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.3 POSITIVE AND NEGATIVE CORRELATION; STRENGTH
------------------------------------------------------------------------
  r > 0: as x increases y tends to increase (height and weight).
  r < 0: as x increases y tends to decrease (price and quantity demanded).
  |r| near 1: points close to a line; |r| near 0: little linear pattern.
  Strength is about the SCATTER around the line, not about the slope:
  two datasets can have the same slope but different r.
  r = 0.9 with slope 0.01 means a very tight, nearly flat line.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.4 CORRELATION vs CAUSATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
STATEMENT: a correlation between X and Y does NOT establish that X causes Y.
POSSIBLE EXPLANATIONS for an observed correlation
   1. X causes Y.
   2. Y causes X (REVERSE CAUSATION): cities with more police have more
      crime; more crime leads to more police.
   3. A CONFOUNDER Z causes both: ice-cream sales and drownings correlate
      because hot weather increases both.
   4. COINCIDENCE or data dredging (test many pairs and some will appear
      correlated by chance; Module 39 multiple testing); trending series
      (two increasing time series correlate spuriously).
   5. Selection bias or collider conditioning.
   6. Aggregation effects (Simpson's paradox).
HOW CAUSAL CLAIMS ARE SUPPORTED: RANDOMIZED CONTROLLED EXPERIMENTS (A/B
tests) break links with confounders; observational causal inference
(adjustment, matching, instrumental variables, difference-in-differences,
causal graphs) needs explicit assumptions.
DATA SCIENCE EXAMPLE: a model finds that customers who use the premium
support feature churn less. Support may not cause retention: engaged
customers may be both likelier to use the feature and to stay (a
confounder). A feature importance is not a causal effect.
MISTAKES: "x predicts y, therefore x causes y"; policy action from
correlational features; ignoring lurking variables.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
32.5 EFFECT OF SCALING AND TRANSFORMATIONS; INVARIANCE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F32.3  INVARIANCE OF CORRELATION UNDER LINEAR TRANSFORMATIONS
A. NAME: Invariance under positive linear scaling (and shifts).
B. FORMULA: for constants a, b, c, d with a != 0, c != 0:
        r(aX + b, cY + d) = sign(a c) * r(X, Y).
   Thus r is UNCHANGED by any shift and by positive rescaling (a, c > 0),
   and only changes sign if exactly one scale factor is negative.
   By contrast Cov(aX + b, cY + d) = a c Cov(X, Y) (changes magnitude).
C. SYMBOLS: a, c scale factors; b, d shifts; X, Y variables; r the
   correlation.
D. CONCEPT: correlation is computed from standardized values, and standardizing
   removes shifts (subtract mean) and positive scalings (divide by SD).
E. CONDITIONS: holds exactly for linear changes of units; not for
   nonlinear transformations.
F. DERIVATION: Cov(aX + b, cY + d) = a c Cov(X,Y); SDs scale by |a| and |c|:
   r' = a c Cov / (|a| |c| s_x s_y) = sign(a c) r.
G. EXAMPLE: x = 1..5, y = 2, 4, 5, 4, 5 with r = 0.7746.
   Convert y to y' = 2y + 10: r = 0.7746 (same), covariance 3.0 (doubled).
   Convert y to y'' = -y: r = -0.7746; covariance -1.5.
   Celsius to Fahrenheit for a temperature variable leaves r unchanged.
H. INTERPRETATION: units do not matter for correlation; they do for
   covariance, which is why correlation is used to compare strengths.
NONLINEAR TRANSFORMATIONS DO CHANGE r: correlating log(x) and log(y) can
differ greatly from correlating x and y; monotone transforms keep
Spearman's rank correlation unchanged.
OTHER EFFECTS: outliers/leverage points can create or destroy r; restricting
the range of x lowers |r|; aggregating (averaging) data by groups usually
inflates |r| (ecological correlation); measurement error in x or y biases
|r| toward 0 (attenuation).
I. COMMON MISTAKES: expecting r to be unchanged by log transforms;
   forgetting that a negative scale factor flips the sign.
J. RELATED: standardization (F42.1), Spearman correlation (F32.2).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: correlation matrices and heatmaps for feature screening;
high correlation between predictors = multicollinearity (unstable
coefficients; variance inflation factor VIF); correlation with the target
is a screening heuristic that misses nonlinear and interaction effects;
standardizing features does not change correlations but puts covariance
and correlation matrices on the same footing (the covariance matrix of
standardized data IS the correlation matrix).

COMPARISON TABLE: COVARIANCE vs CORRELATION
  Aspect               Covariance                       Correlation (Pearson r)
  Formula              sum dx dy/(n-1)                  Cov/(s_x s_y)
  Units                unit_x * unit_y                  none (dimensionless)
  Range                (-inf, inf)                      [-1, 1]
  Changes with units?  yes (scales with a, c)           no (for positive scalings)
  Compares strength?   no                               yes (roughly)
  Sign                 direction of linear trend        direction of linear trend
  Zero means           no linear association            no linear association
  Used in              covariance matrix, PCA,          feature screening, heatmaps,
                       portfolio variance, Gaussians    interpretation
  Relation             r = Cov(X,Y)/(s_x s_y); Cov = r s_x s_y


========================================================================
C2. PROBABILITY
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 33: PROBABILITY FOUNDATIONS
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Probability is a number between 0 and 1 that measures how likely an
uncertain outcome is. 0 means impossible, 1 means certain. It gives a
consistent language for reasoning about uncertainty, which is the basis
of statistics and machine learning.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.1 SAMPLE SPACES AND EVENTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   RANDOM EXPERIMENT: a process whose result is uncertain but whose
      possible results are known (roll a die, test a patient, observe
      tomorrow's temperature).
   OUTCOME: one possible result of the experiment.
   SAMPLE SPACE (Omega): the set of ALL possible outcomes.
   EVENT: a subset of the sample space (a collection of outcomes). The
      event "occurs" if the outcome is one of its members.
   ELEMENTARY (simple) EVENT: a single outcome.
   CERTAIN event: Omega. IMPOSSIBLE event: the empty set.
EXAMPLES
   Die: Omega = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6}. Event A = "even" = {2, 4, 6}.
   Two coin tosses: Omega = {HH, HT, TH, TT} (4 equally likely outcomes).
      "At least one head" = {HH, HT, TH}. NOTE: the outcomes HT and TH are
      different; treating "one head, one tail" as a single outcome gives
      wrong probabilities.
   Two dice: Omega has 36 ordered pairs (d1, d2). Event "sum = 7" has 6
      outcomes: (1,6), (2,5), (3,4), (4,3), (5,2), (6,1).
   Continuous: time until a customer arrives: Omega = [0, infinity).
      Probabilities are then assigned to intervals, not single points
      (Module 35).
   Classifier output: Omega = {spam, not spam}.
F33.1  PROBABILITY AXIOMS (KOLMOGOROV) AND CONSEQUENCES
B. A probability measure P assigns a number to each event so that
     (1) P(A) >= 0 for every event A;
     (2) P(Omega) = 1;
     (3) if A_1, A_2, ... are MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE (pairwise disjoint) events,
         P(A_1 U A_2 U ...) = P(A_1) + P(A_2) + ...
   CONSEQUENCES: P(empty set) = 0; 0 <= P(A) <= 1; if A is a subset of B then
   P(A) <= P(B); P(A^c) = 1 - P(A).
C. SYMBOLS: U union ("or"); A^c complement ("not A").
D. CONCEPT: everything else in probability follows from these three rules.
E. HOW PROBABILITIES ARE ASSIGNED
     CLASSICAL (equally likely outcomes, finite Omega):
         P(A) = |A| / |Omega|  (number of favorable / total outcomes).
         Valid ONLY if the outcomes are genuinely equally likely.
     EMPIRICAL (relative frequency): P(A) approx (times A occurred)/(number
         of trials), converging as trials grow (law of large numbers).
     SUBJECTIVE: degree of belief (Bayesian).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: fair die, P(even) = 3/6 = 0.5. Two dice: P(sum = 7) = 6/36 = 1/6.
   Moderate: loaded die with P(1) = 0.1, P(2) = 0.1, P(3) = 0.1, P(4) = 0.1,
      P(5) = 0.1, P(6) = 0.5 (sums to 1): P(even) = 0.1 + 0.1 + 0.5 = 0.7:
      the classical formula would be wrong because outcomes are not
      equally likely.
H. INTERPRETATION: every rule of probability follows from three simple
   axioms.
I. MISTAKES: assuming outcomes are equally likely when they are not (e.g.
   "it either rains or it does not, so 50%"); unordered outcomes for
   multi-object experiments; probabilities that do not sum to 1.
J. RELATED: complement rule (F33.2), addition rule (F33.3), conditional
   probability (F34.1).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.2 EVENT OPERATIONS, COMPLEMENTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
   UNION A U B: A occurs OR B occurs (or both).
   INTERSECTION A n B (written AB): BOTH occur.
   COMPLEMENT A^c: A does NOT occur. A U A^c = Omega, A n A^c = empty.
   DIFFERENCE A \ B (A and not B) = A n B^c.
   MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE (disjoint): A n B = empty (cannot both occur).
   EXHAUSTIVE: A_1 U ... U A_k = Omega. A PARTITION is a collection that is
      both mutually exclusive and exhaustive.
   DE MORGAN (sets): (A U B)^c = A^c n B^c; (A n B)^c = A^c U B^c.
   SUBSET: A subset of B means A occurring forces B to occur.
EXAMPLE (die): A = {2, 4, 6} (even), B = {4, 5, 6} (greater than 3).
   A U B = {2, 4, 5, 6}; A n B = {4, 6}; A^c = {1, 3, 5};
   A \ B = {2}; (A U B)^c = {1, 3} = A^c n B^c = {1,3,5} n {1,2,3} = {1, 3}. OK.
Venn diagrams help, but write out the event as a set.

F33.2  COMPLEMENT RULE
B. P(A^c) = 1 - P(A).
C. SYMBOLS: A an event; A^c its complement; P(none) the probability that
   no event occurs.
D. CONCEPT: either A happens or it does not. Powerful for "at least one"
   problems: P(at least one) = 1 - P(none).
E. CONDITIONS: always valid; for 'at least one of independent events'
   use 1 minus the product of the (1 - p_i).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: P(not 6 on a die) = 1 - 1/6 = 5/6.
   Moderate: P(at least one 6 in 4 rolls) = 1 - (5/6)^4 = 1 - 625/1296
      = 671/1296 = 0.5177.
   Moderate 2 (birthday problem): in a group of 23 people the probability
      that at least two share a birthday (365 equally likely days, ignore
      leap years) is 1 - (365/365)(364/365)...(343/365) = 0.5073. Counting
      the complement ("all different") is far easier than counting all
      the ways to have matches.
H. INTERPRETATION: counting the complement is often far easier (birthday
   problem, system reliability).
I. MISTAKE: computing "at least one" by adding single-event
   probabilities (double counts; see addition rule).
J. RELATED: addition rule (F33.3), independence (F34.2).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.3 ADDITION AND MULTIPLICATION RULES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F33.3  ADDITION RULE
A. NAME: Addition rule (inclusion-exclusion).
B. FORMULAS
     P(A U B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A n B)
     If A and B are mutually exclusive: P(A U B) = P(A) + P(B).
     Three events: P(A U B U C) = P(A) + P(B) + P(C) - P(AB) - P(AC) - P(BC)
                                  + P(ABC).
C. SYMBOLS: P(A n B) probability both occur.
D. CONCEPT: adding P(A) and P(B) counts the overlap A n B twice, so subtract
   it once.
E. CONDITIONS: always valid (the disjoint form needs mutual exclusivity).
F. DERIVATION: A U B = A + (B \ A) (disjoint), and B = (A n B) + (B \ A);
   so P(A U B) = P(A) + P(B) - P(A n B).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: die, A = even {2,4,6}, B = greater than 3 {4,5,6}:
      P(A U B) = 3/6 + 3/6 - 2/6 = 4/6 = 2/3 (check: {2,4,5,6}, 4 outcomes).
   Moderate: 60% of customers buy product X, 50% buy Y, 30% buy both.
      P(X or Y) = 0.6 + 0.5 - 0.3 = 0.8; P(neither) = 0.2.
      P(only X) = 0.6 - 0.3 = 0.3.
H. INTERPRETATION: subtract the overlap to avoid double counting; the
   three-event version alternately adds and subtracts.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting to subtract the overlap; using the disjoint form
   when events overlap (can give probability above 1: a warning sign).
J. RELATED: counting (F33.5 to F33.7), conditional probability (F34.1).

F33.4  MULTIPLICATION RULE (INTRODUCTION)
B. P(A n B) = P(A) P(B | A) = P(B) P(A | B)  (general, Module 34)
   If A and B are INDEPENDENT: P(A n B) = P(A) P(B).
C. SYMBOLS: P(A n B) the joint probability; P(B | A) the conditional
   probability; independence makes P(B | A) = P(B).
D. CONCEPT: both events must happen: the chance of A, times the chance
   of B once A has happened.
G. EXAMPLES: two fair dice both show 6: (1/6)(1/6) = 1/36.
   Three independent tests each pass with probability 0.9: P(all pass)
   = 0.9^3 = 0.729; P(at least one fails) = 1 - 0.729 = 0.271.
E. INDEPENDENCE must be justified (separate coin tosses: yes; two cards
   drawn without replacement: no). The multiplication rule with
   conditional probabilities is the general form.
H. INTERPRETATION: independent chances multiply; dependent ones need
   conditional probabilities.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: multiplying probabilities of dependent events;
   confusing 'and' (multiply) with 'or' (add and subtract the overlap).
J. RELATED: chain rule (F34.3), independence (F34.2).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.4 COUNTING PRINCIPLES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
For equally likely outcomes, probability is a counting problem.
F33.5  FUNDAMENTAL COUNTING PRINCIPLES
B. MULTIPLICATION PRINCIPLE: if a task has two stages, with m ways for stage 1
   and n ways for stage 2 (independent of stage 1), there are m * n ways
   overall. Extends to any number of stages.
   ADDITION PRINCIPLE: if a task can be done in one of two DISJOINT ways,
   with m and n options respectively, there are m + n options.
   FACTORIAL: n! = n (n-1) ... 2 * 1; 0! = 1. Number of ways to arrange n
   distinct objects in a row.
C. SYMBOLS: m, n the numbers of options; n! the factorial.
D. CONCEPT: independent stages multiply; exclusive alternatives add.
E. CONDITIONS: the number of options at a later stage must not depend on
   earlier choices (otherwise count case by case).
G. EXAMPLES: a license code of 3 letters then 2 digits: 26^3 * 10^2 = 1,757,600.
   An outfit from 4 shirts, 3 trousers, 2 shoes: 4 * 3 * 2 = 24.
   Arrangements of 5 distinct people in a line: 5! = 120.
H. INTERPRETATION: counting is the engine of classical probability and
   of complexity analysis (2^n subsets).
I. MISTAKES: adding when stages should be multiplied (and vice versa);
   forgetting restrictions (no repeats).
J. RELATED: permutations (F33.6), combinations (F33.7).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.5 PERMUTATIONS AND COMBINATIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F33.6  PERMUTATIONS
A. NAME: Permutations (ordered selections).
B. FORMULAS
     Ordered selections of k from n DISTINCT objects, no repetition:
        P(n, k) = n! / (n - k)! = n (n-1) ... (n-k+1)
     With repetition allowed: n^k.
     Arrangements of n objects with repeats (n_1 identical of type 1, ...):
        n! / (n_1! n_2! ... n_r!)
C. SYMBOLS: n number of objects available; k number chosen; n! factorial.
D. CONCEPT: ORDER MATTERS. Choose the first (n ways), second (n-1), ... k-th
   (n-k+1) and multiply; the formula packages that product.
E. CONDITIONS: distinct objects for the first two formulas; 0 <= k <= n.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: 1st, 2nd, 3rd prizes among 5 runners: P(5,3) = 5*4*3 = 60.
   Moderate: distinct arrangements of the letters of MISSISSIPPI (M 1, I 4,
      S 4, P 2; 11 letters): 11!/(1! 4! 4! 2!) = 39,916,800/(24*24*2)
      = 34,650.
   Moderate 2: 4-digit PIN with digits allowed to repeat: 10^4 = 10,000;
      with no repeated digit: P(10, 4) = 5,040.
H. INTERPRETATION: P(n, k) counts ordered lists: arrangements of a
   ranking or a sequence.
I. MISTAKES: using this when order does not matter; forgetting the division
   for identical items.
J. RELATED: combinations (F33.7), factorial growth (Module 17).

F33.7  COMBINATIONS
A. NAME: Combinations (unordered selections) and binomial coefficients.
B. FORMULAS
     C(n, k) = n! / ( k! (n - k)! ) = P(n, k) / k!
     Properties: C(n, k) = C(n, n - k); C(n, 0) = C(n, n) = 1;
        C(n, 1) = n; C(n, k) = C(n-1, k-1) + C(n-1, k) (Pascal);
        sum[k=0..n] C(n, k) = 2^n.
     Multisets (choose k from n types WITH repetition, order irrelevant):
        C(n + k - 1, k)  [SUPPLEMENTARY]
C. SYMBOLS: C(n, k) "n choose k" (also written with n over k, or nCk).
D. CONCEPT: choose which k items form the group; each group of k can be
   arranged in k! orders, which we do NOT want to count separately, so
   divide the permutations by k!.
E. CONDITIONS: 0 <= k <= n; selections without repetition (basic version).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: choose a committee of 3 from 10 people: C(10, 3) = 120.
   Moderate: committee of 4 from 6 men and 5 women with at least 2 women:
      exactly 2 women: C(5,2) C(6,2) = 10 * 15 = 150; 3 women: C(5,3) C(6,1)
      = 10 * 6 = 60; 4 women: C(5,4) = 5. Total 215.
      (Check by complement: all C(11,4) = 330, minus 0 women C(6,4) = 15
      and 1 woman C(5,1) C(6,3) = 100: 330 - 115 = 215. OK.)
   Moderate 2: lottery pick 6 numbers from 49: C(49, 6) = 13,983,816, so
      P(jackpot) = 1/13,983,816 = 7.15 x 10^-8.
H. INTERPRETATION: C(n, k) counts groups; it appears as the binomial
   coefficients and in the binomial distribution.
I. MISTAKES: using permutations when the problem says "group", "committee",
   "hand", "subset"; double counting by treating a combination as ordered.
J. RELATED: binomial theorem (F1.7), binomial distribution (F35.7),
   hypergeometric (F33.8).

COMPARISON TABLE: PERMUTATIONS vs COMBINATIONS
  Aspect               Permutation                  Combination
  Order matters?       YES                          NO
  Counts               arrangements/sequences       groups/subsets
  Formula (no repeat)  n!/(n-k)!                    n!/(k!(n-k)!)
  Relationship         P(n,k) = C(n,k) * k!         C(n,k) = P(n,k)/k!
  Keywords             arrange, rank, schedule,     choose, select, committee,
                       PIN, race positions          hand, team, sample
  Example n=5, k=3     60                           10
  Test question: "Would swapping two chosen items give a different result?"
  If yes: permutation; if no: combination.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
33.6 SAMPLING WITHOUT REPLACEMENT
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   WITH REPLACEMENT: each selected item is returned before the next draw, so
      the draws are INDEPENDENT with unchanged probabilities.
   WITHOUT REPLACEMENT: items are not returned; the composition changes,
      making the draws DEPENDENT.
   (Simple random sample of n units from N is drawn without replacement.)
F33.8  PROBABILITY FOR SAMPLING WITHOUT REPLACEMENT (HYPERGEOMETRIC)
B. POPULATION of N items with K "successes". Draw n WITHOUT replacement.
        P( exactly k successes ) = C(K, k) C(N - K, n - k) / C(N, n).
   Sequence form (chain rule): P(first two are successes) =
        (K/N) * ((K - 1)/(N - 1)).
C. SYMBOLS: N population size; K number of successes in the population; n
   sample size; k successes observed.
D. CONCEPT: count samples with exactly k successes (choose which successes
   and which failures) over all equally likely samples of size n.
E. CONDITIONS: equally likely samples (random draw); without replacement.
   If n is small relative to N (rule of thumb n < 0.05 N or 0.1 N) the
   binomial approximation (Module 35) is adequate.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (urn): 5 red and 3 blue balls; draw 2 without replacement.
      P(both red) = (5/8)(4/7) = 20/56 = 5/14 = 0.357.
      With replacement it would be (5/8)^2 = 0.391 (different!).
   Moderate (cards): 5-card hand from 52 cards. P(exactly 2 hearts)
      = C(13,2) C(39,3) / C(52,5) = 78 * 9,139 / 2,598,960 = 712,842/
      2,598,960 = 0.2743. P(all 5 hearts) = C(13,5)/C(52,5) = 1287/2,598,960
      = 0.000495.
   Moderate 2 (chain rule): P(three hearts in a row) = (13/52)(12/51)(11/50)
      = 1716/132,600 = 0.01294.
   Data science: choosing a random validation set of 20 from 100 samples
      with 30 positives: the number of positives in it follows a
      hypergeometric distribution.
H. INTERPRETATION: each individual draw, viewed unconditionally, has the same
   probability of being a success (K/N), even though successive draws are
   dependent (exchangeability).
I. MISTAKES: using independent-trial (binomial) formulas for small
   populations; forgetting that denominators shrink (N, N-1, N-2, ...).
J. RELATED: binomial distribution (F35.7; the independent-trials
   approximation), combinations (F33.7), finite population correction
   (F37.2).

COMPARISON TABLE: SAMPLING WITH vs WITHOUT REPLACEMENT
  Aspect                With replacement         Without replacement
  Draws                 independent              dependent
  Probability per draw  constant                 changes
  Number of ordered     N^n                      N!/(N-n)!
  samples
  Number of unordered   (multisets) C(N+n-1, n)  C(N, n)
  samples
  Distribution of #     binomial(n, K/N)         hypergeometric
  successes
  Variance of count     n p (1-p)                n p (1-p)(N-n)/(N-1) (smaller)
  Machine learning      bootstrap resampling     train/test split, k-fold CV
DATA SCIENCE LINK (Module 33): sample spaces underlie train/test splitting;
counting subsets underlies feature selection (2^p subsets); permutation
tests; combinatorics in the number of possible trees/splits; the
birthday paradox explains hash collisions (Module 18).


========================================================================
MODULE 34: CONDITIONAL PROBABILITY
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.1 CONDITIONAL PROBABILITY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Probabilities change when you learn something. The probability that a
card is a king is 4/52, but if you learn that it is a face card, it
becomes 4/12. Conditional probability is "the probability of A, given
that we know B has happened".
F34.1  CONDITIONAL PROBABILITY
A. NAME: Conditional probability.
B. FORMULA:   P(A | B) = P(A n B) / P(B),   P(B) > 0.
C. SYMBOLS: P(A | B) read "probability of A given B"; P(A n B) probability of
   both; P(B) the probability of the conditioning event.
D. CONCEPT: restrict attention to the outcomes in B (B becomes the new,
   smaller sample space) and ask what fraction of them also lie in A.
   Dividing by P(B) re-normalizes so that P(B | B) = 1.
E. CONDITIONS: requires P(B) > 0. Conditional probabilities obey all the
   axioms (e.g. P(A^c | B) = 1 - P(A | B)). In general P(A | B) != P(B | A).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (die): A = {5, 6} (at least 5), B = {2, 4, 6} (even).
      P(A n B) = P({6}) = 1/6; P(B) = 1/2; P(A | B) = (1/6)/(1/2) = 1/3.
      (Directly: of the 3 even outcomes only 6 is in A.)
   Moderate (two-way table): 200 customers: 120 are subscribers; 80 are
      not. Of the subscribers, 30 churned; of the non-subscribers, 40
      churned. P(churn) = 70/200 = 0.35.
      P(churn | subscriber) = 30/120 = 0.25.
      P(churn | not subscriber) = 40/80 = 0.5.
      P(subscriber | churn) = 30/70 = 0.4286 (different from
      P(churn | subscriber) = 0.25: direction matters).
   Moderate 2 (two cards): P(second is an ace | first was an ace) = 3/51.
H. INTERPRETATION: conditioning on information changes probabilities;
   features in a classifier are exactly conditioning information:
   P(y | x).
I. COMMON MISTAKES: the "confusion of the inverse" (treating P(A | B) as
   P(B | A)): "most terrorists are X" is not "most X are terrorists";
   conditioning on the wrong event; forgetting to restrict the sample
   space; dividing by P(A) instead of P(B).
J. RELATED: multiplication rule, Bayes' theorem.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.2 INDEPENDENT AND DEPENDENT EVENTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F34.2  INDEPENDENCE
A. NAME: Independence of events.
B. FORMULAS: A and B are INDEPENDENT iff
        P(A n B) = P(A) P(B)
   equivalently P(A | B) = P(A) (if P(B) > 0) and P(B | A) = P(B):
   knowing one tells nothing about the other.
   Several events A_1..A_n are MUTUALLY independent if the product rule
   holds for EVERY sub-collection (pairwise independence alone is not
   enough). If A and B are independent so are A and B^c, A^c and B^c.
   CONDITIONAL independence: A and B independent GIVEN C if
   P(A n B | C) = P(A | C) P(B | C).
C. SYMBOLS: as above.
D. CONCEPT: the occurrence of B does not shift the probability of A.
E. CONDITIONS: independence is an ASSUMPTION to be justified (by the physical
   process, e.g. separate dice, or tested from data), not something to
   assume because the events "feel unrelated".
G. EXAMPLES
   Independent (die): A = even {2,4,6}, B = at most 4 {1,2,3,4}: P(A) = 1/2,
      P(B) = 2/3, P(A n B) = P({2,4}) = 1/3 = (1/2)(2/3): INDEPENDENT.
   Dependent (die): A = even, B = at least 4 {4,5,6}: P(A n B) = P({4,6})
      = 1/3, but P(A) P(B) = (1/2)(1/2) = 1/4: DEPENDENT.
   Coins: each toss independent: P(HHH) = 1/8.
   Dependent draws: two cards without replacement: P(both aces) =
      (4/52)(3/51) = 1/221 = 0.00452, not (4/52)^2 = 0.00592.
H. INTERPRETATION: independence means that learning one event tells
   nothing about the other.
I. COMMON MISTAKES (very frequent)
   - Confusing MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE with INDEPENDENT. If A and B are mutually
     exclusive and both have positive probability they are DEPENDENT
     (if A occurs, B cannot: P(B | A) = 0 != P(B)).
   - The gambler's fallacy: after 5 heads in a row the next toss is still
     50% heads (independent tosses).
   - Assuming independence for convenience (multiplying probabilities of
     correlated events understates joint risk).
J. RELATED: conditional probability (F34.1), multiplication rule
   (F34.3), covariance (F32.1).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: "naive" Bayes assumes features are conditionally
independent given the class; i.i.d. data (independent and identically
distributed) is the standard modeling assumption; time series and
clustered data violate independence (inflating confidence).

COMPARISON TABLE: MUTUALLY EXCLUSIVE vs INDEPENDENT vs DEPENDENT
  Aspect               Mutually exclusive        Independent              Dependent
  Definition           A n B = empty             P(AB) = P(A)P(B)         P(AB) != P(A)P(B)
  P(A n B)             0                         P(A) P(B)                P(A) P(B | A)
  P(A U B)             P(A) + P(B)               P(A)+P(B)-P(A)P(B)       P(A)+P(B)-P(A n B)
  P(A | B)             0                         P(A)                     differs from P(A)
  Information          B occurring rules out A   B tells nothing about A  B shifts probability of A
  Relation             exclusive (both P > 0) => dependent
  Example (die)        {1,2} and {5,6}           even and <= 4            even and >= 4

------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.3 THE MULTIPLICATION RULE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F34.3  GENERAL MULTIPLICATION (CHAIN) RULE
B. P(A n B) = P(A) P(B | A)
   P(A_1 n A_2 n ... n A_n) = P(A_1) P(A_2 | A_1) P(A_3 | A_1 A_2) ...
                              P(A_n | A_1 ... A_(n-1))
C. SYMBOLS: A_1, ..., A_n events in order; P(A_k | A_1 ... A_(k-1)) the
   probability of the k-th given all previous ones.
D. CONCEPT: the probability of a sequence of events is the probability of the
   first, times the probability of the second given the first, and so
   on (rearranging the definition of conditional probability).
   Tree diagrams multiply along branches.
E. CONDITIONS: always valid; simplifies to a product of unconditional
   probabilities when events are independent.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: urn with 4 red, 6 blue; draw two without replacement: P(red then
      blue) = (4/10)(6/9) = 24/90 = 0.2667.
   Moderate: chain: P(three aces in a row from a deck) = (4/52)(3/51)(2/50)
      = 24/132,600 = 0.000181.
   Data science: the joint distribution of a sequence factorizes via the
      chain rule, P(w1, w2, w3) = P(w1) P(w2 | w1) P(w3 | w1, w2): the
      basis of language models and autoregressive models.
H. INTERPRETATION: factorizes a joint probability into steps; tree
   diagrams multiply along branches.
I. MISTAKE: using unconditional probabilities for the later factors when
   events are dependent.
J. RELATED: independence (F34.2), Bayes (F34.5), autoregressive models.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.4 LAW OF TOTAL PROBABILITY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F34.4  LAW OF TOTAL PROBABILITY
A. NAME: Law of total probability.
B. FORMULA: if B_1, ..., B_n form a PARTITION of the sample space (mutually
   exclusive, exhaustive, each with P(B_i) > 0), then for any event A:
        P(A) = sum[i=1..n] P(A | B_i) P(B_i)
   Two-part version: P(A) = P(A | B) P(B) + P(A | B^c) P(B^c).
C. SYMBOLS: B_i the "cases" or "causes"; P(B_i) their probabilities (prior,
   weights); P(A | B_i) the probability of A within each case.
D. CONCEPT: break the problem into cases and take a weighted average of
   the case-wise probabilities, weighted by how likely each case is.
E. CONDITIONS: the B_i MUST partition the whole space (no gaps, no
   overlaps), and the weights sum to 1.
F. DERIVATION: A = (A n B_1) U ... U (A n B_n), disjoint, so P(A) = sum P(A n
   B_i) = sum P(A | B_i) P(B_i).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (factories): machine M1 makes 60% of items with 2% defective;
      machine M2 makes 40% with 5% defective.
      P(defective) = 0.02 * 0.6 + 0.05 * 0.4 = 0.012 + 0.020 = 0.032.
   Moderate (three sources): a model is trained on data from regions R1, R2,
      R3 with shares 50%, 30%, 20% and error rates 10%, 20%, 40%.
      Overall error = 0.5 * 0.10 + 0.3 * 0.20 + 0.2 * 0.40 = 0.05 + 0.06
      + 0.08 = 0.19.
H. INTERPRETATION: the overall rate is a mixture; the same arithmetic
   gives the marginal distribution P(x) = sum_y P(x | y) P(y) in
   classification (the "evidence" in Bayes' theorem).
I. MISTAKES: weights that do not sum to 1; overlapping cases; using
   P(B_i | A) instead of P(A | B_i).
J. RELATED: Bayes' theorem (F34.5; its denominator), marginal
   distributions (Module 35).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.5 BAYES' THEOREM
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F34.5  BAYES' THEOREM
A. NAME: Bayes' theorem (rule).
B. FORMULAS
     P(A | B) = P(B | A) P(A) / P(B)
     With a partition A_1..A_n of the sample space:
        P(A_j | B) = P(B | A_j) P(A_j) / sum_i P(B | A_i) P(A_i)
     Odds form:  posterior odds = prior odds x likelihood ratio,
        P(A | B)/P(A^c | B) = [P(A)/P(A^c)] * [P(B | A)/P(B | A^c)]
C. SYMBOLS
   P(A)      PRIOR probability of A (before seeing B).
   P(B | A)  LIKELIHOOD of the evidence B if A is true.
   P(B)      EVIDENCE / marginal probability of B (from total probability).
   P(A | B)  POSTERIOR probability of A after seeing B.
D. CONCEPT: Bayes reverses a conditional probability. We often know
   P(evidence | cause) (how a test behaves if the disease is present) but
   need P(cause | evidence) (how likely the disease is given a positive
   test). The answer depends on BOTH the test's characteristics and the
   prior (base rate).
E. CONDITIONS: P(B) > 0; priors must be meaningful and sum to 1 over the
   partition. The posterior is only as good as the prior and the
   likelihood model.
F. DERIVATION: from the multiplication rule P(A n B) = P(A | B) P(B) =
   P(B | A) P(A); divide by P(B). Expand P(B) by total probability.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (factories continued): an item is defective. P(it came from M1) =
      P(def | M1) P(M1)/P(def) = 0.012/0.032 = 0.375. (M1 makes 60% of
      items but only 37.5% of the defects.)
   Moderate (spam filter): 20% of mail is spam. The word "free" appears in
      40% of spam and 5% of non-spam. P(spam | "free")
      = 0.4 * 0.2 / (0.4 * 0.2 + 0.05 * 0.8) = 0.08/(0.08 + 0.04) = 0.667.
      Odds form: prior odds 0.2/0.8 = 0.25; LR = 0.4/0.05 = 8; posterior
      odds 2 -> probability 2/3. OK.
H. INTERPRETATION: evidence with a likelihood ratio of 8 multiplies the odds by
   8. Updating can be repeated: posterior becomes the next prior.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: ignoring the prior (BASE RATE FALLACY); equating
   P(A | B) with P(B | A) (PROSECUTOR'S FALLACY: P(evidence | innocent)
   small does not mean P(innocent | evidence) small); not summing over
   ALL alternative explanations in the denominator.
J. RELATED: Bayesian estimation and priors (Module 38), naive Bayes classifier
   (Module 48), posterior predictive distributions.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
34.6 DIAGNOSTIC TESTING EXAMPLES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
TERMS (D = condition present, + = positive test)
   PREVALENCE: P(D) (the prior / base rate).
   SENSITIVITY (true positive rate, recall): P(+ | D).
   SPECIFICITY (true negative rate): P(- | not D).
   FALSE POSITIVE RATE: P(+ | not D) = 1 - specificity.
   POSITIVE PREDICTIVE VALUE (PPV, precision): P(D | +).
   NEGATIVE PREDICTIVE VALUE (NPV): P(not D | -).
F34.6  PREDICTIVE VALUES FROM SENSITIVITY, SPECIFICITY AND PREVALENCE
B. PPV = (Se * Pr) / ( Se * Pr + (1 - Sp)(1 - Pr) )
   NPV = (Sp (1 - Pr)) / ( Sp (1 - Pr) + (1 - Se) Pr )
C. SYMBOLS: Se sensitivity; Sp specificity; Pr prevalence.
D. CONCEPT: a direct application of Bayes' theorem with the partition
   {D, not D}. The PPV depends strongly on prevalence.
E. CONDITIONS: assumes sensitivity and specificity are the same across
   the population and that Pr is the prevalence in the population where
   the test is applied.
G. EXAMPLE: Se = 0.95, Sp = 0.90, Pr = 0.01: PPV = 0.0095/(0.0095 +
   0.099) = 0.0876; NPV = 0.891/(0.891 + 0.0005) = 0.9994.
H. INTERPRETATION: predictive values depend on prevalence: the same test
   has a high PPV in a high-risk group and a low PPV in general
   screening.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: treating sensitivity as PPV; ignoring prevalence.
J. RELATED: Bayes (F34.5), precision and recall (F46.3), accuracy
   paradox (section 46.8).
WORKED EXAMPLE: prevalence 1%, sensitivity 95%, specificity 90%.
   Method 1 (formula):
      P(+) = 0.95 * 0.01 + 0.10 * 0.99 = 0.0095 + 0.099 = 0.1085.
      PPV = 0.0095/0.1085 = 0.0876 (8.76%).
   Method 2 (natural frequencies, imagine 10,000 people):
      100 have the condition: 95 test positive (true positives), 5 test
      negative. 9,900 do not: 990 test positive (false positives), 8,910
      test negative.
      Positives in total: 95 + 990 = 1,085. PPV = 95/1,085 = 8.76%.
      NPV = 8,910/(8,910 + 5) = 8,910/8,915 = 99.94%.
   Interpretation: although the test seems accurate (95% / 90%), a positive
   result means only about a 9% chance of having the condition, because
   the condition is rare and false positives from the large healthy group
   outnumber true positives roughly 10 to 1.
   Repeat testing: after a first positive the new prior is 0.0876. A second
   independent positive result (same test; assumes conditional
   independence of test errors) gives posterior
   0.95 * 0.0876/(0.95 * 0.0876 + 0.10 * 0.9124) = 0.0832/(0.0832 +
   0.0912) = 0.477.
   Effect of prevalence: at prevalence 20%, PPV = 0.19/(0.19 + 0.08) = 0.704.
H. INTERPRETATION: in screening for rare conditions, even good tests give many
   false alarms; confirmation tests are needed.
I. MISTAKES: reporting "the test is 95% accurate, so a positive means 95%
   likely"; using sensitivity where PPV is needed; forgetting that
   PPV changes when the test is used in a different population.
DATA SCIENCE LINK
   - Precision of a classifier = PPV; recall = sensitivity. With imbalanced
     classes (fraud: 0.1% prevalence) even a model with 99% specificity
     has low precision (check: Se = 0.9, Sp = 0.99, Pr = 0.001 gives
     PPV = 0.0009/(0.0009 + 0.00999) = 8.3%).
   - Accuracy can be misleading when classes are imbalanced (Module 46).
   - Bayesian updating, naive Bayes classifiers, A/B testing posteriors,
     and recommender models all use these rules.
MONTY HALL [SUPPLEMENTARY]: three doors, one car. You pick door 1; the host,
who knows where the car is, opens a goat door among the others. Switching
wins with probability 2/3 (staying 1/3) because the host's action is
informative. Simulation confirms 0.667.


========================================================================
MODULE 35: RANDOM VARIABLES AND DISTRIBUTIONS
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.1 RANDOM VARIABLES: DISCRETE AND CONTINUOUS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
A random variable turns the uncertain outcome of an experiment into a
NUMBER, so that we can use algebra and calculus on it. "The number of
heads in 10 tosses", "tomorrow's temperature", "the time until the next
customer" are random variables.

F35.1  RANDOM VARIABLE
A. NAME: Random variable.
B. DEFINITION: a function X : Omega -> R that assigns a real number X(w) to
   every outcome w of the sample space.
   Notation: capital letter X = the random variable (before the
   experiment); lower-case x = a particular value it may take; the
   event {X = x} means "X takes the value x"; P(X <= x) is the
   probability of the event {w : X(w) <= x}.
C. SYMBOLS: Omega sample space; w an outcome; R the real numbers.
D. CONCEPT: the randomness is in the outcome w; X is an ordinary function that
   reports a numerical feature of it. Two coin tosses: X = number of
   heads takes X(HH) = 2, X(HT) = X(TH) = 1, X(TT) = 0.
KINDS
   DISCRETE: takes a finite or countably infinite set of values (counts,
      categories coded as numbers): number of heads, defects per
      batch, die roll. Described by a PMF.
   CONTINUOUS: takes values in an interval (uncountably many): height,
      time, temperature, error. Described by a PDF.
   (Mixed variables exist: e.g. rainfall is 0 with positive probability
   and continuous otherwise [SUPPLEMENTARY].)
   TEST: if you can list the possible values (even an infinite list
   0, 1, 2, ...), it is discrete; if between any two values there is
   another possible value, it is continuous.
   Note: a measurement recorded to the nearest cm is DISCRETE in practice
   but modeled as continuous when the resolution is fine.
E. CONDITIONS: X must be a function for which P(X <= x) is defined; its
   type (discrete or continuous) determines how probabilities are
   described.
G. EXAMPLES: the number of heads in 2 tosses (values 0, 1, 2); the sum
   of two dice; the height of a random adult; tomorrow's rainfall.
H. INTERPRETATION: random variables let us compute averages, spreads and
   probabilities with algebra and calculus.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: confusing the random variable X with an observed
   value x (a fixed number); treating category labels as numbers without
   justification.
J. RELATED: PMF, PDF and CDF (F35.2 to F35.4), expectation (F36.1).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: features and targets are random variables; a regression
target is modeled as continuous, a classification target as
discrete (Bernoulli/categorical); the model estimates the distribution
(or its mean) of Y given X.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.2 PROBABILITY MASS FUNCTION (PMF)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.2  PMF
A. NAME: Probability mass function (discrete variables).
B. FORMULA:   p(x) = P(X = x)
   Requirements: p(x) >= 0 for all x;  sum over all x of p(x) = 1.
   Probability of an event: P(X in S) = sum[x in S] p(x).
C. SYMBOLS: p(x) (also p_X(x)) the probability that X equals exactly x.
D. CONCEPT: a table (or formula) listing each possible value with its
   probability: the probability "mass" placed at each point.
E. CONDITIONS: only for discrete variables; each p(x) is a genuine probability
   (between 0 and 1).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: fair die: p(x) = 1/6 for x = 1..6; P(X >= 5) = 2/6.
   Moderate: sum S of two fair dice: values 2..12 with counts
      1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 5, 4, 3, 2, 1 out of 36:
      p(2) = 1/36, p(7) = 6/36, p(12) = 1/36. P(S >= 10) = (3 + 2 + 1)/36
      = 1/6. P(S is even) = (1 + 3 + 5 + 5 + 3 + 1)/36 = 18/36 = 1/2.
H. INTERPRETATION: the heights of the bars of a bar chart of
   probabilities.
I. MISTAKES: PMF values not summing to 1; using a PMF for a continuous
   variable.
J. RELATED: CDF (F35.4), binomial (F35.7), expectation (F36.1).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.3 PROBABILITY DENSITY FUNCTION (PDF)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.3  PDF
A. NAME: Probability density function (continuous variables).
B. FORMULA: f(x) is a PDF of X if
        f(x) >= 0 for all x;   int[-inf..inf] f(x) dx = 1;
        P(a <= X <= b) = int[a..b] f(x) dx.
   Interpretation of the value: f(x) dx approx P(x <= X <= x + dx).
C. SYMBOLS: f(x) (also f_X(x)) the density at x; the integral gives AREA under
   the curve.
D. CONCEPT: probability is AREA under the density curve, not the height.
   Density = probability per unit length: like the density of a material
   (mass per volume), whose mass requires integrating over a region.
   The density height can exceed 1 (e.g. U(0, 0.5) has f = 2 on its
   support); only the AREA must be at most 1.
E. CONDITIONS: the density must integrate to 1 (normalization); non-negative;
   improper integrals must converge (Module 4). Distinguish the density
   f(x) (not a probability) from the probability P(X = x) = 0 for any
   single x (Module 36).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: f(x) = 2x for 0 <= x <= 1 (0 elsewhere). Check: int[0..1] 2x dx = 1.
      P(0.5 <= X <= 0.8) = [x^2] from 0.5 to 0.8 = 0.64 - 0.25 = 0.39.
   Moderate: find c so that f(x) = c x^2 on [0, 3] is a density:
      int[0..3] c x^2 dx = c * 9 = 1 -> c = 1/9. P(X <= 2) = (1/9)(8/3)
      = 8/27 = 0.296.
   Normalization constants in practice: the normal density's factor
      1/(sigma sqrt(2 pi)) exists to make the integral 1 (F4: Gaussian
      integral).
H. INTERPRETATION: a density shows where probability is concentrated;
   areas, not heights, are probabilities.
I. MISTAKES: treating f(x) as a probability (it may exceed 1); forgetting
   to check normalization; integrating over the wrong limits; confusing
   f with the CDF F.
J. RELATED: histogram (F30.1), CDF (F35.4), improper integrals (F4.8).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.4 CUMULATIVE DISTRIBUTION FUNCTION (CDF)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.4  CDF
A. NAME: Cumulative distribution function.
B. FORMULAS (valid for both discrete and continuous X)
     F(x) = P(X <= x)
     Discrete:    F(x) = sum[t <= x] p(t)       (a STEP function)
     Continuous:  F(x) = int[-inf..x] f(t) dt    (a continuous curve)
                  f(x) = F'(x) wherever F is differentiable
     Interval probability: P(a < X <= b) = F(b) - F(a)
     Properties: non-decreasing; right-continuous; F(-inf) = 0, F(+inf) = 1;
        0 <= F(x) <= 1; P(X > x) = 1 - F(x) (SURVIVAL function).
     Quantile (inverse CDF): the p-quantile is x_p with F(x_p) = p; the median
        solves F(x) = 0.5.
C. SYMBOLS: F(x) running total of probability up to x; t the dummy variable.
D. CONCEPT: "how much probability lies at or below x". It always exists
   (even for mixed variables) and is the most general description of a
   distribution. Jumps in F correspond to point masses (discrete part);
   the size of a jump at x equals P(X = x).
E. CONDITIONS: F always exists; it is right-continuous; for continuous X
   it is continuous, for discrete X it jumps.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (discrete): die: F(3) = 3/6 = 0.5; F(3.7) = 0.5 (flat between
      jumps); F(0) = 0; F(6) = 1.
   Moderate (continuous): f(x) = 2x on [0, 1]: F(x) = 0 for x < 0;
      F(x) = x^2 on [0, 1]; F(x) = 1 for x > 1.
      P(0.5 < X <= 0.8) = F(0.8) - F(0.5) = 0.64 - 0.25 = 0.39 (as before).
      Median: x^2 = 0.5 -> x = 0.7071. 90th percentile: x = sqrt(0.9) = 0.9487.
      Differentiating F gives back f(x) = 2x.
H. INTERPRETATION: reading quantiles from a CDF gives percentiles; the
   inverse CDF is used to simulate random numbers (inverse-transform
   sampling, Module 36); the ECDF (Module 30) estimates F from data;
   the ROC curve and the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test are CDF-based.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting that F accumulates from the left; using P(a < X < b)
   = F(b) - F(a) for discrete variables without care about endpoints
   (P(a < X <= b) is the exact version); confusing the CDF with the PDF.
J. RELATED: ECDF (F30.2), quantiles, inverse-transform sampling (F36.5).

COMPARISON TABLE: PMF vs PDF vs CDF
  Aspect            PMF p(x)               PDF f(x)                 CDF F(x)
  Applies to        discrete               continuous               both
  Value means       P(X = x) exactly       density (prob. per unit  P(X <= x)
                                           length), NOT a prob.
  Range             [0, 1]                 [0, inf)                 [0, 1], non-decreasing
  Total             sum = 1                integral = 1             limit at +inf is 1
  Prob. of interval sum over values        integral over interval   F(b) - F(a)
  P(X = x)          p(x)                   0                        jump size at x
  Shape             spikes/bars            smooth curve             staircase or smooth S
  Relation          F(x) = sum of p        f = F'; F = integral of f  accumulates p or f

COMPARISON TABLE: DISCRETE vs CONTINUOUS RANDOM VARIABLES
  Aspect              Discrete                      Continuous
  Possible values     countable (0, 1, 2, ...)      an interval/uncountable
  Described by        PMF (and CDF)                 PDF (and CDF)
  P(X = x)            can be positive               always 0
  P(X <= x) vs P(X < x) differ if x is a value      equal
  Expectation         sum x p(x)                    integral x f(x) dx
  Examples            counts, binomial, Poisson     time, height, normal, exponential
  Visualization       bar chart                     smooth density / histogram

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.5 UNIFORM DISTRIBUTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.5  CONTINUOUS UNIFORM DISTRIBUTION U(a, b)
A. NAME: Uniform distribution on [a, b].
B. FORMULAS
     f(x) = 1/(b - a) for a <= x <= b (0 otherwise)
     F(x) = (x - a)/(b - a) for a <= x <= b
     mean = (a + b)/2;   variance = (b - a)^2 / 12;   median = (a + b)/2.
C. SYMBOLS: a, b the lower and upper limits (a < b).
D. CONCEPT: every subinterval of the same length is equally likely: complete
   ignorance within the range. The height is constant 1/(b - a) so that the
   area (b - a) * 1/(b - a) = 1.
E. CONDITIONS: bounded support; values outside [a, b] impossible.
F. DERIVATION of the variance: E[X^2] = int[a..b] x^2/(b - a) dx = (a^2 + ab +
   b^2)/3; Var = E[X^2] - ((a + b)/2)^2 = (b - a)^2/12.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: waiting time X ~ U(0, 10) minutes. P(2 < X < 5) = 3/10 = 0.3;
      mean 5; variance 100/12 = 8.33; sd 2.887. P(X > 7) = 0.3.
   Moderate: random numbers: U(0, 1) is the basic output of random number
      generators; it is transformed into other distributions (F36.5).
   Discrete uniform on {1, ..., n}: p = 1/n; die: mean 3.5, variance
      (n^2 - 1)/12 = 35/12 = 2.917.
H. INTERPRETATION: p-values are uniformly distributed on (0, 1) when the
   null hypothesis is true (Module 39).
I. MISTAKES: forgetting the density is zero outside [a, b]; using the
   continuous variance formula for the discrete uniform.
J. RELATED: density (F35.3), p-values under H0 (F39.5), random number
   generation (section 41.6).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.6 BERNOULLI DISTRIBUTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.6  BERNOULLI(p)
A. NAME: Bernoulli distribution.
B. FORMULAS
     P(X = 1) = p, P(X = 0) = 1 - p;  PMF: p(x) = p^x (1 - p)^(1 - x), x in {0, 1}
     mean E[X] = p;  variance Var(X) = p (1 - p).
C. SYMBOLS: p the success probability (0 <= p <= 1); X = 1 "success", 0
   "failure".
D. CONCEPT: a single yes/no trial; the building block of the binomial
   distribution. X is an INDICATOR variable: X = 1[event].
E. CONDITIONS: exactly two outcomes with fixed probability p.
F. DERIVATION: E[X] = 1 * p + 0 * (1 - p) = p; E[X^2] = p (since X^2 = X);
   Var = p - p^2 = p (1 - p). The variance is largest at p = 0.5 (0.25) and
   zero at p = 0 or 1 (no uncertainty).
G. EXAMPLES: click on an advert with p = 0.03: mean 0.03; variance 0.03 * 0.97
   = 0.0291; sd = 0.1706. A spam label (1 = spam) with p = 0.2.
H. INTERPRETATION: the mean of Bernoulli outcomes in a sample is the observed
   proportion.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using p as a percentage (0 to 100) instead of a
   number in [0, 1]; forgetting that the variance is largest at p = 0.5.
J. RELATED: binomial (F35.7), logistic regression (F48.1), log-loss
   (F46.5).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: the likelihood of binary labels (log-loss) is a Bernoulli
log-likelihood; logistic regression models p as a function of features.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.7 BINOMIAL DISTRIBUTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.7  BINOMIAL(n, p)
A. NAME: Binomial distribution.
B. FORMULAS
     P(X = k) = C(n, k) p^k (1 - p)^(n - k),    k = 0, 1, ..., n
     mean E[X] = n p;   variance Var(X) = n p (1 - p);   sd = sqrt(n p (1-p)).
     Mode approx floor((n + 1) p).
     CDF: P(X <= k) = sum[j=0..k] C(n, j) p^j (1 - p)^(n - j).
C. SYMBOLS: n number of trials; p success probability per trial; k number of
   successes; C(n, k) the number of ways to choose which trials succeed.
D. CONCEPT: the number of successes in n independent Bernoulli(p) trials;
   X = X_1 + ... + X_n with each X_i ~ Bernoulli(p).
E. CONDITIONS (BINS): Binary outcome per trial; Independent trials;
   Number of trials fixed in advance; Same probability p on every trial.
   Violations: sampling WITHOUT replacement from a small population
   (hypergeometric instead); p changing over time; clustered/dependent
   data; unknown n.
F. DERIVATION of the PMF: one particular sequence with k successes and n - k
   failures has probability p^k (1 - p)^(n - k) by independence; there are
   C(n, k) such sequences (choose the positions of the successes),
   mutually exclusive, so add. Mean and variance: sums of n independent
   Bernoulli: E = n p; Var = n p (1 - p) (variances add for independent
   variables).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: 10 tosses of a fair coin: P(X = 3) = C(10, 3)/2^10 = 120/1024
      = 0.1172. P(X >= 8) = (45 + 10 + 1)/1024 = 56/1024 = 0.0547.
   Moderate: a component fails with probability 0.3; test 5 independent
      components. P(at most 1 fails) = 0.7^5 + 5(0.3)(0.7^4)
      = 0.16807 + 0.36015 = 0.52822. Mean failures 1.5, variance
      5 * 0.3 * 0.7 = 1.05.
   Moderate 2 (normal approximation): n = 100, p = 0.5: X approx
      N(50, 5^2). P(X >= 60) exact = 0.0284. Approximation with continuity
      correction: P(X >= 59.5): z = (59.5 - 50)/5 = 1.9: 1 - Phi(1.9) = 0.0287.
      Rule of thumb: need n p >= 10 and n (1 - p) >= 10 (some use 5).
   Data science: a classifier with true accuracy 0.8 on 200 test items:
      the number correct ~ Binomial(200, 0.8): mean 160, sd 5.66 (so
      accuracy has standard error sqrt(0.8 * 0.2/200) = 0.0283).
H. INTERPRETATION: sd = sqrt(n p (1 - p)) grows like sqrt(n) while the mean grows
   like n, so the PROPORTION X/n has sd sqrt(p (1 - p)/n): precision
   improves like 1/sqrt(n) (Module 37).
I. MISTAKES: using the binomial when trials are dependent; forgetting the
   binomial coefficient; confusing P(X = k) with P(X <= k); using the
   normal approximation for small n p or extreme p.
J. RELATED: Bernoulli (n = 1); hypergeometric (without replacement);
   Poisson approximation for large n, small p [SUPPLEMENTARY]: Poisson(lambda)
   has P(X = k) = e^(-lambda) lambda^k / k!, mean = variance = lambda
   (events in a fixed interval, e.g. P(2 events | lambda = 3) =
   e^(-3) * 9/2 = 0.224).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.8 NORMAL (GAUSSIAN) DISTRIBUTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.8  NORMAL DISTRIBUTION N(mu, sigma^2)
A. NAME: Normal (Gaussian) distribution.
B. FORMULAS
     f(x) = 1/(sigma sqrt(2 pi)) * exp( -(x - mu)^2 / (2 sigma^2) ),  x in R
     mean = median = mode = mu;   variance = sigma^2.
     Standardization:  Z = (X - mu)/sigma ~ N(0, 1)  (standard normal)
     Standard normal CDF: Phi(z) = P(Z <= z)  (no closed form; tables or
        software).
     P(a <= X <= b) = Phi( (b - mu)/sigma ) - Phi( (a - mu)/sigma ).
     Symmetry: Phi(-z) = 1 - Phi(z).
     Empirical rule: P(|X - mu| <= sigma) = 0.6827; within 2 sigma = 0.9545;
        within 3 sigma = 0.9973.
     Key quantiles: z = 1.645 (two-sided 90%, one-sided 95%), 1.960 (95%
        two-sided), 2.576 (99% two-sided).
C. SYMBOLS: mu the center (location); sigma > 0 the scale (standard
   deviation); sigma^2 the variance; exp(.) = e^(.); z the standardized
   value ("z-score"): how many standard deviations x is from the mean.
   NOTATION WARNING: N(mu, sigma^2) lists the VARIANCE (convention here); in
   software, scipy.stats.norm(loc = mu, scale = sigma) uses the SD.
D. CONCEPT: the bell curve. Symmetric about mu; inflection points at mu +/- sigma;
   tails decay extremely fast (squared term in the exponent). The
   term (x - mu)^2/(2 sigma^2) measures squared distance in SD units.
   WHY IT IS EVERYWHERE: the Central Limit Theorem (Module 37): sums/averages
   of many small independent effects are approximately normal. Also: the
   normal maximizes entropy for a given mean and variance; linear
   combinations of independent normals are normal.
E. CONDITIONS: a MODEL; real data are only approximately normal. Check with
   histograms, Q-Q plots, tests. Heavy-tailed data (financial returns)
   violate it. Sum rule: if X ~ N(mu1, s1^2) and Y ~ N(mu2, s2^2) are
   independent then X + Y ~ N(mu1 + mu2, s1^2 + s2^2) (VARIANCES add, not
   SDs); aX + b ~ N(a mu + b, a^2 sigma^2).
F. DERIVATION (normalization): int e^(-z^2/2) dz = sqrt(2 pi) (polar-coordinate
   trick, F13.2), which is why the constant 1/sqrt(2 pi) appears.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: IQ ~ N(100, 15^2). P(X > 130): z = (130 - 100)/15 = 2;
      1 - Phi(2) = 0.0228 (about 2.3%).
      P(85 < X < 115) = P(-1 < Z < 1) = 0.6827.
   Moderate 1: the 90th percentile of IQ: x = mu + z_0.90 sigma =
      100 + 1.2816 * 15 = 119.2.
   Moderate 2: exam scores ~ N(70, 10^2). P(X < 55): z = -1.5, Phi(-1.5) =
      0.0668. P(60 < X < 85) = Phi(1.5) - Phi(-1) = 0.9332 - 0.1587 = 0.7745.
   Moderate 3 (sum): two independent measurement errors N(0, 3^2) and
      N(0, 4^2): the total error ~ N(0, 25): sd 5 (not 7).
H. INTERPRETATION: z = 2 means two standard deviations above the mean (top 2.3%).
   Z-scores let you compare values on different scales.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using sigma vs sigma^2 inconsistently (software scale =
   SD); applying the empirical rule to non-normal data; treating the
   density value as a probability; adding SDs instead of variances;
   forgetting to standardize before using tables; assuming "large
   sample" makes the DATA normal (only the MEAN becomes approximately
   normal).
J. RELATED: t-distribution (Module 39), multivariate normal (mean vector and
   covariance matrix Sigma: density proportional to
   exp(-(1/2)(x - mu)^T Sigma^(-1) (x - mu))), log-normal (log X is normal),
   Gaussian noise in regression (Module 40), standardization of features.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
35.9 EXPONENTIAL DISTRIBUTION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F35.9  EXPONENTIAL(lambda)
A. NAME: Exponential distribution.
B. FORMULAS
     f(x) = lambda e^(-lambda x),  x >= 0 (0 for x < 0)
     F(x) = 1 - e^(-lambda x);   survival P(X > x) = e^(-lambda x)
     mean = 1/lambda;  variance = 1/lambda^2;  sd = 1/lambda;
     median = ln(2)/lambda.
     MEMORYLESS property: P(X > s + t | X > s) = P(X > t).
C. SYMBOLS: lambda > 0 the RATE (events per unit time); 1/lambda the mean
   waiting time. NOTATION WARNING: some books/software use the scale
   beta = 1/lambda (scipy.stats.expon(scale = 1/lambda)).
D. CONCEPT: the waiting time until the next event when events occur
   independently at a constant average rate (a Poisson process). Density
   is highest at 0 and decays: short waits are the most likely, long
   waits rare.
E. CONDITIONS: constant rate, independent events; support x >= 0. Real
   lifetimes often have increasing/decreasing hazard (use Weibull
   etc.). The exponential is the only continuous distribution with the
   memoryless property.
F. DERIVATION: P(X > x) = P(no event in [0, x]); for a Poisson process with
   rate lambda that is e^(-lambda x). Hence F(x) = 1 - e^(-lambda x) and
   f = F'. Memoryless: P(X > s + t)/P(X > s) = e^(-lambda(s+t))/e^(-lambda s)
   = e^(-lambda t). Mean by integration by parts (F4.6): int x lambda
   e^(-lambda x) dx = 1/lambda.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: customers arrive at rate lambda = 0.5 per minute (mean wait 2
      minutes). P(wait > 3 min) = e^(-1.5) = 0.2231.
   Moderate: P(1 < X < 4) = F(4) - F(1) = e^(-0.5) - e^(-2) = 0.6065 - 0.1353
      = 0.4712. Median wait = ln 2/0.5 = 1.386 minutes (less than the mean
      2: right-skewed).
   Moderate 2 (memoryless): given you have already waited 5 minutes, the
      probability of waiting at least 3 more is still e^(-1.5) = 0.2231.
   Variance: 1/lambda^2 = 4; sd = 2 minutes = mean (a signature of the
      exponential: coefficient of variation 1).
H. INTERPRETATION: "the past does not matter" for the remaining wait.
I. MISTAKES: mixing rate and scale; using it for lifetimes with wear-out
   (not memoryless); forgetting x >= 0.
J. RELATED: Poisson counts (number of events in time t ~ Poisson(lambda t));
   geometric distribution (discrete analog: trials until first
   success, E = 1/p); gamma distribution (sum of exponentials).

PYTHON (scipy.stats) QUICK REFERENCE
   from scipy import stats
   stats.norm(loc=100, scale=15).sf(130)         # P(X > 130) = 0.0228
   stats.norm.ppf(0.975)                          # 1.96
   stats.binom(n=10, p=0.5).pmf(3)               # 0.1172
   stats.binom(10, 0.5).cdf(7)                   # P(X <= 7)
   stats.expon(scale=1/0.5).cdf(4)               # 1 - e^-2
   stats.uniform(loc=0, scale=10).cdf(5)         # 0.5  (loc=a, scale=b-a)
   Methods: pdf/pmf, cdf, sf (survival 1 - cdf), ppf (inverse CDF), rvs
   (random samples), mean(), var(). Note: uniform uses (loc, scale = b - a).


========================================================================
MODULE 36: EXPECTATION AND VARIANCE
========================================================================

------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.1 EXPECTED VALUE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F36.1  EXPECTED VALUE (MEAN) OF A RANDOM VARIABLE
A. NAME: Expectation / expected value / population mean.
B. FORMULAS
     Discrete:    E[X] = sum[x] x p(x)
     Continuous:  E[X] = int[-inf..inf] x f(x) dx
     Law of the unconscious statistician (LOTUS), for a function g:
        E[g(X)] = sum g(x) p(x)   or   int g(x) f(x) dx
     (no need to find the distribution of g(X)).
C. SYMBOLS: E[.] expectation operator; mu = E[X] often used; p(x) PMF; f(x)
   PDF.
D. CONCEPT: the probability-weighted average of the possible values; the
   long-run average of many independent repetitions (law of large
   numbers); the center of mass of the distribution (balance point).
   It need not be a possible value (a die has E = 3.5).
E. CONDITIONS: the sum/integral must converge absolutely. Counterexample:
   the Cauchy distribution (f(x) = 1/(pi(1 + x^2))) has NO mean (F4.8).
   In general E[g(X)] != g(E[X]); for CONVEX g Jensen's inequality gives
   E[g(X)] >= g(E[X]).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: fair die: E[X] = (1 + 2 + ... + 6)/6 = 3.5.
      Lottery: pays 100 with probability 0.01, otherwise 0; ticket costs 2:
      E[prize] = 1; expected net gain = 1 - 2 = -1.
   Moderate: continuous f(x) = 2x on [0, 1]: E[X] = int[0..1] x * 2x dx
      = 2/3. E[X^2] = int 2x^3 dx = 1/2 (LOTUS).
   Moderate 2 (Jensen): X ~ U(0, 1): E[X^2] = 1/3 but (E[X])^2 = 1/4:
      E[g(X)] != g(E[X]).
   Exponential: E[X] = 1/lambda (F35.9); Bernoulli E = p; binomial E = n p.
H. INTERPRETATION: expected value is a theoretical average, not what you
   expect on a single trial; decisions under uncertainty compare expected
   values (plus risk).
I. MISTAKES: E[1/X] = 1/E[X] (WRONG); E[X^2] = (E[X])^2 (WRONG; the gap is the
   variance); E[XY] = E[X] E[Y] without independence (WRONG);
   computing E of a continuous variable by summing.
J. RELATED: sample mean (F29.1), linearity (F36.2), variance (F36.3).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.2 LINEARITY OF EXPECTATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F36.2  LINEARITY OF EXPECTATION
A. NAME: Linearity of expectation.
B. FORMULAS
     E[a X + b] = a E[X] + b
     E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y]            ALWAYS (no independence needed)
     E[a_1 X_1 + ... + a_n X_n] = a_1 E[X_1] + ... + a_n E[X_n]
     If X and Y are independent: E[X Y] = E[X] E[Y]. More generally,
        E[X Y] = E[X] E[Y] + Cov(X, Y), so the product rule holds exactly
        when Cov(X, Y) = 0 (X and Y uncorrelated), which is weaker than
        independence.
C. SYMBOLS: a, b constants; X, Y any random variables with finite means.
D. CONCEPT: the average of a sum is the sum of averages, regardless of how
   the variables depend on each other. This makes hard problems easy:
   break a complicated variable into a sum of simple indicator variables.
E. CONDITIONS: holds ALWAYS for sums (the strength of the theorem); the
   product rule needs independence (or zero covariance).
F. DERIVATION: E[X + Y] = sum_x sum_y (x + y) p(x, y) = sum x p_X(x) + sum y p_Y(y),
   using only the definition of marginals.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: sum of two dice: E = 3.5 + 3.5 = 7.
   Moderate (indicator trick): binomial mean. X = X_1 + ... + X_n with E[X_i]
      = p: E[X] = n p (no PMF sums needed).
   Moderate 2 (dependent variables): n people put their hats in a box and
      each takes one at random. Let X_i = 1 if person i gets their own
      hat; E[X_i] = 1/n. Expected number of matches = n * (1/n) = 1 for
      EVERY n, even though the X_i are dependent.
   Moderate 3 (affine): temperature X in C with mean 20; in F: 1.8 * 20 + 32
      = 68.
H. INTERPRETATION: the mean of a sample mean is the population mean:
   E[X_bar] = mu (unbiasedness, Module 37).
I. MISTAKES: thinking independence is needed for the sum rule; extending the
   rule to variances (it needs independence/covariance); applying to
   non-linear functions.
J. RELATED: variance of sums (F36.3), binomial mean (F35.7),
   unbiasedness of the sample mean (F37.2).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.3 VARIANCE AND STANDARD DEVIATION OF RANDOM VARIABLES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F36.3  VARIANCE
A. NAME: Variance and standard deviation of a random variable.
B. FORMULAS
     Var(X) = E[ (X - mu)^2 ] = E[X^2] - (E[X])^2
     sd(X) = sigma = sqrt(Var(X))
     Var(a X + b) = a^2 Var(X);   sd(a X + b) = |a| sd(X)
     Var(X + Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y) + 2 Cov(X, Y)
     If X_1..X_n are INDEPENDENT (or uncorrelated):
        Var(sum X_i) = sum Var(X_i);    Var(a X + b Y) = a^2 Var X + b^2 Var Y
     Mean of n iid values with variance sigma^2: Var(X_bar) = sigma^2/n.
C. SYMBOLS: mu = E[X]; Cov covariance; X_bar the sample mean.
D. CONCEPT: the expected squared distance from the mean: the average
   amount of "wobble". It is the population analog of the sample
   variance of Module 31; the sample variance s^2 ESTIMATES it.
E. CONDITIONS: needs a finite second moment. Variances add only when
   covariances vanish (otherwise include 2 Cov). Standard deviations do
   NOT add.
F. DERIVATION of the shortcut: E[(X - mu)^2] = E[X^2 - 2 mu X + mu^2] =
   E[X^2] - 2 mu^2 + mu^2 = E[X^2] - mu^2 (using linearity).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: fair die: E[X^2] = (1 + 4 + 9 + 16 + 25 + 36)/6 = 91/6 = 15.167;
      Var = 91/6 - 3.5^2 = 15.167 - 12.25 = 2.917 = 35/12.
   Moderate: sum of two dice (independent): Var = 35/12 + 35/12 = 35/6 = 5.833;
      sd = 2.415. (Check by enumeration: variance of the 36 sums is 5.833.)
   Moderate 2: Bernoulli: p - p^2; binomial n p (1 - p) (sum of n independent
      Bernoulli); X ~ U(a, b): (b - a)^2/12; exponential 1/lambda^2.
   Moderate 3: Y = 2X + 3 with Var(X) = 4: Var(Y) = 4 * 4 = 16; sd(Y) = 4 (the
      shift 3 does not matter).
   Moderate 4 (negative association): Var(X - Y) = Var X + Var Y - 2 Cov(X, Y):
      for portfolios, covariance (diversification) reduces risk.
H. INTERPRETATION: the sd is in the original units; Chebyshev: P(|X - mu| >=
   k sigma) <= 1/k^2 for any distribution (k = 2: at most 25%).
I. MISTAKES: Var(a X) = a Var(X) (WRONG: a^2); adding standard deviations;
   Var(X - Y) = Var X - Var Y (WRONG: variances ADD for independent
   differences); applying Var(sum) = sum Var to dependent variables;
   using population and sample formulas interchangeably (Module 31).
J. RELATED: covariance (Module 32); standard error sigma/sqrt(n) (Module
   37); bias-variance decomposition (Module 46).

SUMMARY TABLE: MEANS AND VARIANCES OF STANDARD DISTRIBUTIONS
  Distribution       Parameters   Mean        Variance
