  Bernoulli          p            p           p(1 - p)
  Binomial           n, p         n p         n p (1 - p)
  Poisson [supp.]    lambda       lambda      lambda
  Geometric [supp.]  p            1/p         (1 - p)/p^2
  Uniform (cont.)    a, b         (a + b)/2   (b - a)^2/12
  Normal             mu, sigma^2  mu          sigma^2
  Exponential        lambda       1/lambda    1/lambda^2

------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.4 MOMENTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F36.4  MOMENTS AND THE MOMENT GENERATING FUNCTION
A. NAME: Raw moments, central moments, standardized moments, MGF.
B. FORMULAS
     k-th RAW moment:       m_k = E[X^k]
     k-th CENTRAL moment:   mu_k = E[(X - mu)^k]
     Mean = m_1; variance = mu_2.
     SKEWNESS: gamma_1 = mu_3/sigma^3 (asymmetry);
     KURTOSIS: mu_4/sigma^4 (tail heaviness; normal = 3; EXCESS kurtosis =
        mu_4/sigma^4 - 3).
     MGF: M(t) = E[e^(tX)]; the k-th moment is the k-th derivative at 0:
        E[X^k] = M^(k)(0).
C. SYMBOLS: t a real number near 0; M^(k)(0) the k-th derivative of M at t = 0.
D. CONCEPT: moments are numbers that describe the shape of a distribution:
   location (1st), spread (2nd), asymmetry (3rd), tail weight (4th). The
   method of moments (Module 38) estimates parameters by matching sample
   moments to theoretical ones. Under mild conditions, the sequence of
   moments (or the MGF, if it exists) identifies the distribution.
E. CONDITIONS: higher moments may not exist for heavy-tailed distributions
   (Cauchy: none; Student t with 3 degrees of freedom: no 4th moment).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: Bernoulli(p): M(t) = (1 - p) + p e^t; M'(0) = p = E[X]; M''(0) = p
      = E[X^2]; Var = p - p^2.
   Moderate: Exponential(lambda): M(t) = lambda/(lambda - t) for t < lambda.
      M'(t) = lambda/(lambda - t)^2 -> M'(0) = 1/lambda;
      M''(t) = 2 lambda/(lambda - t)^3 -> E[X^2] = 2/lambda^2;
      Var = 2/lambda^2 - 1/lambda^2 = 1/lambda^2. OK.
      Skewness of the exponential = 2 (right-skewed); normal skewness 0.
   Moderate 2: raw to central: mu_3 = E[X^3] - 3 mu E[X^2] + 2 mu^3.
H. INTERPRETATION: skewness > 0 and large kurtosis flag asymmetric,
   outlier-prone data (Module 30 sample versions). Sample versions of
   moments are used in the method of moments.
I. MISTAKES: mixing raw and central moments; forgetting to divide by
   sigma^k for standardized moments; assuming all moments exist.
J. RELATED: skewness and kurtosis of data (F30.3), method of moments
   (F38.1).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.5 TRANSFORMATIONS OF RANDOM VARIABLES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F36.5  LINEAR AND NONLINEAR TRANSFORMATIONS
A. NAME: Distribution of Y = g(X).
B. FORMULAS
     LINEAR: Y = a X + b:  E[Y] = a E[X] + b;  Var(Y) = a^2 Var(X);
        sd(Y) = |a| sd(X). If X ~ N(mu, sigma^2) then Y ~ N(a mu + b, a^2 sigma^2).
        Standardizing: Z = (X - mu)/sigma has mean 0 and variance 1.
     CDF METHOD (any g): F_Y(y) = P(g(X) <= y) = P(X in {x : g(x) <= y}); then
        differentiate to get f_Y.
     MONOTONE g (strictly increasing or decreasing, differentiable) with
        inverse x = g^(-1)(y):
          f_Y(y) = f_X( g^(-1)(y) ) * | d g^(-1)(y)/dy |.
        (A one-dimensional change of variables; the factor is the
        Jacobian, F13.3.)
     INVERSE-TRANSFORM SAMPLING: if U ~ U(0, 1) and F is a CDF, then
        X = F^(-1)(U) has CDF F.
C. SYMBOLS: g a function; g^(-1) its inverse; |dx/dy| the absolute
   derivative of the inverse (Jacobian factor).
D. CONCEPT: transforming a random variable changes its distribution; for
   linear maps only the location and scale change, for nonlinear maps
   the SHAPE changes.
E. CONDITIONS: the density formula needs g monotone on the support (else
   split into monotone pieces and add contributions). Expectation of a
   nonlinear transform: use LOTUS, not g(E[X]).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (linear): temperature X ~ N(20, 4^2) C. In F: Y = 1.8 X + 32 ~
      N(68, 7.2^2) (sd = 1.8 * 4 = 7.2).
   Moderate 1 (inverse transform): U ~ U(0, 1) and Y = -ln(U)/lambda. Then
      F_Y(y) = P(-ln U/lambda <= y) = P(U >= e^(-lambda y)) = 1 - e^(-lambda y):
      Y ~ Exponential(lambda). Used to generate exponential random numbers.
   Moderate 2 (squaring): U ~ U(0, 1), Y = U^2: F_Y(y) = P(U <= sqrt(y))
      = sqrt(y) for 0 < y < 1; f_Y(y) = 1/(2 sqrt(y)) (unbounded near 0).
      E[Y] = 1/3 (LOTUS), not (1/2)^2 = 1/4.
   Moderate 3 (log-normal): if X ~ N(mu, sigma^2), Y = e^X is log-normal with
      mean e^(mu + sigma^2/2) (greater than e^mu: Jensen).
H. INTERPRETATION: log transforms turn multiplicative effects into additive
   ones and make right-skewed data more symmetric; back-transforming a
   mean of logs gives the geometric mean, not the arithmetic mean.
I. MISTAKES: E[g(X)] = g(E[X]); forgetting the Jacobian factor; applying the
   monotone formula to non-monotone g; forgetting that standardization
   changes the scale of the units.
J. RELATED: change of variables (Module 13); z-scores (Module 35);
   feature scaling and Box-Cox/log transforms; the delta method:
   Var(g(X)) approx (g'(mu))^2 sigma^2 for small sigma [SUPPLEMENTARY].

------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.6 PROBABILITY OF INTERVALS; ZERO PROBABILITY AT POINTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F36.6  INTERVAL PROBABILITIES
B. Continuous X with CDF F and PDF f:
     P(a <= X <= b) = P(a < X < b) = P(a < X <= b) = F(b) - F(a)
                    = int[a..b] f(x) dx.
   Discrete X: endpoints MATTER: P(a < X <= b) = F(b) - F(a);
     P(a <= X <= b) = F(b) - F(a-) = F(b) - F(a) + p(a).
   One-sided: P(X <= b) = F(b); P(X > a) = 1 - F(a).
C. SYMBOLS: F the CDF; F(a-) the left limit of F at a; p(a) the point
   probability.
D. CONCEPT: the probability of an interval is the probability
   accumulated between its endpoints: a difference of CDF values.
E. CONDITIONS: for continuous X the endpoints are irrelevant; for
   discrete X they matter.
G. EXAMPLES: Exponential(lambda = 0.5): P(1 < X < 4) = 0.4712 (whether or not
   the endpoints are included).
   Binomial(10, 0.5): P(3 <= X <= 5) = p(3) + p(4) + p(5)
   = (120 + 210 + 252)/1024 = 582/1024 = 0.5684; P(3 < X <= 5) = 462/1024.
H. INTERPRETATION: lets us read 'between' probabilities directly from a
   CDF table or function.
I. MISTAKE: ignoring endpoint inclusion for discrete variables ("at least 3"
   vs "more than 3").

WHY CONTINUOUS RANDOM VARIABLES HAVE ZERO PROBABILITY AT INDIVIDUAL POINTS
STATEMENT: if X is continuous with density f, then P(X = x) = 0 for every
single value x.
EXPLANATION
   1. By the definition of the PDF, P(X = x) = P(x <= X <= x) = int[x..x] f(t) dt
      = 0: an integral over an interval of zero width has zero area.
   2. Limit argument: for any h > 0, P(X = x) <= P(x - h <= X <= x + h)
      = int[x-h..x+h] f(t) dt <= 2 h max f. Let h -> 0: the bound -> 0.
   3. Counting/total-mass argument: there are uncountably many possible
      values and total probability is 1. If each point had positive
      probability, then for any c > 0 only finitely many (at most 1/c)
      points can have probability at least c, so at most countably many
      points could have positive probability. A continuous variable
      spreads its probability over a continuum, so each point gets 0.
   4. Measurement view: "exactly 1.80000... m tall" with infinite precision
      is an event no real person satisfies; real statements concern
      INTERVALS (to the nearest cm).
J. RELATED: CDF (F35.4), density (F35.3), p-values (F39.5).
IMPORTANT CONSEQUENCES AND CLARIFICATIONS
   - Probability ZERO does NOT mean IMPOSSIBLE. For X ~ U(0, 1) the value 0.5 is
     possible, yet P(X = 0.5) = 0 ("almost surely" X != 0.5).
     Impossible events have probability 0, but not conversely in the
     continuous setting.
   - The density f(x) is NOT P(X = x). f(x) dx approx P(x <= X <= x + dx): for a
     tiny window of width h, probability approx f(x) h.
     Example: U(0, 1): P(0.5 <= X <= 0.5 + h) = h.
   - Hence P(X <= x) = P(X < x), and for continuous variables the
     inclusion of endpoints is irrelevant.
   - Likelihood of continuous data uses DENSITIES (not probabilities) as
     the quantity to maximize (Module 38).
   - In practice, rounding makes real measurements discrete: probabilities
     of observed values are positive because the value represents an
     interval.
COMMON MISTAKES: reading f(x) as "the probability of x"; concluding that
"P = 0" events never happen; summing densities instead of integrating;
asking for P(X = x) of a continuous variable instead of an interval.

DATA SCIENCE LINK (Modules 35-36): loss functions derive from distributions
(squared error from the normal, cross-entropy from Bernoulli/categorical);
probability calibration; predicted distributions; expectation underlies
risk (expected loss), bias-variance, and policy value in reinforcement
learning; sampling from distributions underlies Monte Carlo simulation,
bootstrapping and generative models.


========================================================================
C3. STATISTICAL INFERENCE AND LINEAR MODELS
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 37: SAMPLING AND ESTIMATION
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
We rarely observe everyone. We observe a SAMPLE and use it to learn
about the whole POPULATION. Statistical inference is the science of
doing this honestly: quantifying how far the sample result may be from
the truth, and how that error shrinks as the sample grows.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.1 POPULATION vs SAMPLE; PARAMETERS vs STATISTICS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   POPULATION: the complete collection of units we want to learn about
      (all customers, all future transactions, all possible measurements).
      It can be a hypothetical infinite process.
   SAMPLE: the subset actually observed, size n.
   PARAMETER: a fixed (usually unknown) numerical summary of the
      population: mu, sigma^2, proportion p, regression coefficient beta.
   STATISTIC: a numerical summary computed from the sample: x_bar, s^2, p_hat.
      A statistic is a RANDOM VARIABLE: a different sample would give a
      different value.
   ESTIMATOR: a rule/function of the sample used to estimate a parameter;
      written with a hat: theta_hat = g(X_1, ..., X_n). It is random.
   ESTIMATE: the specific number obtained from one observed sample.
      Example: x_bar = (1/n) sum X_i is the ESTIMATOR of mu; x_bar = 5.2
      from your data is the ESTIMATE.
  Quantity                Population (parameter)   Sample (statistic)
  Mean                    mu                       x_bar
  Variance                sigma^2                  s^2
  Standard deviation      sigma                    s
  Proportion              p                        p_hat
  Correlation             rho                      r
  Size                    N                        n
  Fixed or random?        fixed, unknown           random (varies by sample)
SAMPLING METHODS
   SIMPLE RANDOM SAMPLE (SRS): every subset of size n is equally likely.
   STRATIFIED: split the population into homogeneous strata, sample within
      each (reduces variance, guarantees group coverage).
   CLUSTER: sample whole groups (schools, villages): cheaper, usually
      higher variance.
   SYSTEMATIC: every k-th unit.
   CONVENIENCE/VOLUNTARY-RESPONSE samples are NOT random and are typically
      biased.
   IID ASSUMPTION: observations Independent and Identically Distributed:
      the standard model behind most formulas in this module.
SAMPLING ERROR vs BIAS
   SAMPLING ERROR: random variation from using a sample; shrinks with n.
   SAMPLING BIAS (selection bias, non-response, survivorship): systematic
      error from HOW the sample was obtained; does NOT shrink with n.
      Famous example: a huge biased poll can be worse than a small random
      one. A large n makes a biased estimate precisely wrong.
DATA SCIENCE LINK: the training data is a sample from the population of
future cases; distribution shift between training and deployment is
sampling bias. Train/test splits assume the same population.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.2 SAMPLING DISTRIBUTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F37.1  SAMPLING DISTRIBUTION OF A STATISTIC
A. NAME: Sampling distribution.
B. DEFINITION: the probability distribution of a statistic (e.g. x_bar) over
   all possible samples of size n from the population.
C. SYMBOLS: n the sample size; x_bar the statistic; the sampling
   distribution lives over all possible samples of size n.
D. CONCEPT: imagine repeating the sampling process many times, computing the
   statistic each time; the histogram of those values is the sampling
   distribution. It describes the REPEATED-SAMPLING behavior of the
   statistic, not the variability of individual data points. All
   frequentist inference (standard errors, confidence intervals, p-values)
   relies on it.
E. CONDITIONS: it depends on the population, the sampling scheme and n.
G. WORKED EXAMPLE (exact enumeration): population {2, 4, 6, 8}
   (N = 4: mu = 5, sigma^2 = (9 + 1 + 1 + 9)/4 = 5). Draw samples of size
   n = 2 WITH replacement: 16 equally likely ordered pairs.
     Sample means: 2 (once), 3 (twice), 4 (three times), 5 (four times),
     6 (three), 7 (twice), 8 (once)  -> probabilities /16.
     E[x_bar] = (2*1 + 3*2 + 4*3 + 5*4 + 6*3 + 7*2 + 8*1)/16 = 80/16 = 5 = mu.
     Var(x_bar) = (9*1 + 4*2 + 1*3 + 0 + 1*3 + 4*2 + 9*1)/16 = 40/16 = 2.5
     = sigma^2/n = 5/2. OK.
   So the sampling distribution is centered at mu with spread sigma/sqrt(n);
   it is triangular here, more bell-like than the (uniform) population.
H. INTERPRETATION: it is the reference distribution for standard errors,
   confidence intervals and tests.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: confusing the sampling distribution of x_bar with
   the distribution of the data; thinking only one sample exists.
J. RELATED: CLT (F37.4), standard error (F37.8), bootstrap (section
   37.6).
F37.2  MEAN AND VARIANCE OF THE SAMPLE MEAN
B. If X_1..X_n are iid with mean mu and variance sigma^2:
        E[x_bar] = mu;   Var(x_bar) = sigma^2/n;   sd(x_bar) = sigma/sqrt(n).
C. SYMBOLS: mu, sigma^2 the population mean and variance; n the sample
   size; X_i the observations.
D. CONCEPT: averaging cancels random errors: the mean stays centered at
   mu while its spread shrinks like 1/sqrt(n).
F. DERIVATION: E[x_bar] = (1/n) sum E[X_i] = mu (linearity, F36.2);
   Var(x_bar) = (1/n^2) sum Var(X_i) = n sigma^2/n^2 (independence).
E. CONDITIONS: independence (clustered or serially correlated data have a
   larger variance of x_bar). If sampling WITHOUT replacement from a finite
   population: Var(x_bar) = (sigma^2/n) (N - n)/(N - 1), the FINITE
   POPULATION CORRECTION (fpc); negligible when n << N.
G. EXAMPLE: sigma = 15, n = 36: sd(x_bar) = 15/6 = 2.5; n = 144: 1.25.
   For the population {2, 4, 6, 8} with n = 2: Var(x_bar) = 5/2 = 2.5
   (F37.1).
H. INTERPRETATION: quadrupling the sample size halves the standard
   error.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: forgetting independence; confusing sigma/sqrt(n)
   with sigma; using it for dependent data.
J. RELATED: standard error (F37.8), CLT (F37.4), variance rules (F36.2,
   F36.3).

F37.3  LAW OF LARGE NUMBERS (LLN)
B. If X_i are iid with finite mean mu then x_bar_n -> mu in probability as
   n -> infinity: for every epsilon > 0, P(|x_bar_n - mu| > epsilon) -> 0.
C. SYMBOLS: x_bar_n the mean of n observations; mu the true mean;
   epsilon > 0 a tolerance.
D. CONCEPT: sample averages settle down to the true mean as n grows
   (justifies simulation and the frequency interpretation of
   probability). It says nothing about short runs ("law of averages"
   fallacy: past heads do not "owe" tails).
E. PROOF IDEA (Chebyshev): P(|x_bar - mu| > eps) <= Var(x_bar)/eps^2 =
   sigma^2/(n eps^2) -> 0.
G. EXAMPLE: the proportion of heads in n tosses of a fair coin: for n =
   10 it typically lies between 0.3 and 0.7; for n = 10,000 it is within
   about +/-0.01 of 0.5 (sd = 0.005).
H. INTERPRETATION: justifies Monte Carlo estimates and the frequency
   meaning of probability.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: the gambler's fallacy (past deviations are not
   'corrected'); applying it to distributions without a finite mean
   (Cauchy).
J. RELATED: consistency (F37.7), CLT (F37.4), simulation (section 41.6).

F37.4  CENTRAL LIMIT THEOREM (CLT)
A. NAME: Central limit theorem.
B. STATEMENT: let X_1..X_n be iid with mean mu and finite variance sigma^2.
   Then as n grows
        Z_n = (x_bar - mu) / (sigma/sqrt(n))  ->  N(0, 1) in distribution,
   i.e. for large n, x_bar is approximately N(mu, sigma^2/n); the sum
   S_n = sum X_i is approximately N(n mu, n sigma^2).
C. SYMBOLS: Z_n the standardized sample mean; "->" convergence in distribution.
D. CONCEPT: averaging many independent contributions washes out the
   peculiarities of the underlying distribution, leaving a bell shape
   whatever the original shape (as long as the variance is finite).
E. CONDITIONS & CAUTIONS
   - Independence and finite variance (the Cauchy distribution has no
     variance: its sample mean is as variable as one observation).
   - The speed of convergence depends on the population: symmetric
     distributions converge quickly; heavily skewed ones need larger n.
     Rule of thumb n >= 30 (not a law).
   - The CLT is about the distribution of the MEAN (or sum), NOT about the
     data becoming normal.
   - Proportions: p_hat approx N(p, p(1 - p)/n) when n p >= 10 and
     n(1 - p) >= 10 (the binomial CLT).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: roll a fair die n = 50 times. mu = 3.5, sigma^2 = 35/12 = 2.917.
      x_bar approx N(3.5, 2.917/50 = 0.0583): sd = 0.2415.
      P(x_bar > 3.8) = P(Z > 0.3/0.2415 = 1.242) = 0.107.
   Moderate (skewed population): waiting times Exponential with mean 2
      (sigma = 2), n = 40: x_bar approx N(2, 4/40 = 0.1) (sd 0.316).
      P(x_bar > 2.5) approx P(Z > 1.581) = 0.057. The exact value
      (the sum is Gamma(40)) is 0.065: the approximation is decent but
      not perfect at n = 40 for skewed data.
H. INTERPRETATION: why the normal distribution and the z/t procedures are
   used so widely for means.
I. MISTAKES: "with n > 30 the data are normal"; applying it to
   heavy-tailed data without checking; ignoring dependence.
J. RELATED: sampling distribution (F37.1), normal distribution (F35.8),
   confidence intervals (F39.7).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.3 ESTIMATORS, BIAS AND UNBIASEDNESS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F37.5  BIAS AND UNBIASED ESTIMATORS
A. NAME: Bias of an estimator.
B. FORMULAS
     Bias(theta_hat) = E[theta_hat] - theta
     UNBIASED: E[theta_hat] = theta for every possible value of theta.
C. SYMBOLS: theta the true parameter; theta_hat the estimator (a random
   variable); the expectation is over repeated samples (the sampling
   distribution).
D. CONCEPT: on average over many samples an unbiased estimator hits the
   target; a biased one is systematically too high or low. Bias is a
   property of the PROCEDURE, not of a single estimate.
E. CONDITIONS: unbiasedness refers to the sampling distribution, with the
   model assumptions (e.g. iid). It is NOT sufficient for a good
   estimator (see variance) nor necessary (see MSE).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (unbiased): x_bar for mu: E[x_bar] = mu. Sample variance s^2 (divisor
      n - 1) is unbiased for sigma^2 (F31.4). The sample proportion p_hat is
      unbiased for p.
   Moderate 1 (biased): divide-by-n variance has E = (n - 1)/n sigma^2: bias =
      -sigma^2/n (too small). Sample sd s is slightly biased low for
      sigma (E[s] < sigma, Jensen).
   Moderate 2: X_i ~ U(0, theta); the sample maximum M estimates theta. E[M]
      = n theta/(n + 1): biased low (always below theta). For n = 3,
      E[M] = 0.75 theta (simulation: 0.7504 for theta = 1). The corrected
      estimator ((n + 1)/n) M is unbiased.
   Moderate 3: estimating mu with 0.9 x_bar: bias = -0.1 mu (biased).
H. INTERPRETATION: unbiasedness is a repeated-sampling guarantee; you only
   have one sample.
I. MISTAKES: calling an estimate (a number) biased (bias belongs to the
   estimator); assuming unbiased means accurate; thinking all
   "natural" estimators are unbiased; forgetting that s is not unbiased
   even though s^2 is.
J. RELATED: variance and MSE (F37.6), consistency (F37.7), the n - 1
   divisor (F31.4).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.4 VARIANCE OF ESTIMATORS; MEAN SQUARED ERROR
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F37.6  VARIANCE OF AN ESTIMATOR, EFFICIENCY AND MSE
A. NAME: Estimator variance; mean squared error (MSE) and the
   bias-variance decomposition.
B. FORMULAS
     Var(theta_hat) = E[ (theta_hat - E[theta_hat])^2 ]
     MSE(theta_hat) = E[ (theta_hat - theta)^2 ] = Var(theta_hat) + Bias(theta_hat)^2
     RELATIVE EFFICIENCY of two unbiased estimators: Var(T_2)/Var(T_1)
        (the one with smaller variance is more efficient).
C. SYMBOLS: Var describes the spread of the estimator over repeated samples;
   MSE is the expected squared distance from the truth.
D. CONCEPT: accuracy has two ingredients: SYSTEMATIC error (bias) and RANDOM
   error (variance). MSE combines them. A slightly biased estimator
   with much lower variance can beat an unbiased noisy one: the
   BIAS-VARIANCE TRADE-OFF (also Module 46).
E. CONDITIONS: MSE needs finite second moments; the decomposition holds
   for any estimator with finite variance.
F. DERIVATION: write theta_hat - theta = (theta_hat - E[theta_hat]) +
   (E[theta_hat] - theta) = (random part) + (bias, a constant). Squaring and
   taking expectations, the cross term vanishes because
   E[theta_hat - E theta_hat] = 0. Hence MSE = Var + Bias^2.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (precision grows with n): Var(x_bar) = sigma^2/n. For normal data the
      sample MEDIAN has variance about (pi/2) sigma^2/n = 1.57 sigma^2/n,
      so the mean has relative efficiency 64% (simulation: variances 0.0399
      vs 0.0616 for n = 25). The mean is better for normal data; the
      median wins for heavy-tailed data.
   Moderate (shrinkage): estimate mu with T = 0.9 x_bar when mu = 1, sigma^2 = 4,
      n = 10. x_bar: bias 0, Var 0.4, MSE 0.4. T: bias -0.1, Var 0.81 * 0.4
      = 0.324; MSE = 0.324 + 0.01 = 0.334 < 0.4. The biased estimator has
      LOWER MSE here (shrinkage; ridge regression and James-Stein
      estimation work this way).
H. INTERPRETATION: choose estimators by MSE (or by out-of-sample prediction
   error), not by unbiasedness alone. Cramer-Rao bound (Module 38): no
   unbiased estimator can have variance below 1/(n I(theta)).
I. MISTAKES: ignoring variance; thinking low bias alone justifies an
   estimator; confusing the variance of the estimator with the variance of
   the data.
J. RELATED: bias-variance decomposition for predictions (F46.1),
   Cramer-Rao bound (F38.4), regularization (section 43.8).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.5 CONSISTENCY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F37.7  CONSISTENCY
A. NAME: Consistency of an estimator.
B. DEFINITION: theta_hat_n is CONSISTENT for theta if theta_hat_n converges to
   theta in probability as n -> infinity: for every eps > 0,
   P(|theta_hat_n - theta| > eps) -> 0.
   SUFFICIENT CONDITION: Bias(theta_hat_n) -> 0 and Var(theta_hat_n) -> 0 (then
   MSE -> 0).
C. SYMBOLS: theta_hat_n the estimator based on n observations; theta the
   true value; 'in probability' means P(|theta_hat_n - theta| > eps)
   tends to 0.
D. CONCEPT: with enough data the estimator closes in on the truth. It is a
   large-sample property, distinct from unbiasedness (a finite-sample
   average property).
E. CONDITIONS: a large-sample property; bias tending to 0 and variance
   tending to 0 is sufficient.
G. EXAMPLES
   Consistent: x_bar (LLN); s^2; p_hat; the divide-by-n variance
      (biased but consistent: bias -sigma^2/n -> 0); the MLE (under
      regularity conditions).
   Unbiased but NOT consistent: use only the FIRST observation X_1 to estimate
      mu: E[X_1] = mu (unbiased) but Var = sigma^2 never shrinks.
   Biased and consistent: the sample maximum for U(0, theta): bias = -theta/(n + 1)
      -> 0.
H. INTERPRETATION: consistency is the minimal requirement ("more data should
   help"); an inconsistent estimator is useless however long you collect.
I. MISTAKES: equating consistency with unbiasedness.
J. RELATED: LLN (F37.3), MLE properties (F38.4).

COMPARISON: UNBIASED vs CONSISTENT
   Unbiased: E[theta_hat] = theta for each fixed n (average over samples).
   Consistent: theta_hat_n -> theta as n grows (single-sample behavior).
   Neither implies the other (see examples above).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.6 STANDARD ERROR
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F37.8  STANDARD ERROR
A. NAME: Standard error (SE).
B. FORMULAS
     SE(theta_hat) = sd(theta_hat) = sqrt(Var(theta_hat))  (sd of the SAMPLING
        distribution)
     Sample mean:        SE(x_bar) = sigma/sqrt(n), estimated by s/sqrt(n)
     Sample proportion:  SE(p_hat) = sqrt( p (1 - p) / n ), estimated by
        sqrt( p_hat (1 - p_hat) / n )
     Difference of two independent means: sqrt( s_1^2/n_1 + s_2^2/n_2 )
     With finite population correction: multiply by sqrt((N - n)/(N - 1)).
C. SYMBOLS: s the sample sd (estimating sigma); n sample size.
D. CONCEPT: the typical size of the sampling error: how far the estimate is
   likely to be from the parameter. Precision improves with sqrt(n):
   to HALVE the SE you need FOUR times the data.
E. CONDITIONS: the formulas assume iid sampling (no clustering/serial
   correlation); sigma replaced by s adds uncertainty (t distribution
   for small n).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: s = 15, n = 36: SE = 15/6 = 2.5. With n = 144: SE = 15/12 = 1.25.
   Moderate: a poll finds p_hat = 0.40 with n = 100: SE = sqrt(0.4 * 0.6/100)
      = 0.049. A rough 95% margin of error is 1.96 * 0.049 = 0.096 (about 9.6
      percentage points). For n = 1,000 the margin is about 3 points.
   Moderate 2: difference of two means: group 1: s = 10, n = 25; group 2:
      s = 12, n = 36: SE = sqrt(100/25 + 144/36) = sqrt(4 + 4) = 2.83.
H. INTERPRETATION: SE tells how precise the estimate is; it is the building
   block of confidence intervals (estimate +/- critical value x SE) and
   test statistics (estimate/SE).
I. COMMON MISTAKES: confusing standard DEVIATION (spread of the data) with
   standard ERROR (spread of the estimate); reporting SD when SE is
   meant; assuming the SE shrinks with more data that is biased or
   dependent; using SE of a mean to describe individual variation.
J. RELATED: confidence intervals (F39.7), test statistics (F39.2),
   regression standard errors (F40.4).

COMPARISON TABLE: STANDARD DEVIATION vs STANDARD ERROR
  Aspect           Standard deviation               Standard error
  Describes        spread of individual values      uncertainty of an estimate
  Distribution     data / population                sampling distribution
  Formula (mean)   s                                s/sqrt(n)
  Effect of n      does not shrink (stabilizes)     shrinks like 1/sqrt(n)
  Use              describe variability             inference (CI, tests)

BOOTSTRAP [SUPPLEMENTARY]: estimate the sampling distribution by RESAMPLING
the observed sample WITH replacement (B = 1000-10,000 times), computing the
statistic each time. The sd of the bootstrap statistics estimates the SE;
the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles give a percentile confidence interval.
Works for medians, correlations, model metrics (no formula needed).
   rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
   boots = [np.median(rng.choice(x, size=len(x), replace=True))
            for _ in range(5000)]
   se = np.std(boots, ddof=1); ci = np.percentile(boots, [2.5, 97.5])
Limits: needs a representative sample and iid observations; poor for
extremes (max).
SIMULATION OF A SAMPLING DISTRIBUTION (Python)
   means = [rng.exponential(2, size=40).mean() for _ in range(20000)]
   np.mean(means) -> about 2.0;  np.std(means) -> about 0.316 (= 2/sqrt(40)).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: standard errors of model metrics (accuracy, AUC) from
test sets; cross-validation variability; A/B tests (difference of
proportions); learning curves; why tiny test sets give unreliable
rankings of models.


========================================================================
MODULE 38: PARAMETER ESTIMATION
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
We believe the data come from a distribution family (normal, Bernoulli,
exponential) with unknown parameters. Parameter estimation chooses
parameter values that make the model fit the observed data. Two classic
recipes: match moments (method of moments) or choose the parameter that
makes the observed data most probable (maximum likelihood).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.1 METHOD OF MOMENTS (MoM)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F38.1  METHOD OF MOMENTS
A. NAME: Method of moments.
B. PROCEDURE: for a model with k parameters theta_1..theta_k:
     1. Compute the first k population moments E[X^j] as functions of the
        parameters.
     2. Compute the sample moments m_j = (1/n) sum x_i^j.
     3. Solve the equations E[X^j](theta) = m_j (j = 1..k) for the parameters.
C. SYMBOLS: E[X^j] theoretical j-th raw moment (F36.4); m_j its sample
   counterpart.
D. CONCEPT: the sample moments are good estimates of the true moments (by
   the LLN), so choose parameters whose theoretical moments equal the
   observed ones. Simple, often closed-form.
E. CONDITIONS: needs finite moments; the resulting estimates may lie
   outside the allowed parameter space; MoM estimators are consistent but
   usually less efficient than MLEs.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple (exponential): E[X] = 1/lambda. Data 1.2, 0.8, 2.5, 0.4, 1.6: x_bar =
      6.5/5 = 1.3 -> lambda_hat = 1/1.3 = 0.769.
   Moderate 1 (normal, k = 2): E[X] = mu and E[X^2] = sigma^2 + mu^2. Equate:
      mu_hat = x_bar; sigma_hat^2 = m_2 - x_bar^2 = (1/n) sum (x_i - x_bar)^2
      (divide by n!). For 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9: mu_hat = 5, m_2 = 232/8 = 29,
      sigma_hat^2 = 29 - 25 = 4.
   Moderate 2 (uniform): X ~ U(0, theta): E[X] = theta/2 -> theta_hat = 2 x_bar.
      Sample 3.1, 4.7, 2.2, 5.0, 3.8: x_bar = 3.76 -> theta_hat = 7.52 (>= max
      5.0, fine). But sample 1, 1, 1, 9: x_bar = 3 -> theta_hat = 6, LESS than
      the observed 9: impossible! (A well-known weakness: MoM can give
      infeasible values; the MLE, the sample maximum, never does.)
H. INTERPRETATION: a quick, intuitive estimator and a good starting value for
   iterative MLE algorithms.
I. MISTAKES: using the wrong number of equations; using central/raw moments
   inconsistently; forgetting that the second moment includes mu^2.
J. RELATED: MLE (below); generalized method of moments in econometrics.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.2 LIKELIHOOD AND LOG-LIKELIHOOD
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F38.2  LIKELIHOOD FUNCTION
A. NAME: Likelihood function.
B. FORMULA: for iid observations x_1..x_n from a density (or PMF) f(x; theta):
        L(theta) = prod[i=1..n] f(x_i; theta)
   Log-likelihood:  l(theta) = ln L(theta) = sum[i=1..n] ln f(x_i; theta).
C. SYMBOLS: theta the parameter(s); f(x; theta) the density/PMF evaluated at the
   observed x_i, treated as a function of theta; prod the product.
D. CONCEPT: the same expression as the joint density of the data, but read
   the other way round: the DATA ARE FIXED and the PARAMETER VARIES. L(theta)
   says how plausible each parameter value is, given what was
   observed. For discrete data L(theta) is the probability of the observed
   sample under theta.
   !! WARNING !! L(theta) is NOT a probability distribution over theta (it does
   not integrate to 1 over theta) and is NOT P(theta | data). Likelihood
   values are meaningful only in COMPARISONS (ratios).
E. WHY THE LOGARITHM
   (1) ln is increasing, so L and l have the SAME maximizer;
   (2) it turns the product of n factors into a sum: far easier to
       differentiate (F3.8) and add;
   (3) numerical stability: a product of 1,000 small densities underflows
       to 0, a sum of logs does not;
   (4) the negative log-likelihood is the usual LOSS function (cross-entropy
       for classification, squared error for Gaussian regression).
   Independence is what makes L a product; for dependent data use the
   joint density (chain rule).
G. EXAMPLE (Bernoulli): 20 trials, 7 successes. L(p) = p^7 (1 - p)^13.
   l(p) = 7 ln p + 13 ln(1 - p).
   p = 0.35: l = 7(-1.0498) + 13(-0.4308) = -12.949.
   p = 0.50: l = 20 ln 0.5 = -13.863.
   The ratio L(0.35)/L(0.5) = e^(0.914) = 2.49: the data are about 2.5 times
   more probable under p = 0.35 than under p = 0.5.
H. INTERPRETATION: the parameter value with the highest likelihood is
   the one under which the observed data are least surprising.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: treating L(theta) as a probability of theta;
   multiplying densities of dependent observations.
J. RELATED: MLE (F38.3), cross-entropy loss (F46.5), Bayes (F34.5).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.3 MAXIMUM LIKELIHOOD ESTIMATION (MLE)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F38.3  MAXIMUM LIKELIHOOD ESTIMATOR
A. NAME: Maximum likelihood estimator.
B. DEFINITION AND RECIPE
     theta_hat_MLE = argmax over theta of L(theta) = argmax l(theta).
     Recipe (smooth, interior maximum):
       1. Write l(theta) = sum ln f(x_i; theta).
       2. Solve the SCORE EQUATION  dl/dtheta = 0 (system of equations for
          several parameters).
       3. Check it is a maximum: second derivative < 0 (negative definite
          Hessian for several parameters); compare with boundary values.
       4. If there is no closed form, maximize numerically (gradient
          methods, Newton, Module 43), e.g. minimize -l(theta).
     INVARIANCE: the MLE of g(theta) is g(theta_hat).
C. SYMBOLS: argmax the argument at which the function is largest; dl/dtheta the
   score (derivative of the log-likelihood).
D. CONCEPT: choose the parameter value under which the observed data are
   most probable (least surprising).
E. CONDITIONS: model correctly specified (family); iid (or specified
   dependence); the maximum may lie on the boundary of the parameter space
   (derivative methods fail, see uniform below); the likelihood may have
   several local maxima (mixtures, neural nets) or none.
G. WORKED MLE DERIVATIONS
   (1) BERNOULLI(p), k successes in n trials:
       l(p) = k ln p + (n - k) ln(1 - p).
       l'(p) = k/p - (n - k)/(1 - p) = 0 -> k(1 - p) = (n - k) p -> p_hat = k/n.
       l''(p) = -k/p^2 - (n - k)/(1 - p)^2 < 0: maximum.
       Data: n = 20, k = 7: p_hat = 0.35 (the sample proportion).
   (2) EXPONENTIAL(lambda): f = lambda e^(-lambda x).
       l(lambda) = n ln lambda - lambda sum x_i.
       l' = n/lambda - sum x_i = 0 -> lambda_hat = n/sum x_i = 1/x_bar.
       l'' = -n/lambda^2 < 0. Data 1.2, 0.8, 2.5, 0.4, 1.6: lambda_hat = 5/6.5
       = 0.769 (same as the MoM here).
   (3) NORMAL(mu, sigma^2):
       l(mu, sigma^2) = -(n/2) ln(2 pi) - (n/2) ln sigma^2 - sum (x_i - mu)^2/(2 sigma^2).
       d/d mu: sum (x_i - mu)/sigma^2 = 0 -> mu_hat = x_bar.
       d/d(sigma^2): -n/(2 sigma^2) + sum (x_i - mu)^2/(2 sigma^4) = 0
          -> sigma_hat^2 = (1/n) sum (x_i - x_bar)^2.
       !! The MLE of the variance divides by n, so it is BIASED (bias
       -sigma^2/n) but consistent; s^2 (divide by n - 1) is the unbiased
       alternative. !!
       Data 2, 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 7, 9: mu_hat = 5; sigma_hat^2 = 4 (s^2 = 4.571).
       scipy.stats.norm.fit(data) returns (5.0, 2.0): sd uses divisor n.
   (4) POISSON(lambda) [SUPPLEMENTARY]: l = sum x_i ln lambda - n lambda -
       sum ln x_i!; lambda_hat = x_bar.
   (5) UNIFORM(0, theta) (boundary case): L(theta) = theta^(-n) for theta >=
       max x_i, and 0 if theta < max x_i. L DECREASES in theta, so it is
       maximized at the smallest allowed value: theta_hat = max(x_i). Setting
       the derivative to zero would give no solution. LESSON: always check the
       support. (This MLE is biased: E = n theta/(n + 1).)
   (6) LINEAR REGRESSION with Gaussian errors: y_i = x_i^T beta + eps_i,
       eps_i ~ N(0, sigma^2): maximizing l over beta is equivalent to
       MINIMIZING sum (y_i - x_i^T beta)^2: ordinary least squares (Module
       40). Gaussian noise <=> squared-error loss; Laplace noise <=>
       absolute-error loss.
   (7) LOGISTIC REGRESSION: negative Bernoulli log-likelihood =
       cross-entropy loss; no closed form: gradient descent / Newton
       (IRLS), Module 43.
H. INTERPRETATION: the MLE is the best-supported parameter under the model;
   with the Fisher information it also gives standard errors.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: maximizing L(theta) when the support depends on theta
   without checking boundaries; forgetting to verify a maximum; confusing the
   MLE of sigma^2 (divide by n) with s^2; treating the likelihood as a
   probability of the parameter; using non-independent data in a product
   likelihood; reporting a local maximum.
J. RELATED: MoM, Bayesian (MAP) estimation, cross-entropy loss (Module 48),
   Newton's method (Module 43).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.4 PROPERTIES OF ESTIMATORS (INCLUDING MLE PROPERTIES)
------------------------------------------------------------------------
GENERAL CRITERIA
   UNBIASEDNESS (F37.5); CONSISTENCY (F37.7); EFFICIENCY (low variance among
   unbiased estimators, F37.6); LOW MSE; SUFFICIENCY (the statistic
   retains all the information about theta; e.g. x_bar and s^2 for the
   normal); ROBUSTNESS (insensitivity to outliers or model misspecification).
F38.4  FISHER INFORMATION AND THE CRAMER-RAO BOUND
B. Fisher information (per observation): I(theta) = -E[ d^2 ln f(X; theta)/
   d theta^2 ] = E[ (d ln f/d theta)^2 ].
   CRAMER-RAO LOWER BOUND: any UNBIASED estimator satisfies
        Var(theta_hat) >= 1 / ( n I(theta) ).
   ASYMPTOTIC PROPERTIES OF THE MLE (under regularity conditions):
     - CONSISTENT: theta_hat -> theta.
     - ASYMPTOTICALLY NORMAL: theta_hat approx N( theta, 1/(n I(theta)) ).
     - ASYMPTOTICALLY EFFICIENT: attains the Cramer-Rao bound as n grows.
     - INVARIANT under reparameterization.
   Finite-sample caveats: may be biased (sigma^2 for the normal; the
   uniform maximum); may be hard to compute; sensitive to misspecification.
C. SYMBOLS: I(theta) the information a single observation carries about theta:
   large curvature of the log-likelihood = sharp peak = precise estimate.
D. CONCEPT: the curvature of l at its peak measures how precisely the data
   pin down theta; its reciprocal is the variance.
E. CONDITIONS: regularity conditions (smooth model, support independent
   of theta, interior parameter); the bound applies to unbiased
   estimators; asymptotic results need large n.
G. EXAMPLE (Bernoulli): ln f = x ln p + (1 - x) ln(1 - p); second derivative
   -x/p^2 - (1 - x)/(1 - p)^2; expectation: I(p) = 1/p + 1/(1 - p) =
   1/(p(1 - p)). Bound: Var(p_hat) >= p(1 - p)/n, which the sample proportion
   attains exactly: p_hat is efficient. (n = 100, p = 0.4: SE = 0.049.)
H. INTERPRETATION: standard error of an MLE = 1/sqrt(n I(theta_hat)); the
   observed information (negative Hessian of l at theta_hat) is used in
   practice (software output of standard errors for logistic regression).
I. MISTAKES: expecting the MLE to be unbiased; applying asymptotic results
   with tiny n; ignoring that the bound applies to UNBIASED estimators
   only.
J. RELATED: standard errors of coefficients, Hessian (F12.6), efficiency
   (F37.6).

COMPARISON TABLE: METHOD OF MOMENTS vs MAXIMUM LIKELIHOOD
  Aspect               Method of moments           Maximum likelihood
  Principle            match sample and            maximize probability
                       theoretical moments         of observed data
  Needs                moments as functions of     full distribution f(x; theta)
                       parameters
  Closed form          usually easy                often, sometimes not
  Efficiency           often lower                 asymptotically optimal
  Infeasible values    possible (uniform example)  respects the parameter space
  Bias                 can be biased               can be biased (normal variance)
  Computation          solve equations             optimization (Module 43)
  Use                  quick/starting values       standard in practice (GLMs,
                                                   neural net losses)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.5 IDENTIFIABILITY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F38.5  IDENTIFIABILITY
A. NAME: Parameter identifiability.
B. DEFINITION: a model is IDENTIFIABLE if different parameter values give
   different distributions: f(x; theta_1) = f(x; theta_2) for all x implies
   theta_1 = theta_2. If not, no amount of data can tell the parameters apart.
C. SYMBOLS: theta_1, theta_2 two parameter values; f(x; theta) the model
   distribution.
D. CONCEPT: the parameters must have distinct observable consequences. When
   they do not, the likelihood has a flat ridge or several equal peaks and
   the MLE is not unique.
E. CONDITIONS: identifiability can be global or local;
   near-non-identifiability (weak identification) is also harmful in
   practice.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: X ~ N(a + b, sigma^2) with two parameters a and b: only the SUM
      a + b affects the distribution; a and b are NOT separately
      identifiable. Reparameterize with c = a + b (identifiable).
   Moderate 1 (collinearity): regression y = b0 + b1 x1 + b2 x2 where x2 = 2 x1
      exactly: only b1 + 2 b2 is identified; X^T X is singular (Module 8:
      dependent columns, nullity > 0): infinitely many least-squares
      solutions. The "dummy-variable trap" (including all category dummies
      plus an intercept) is the same problem.
   Moderate 2 (label switching): a two-component Gaussian mixture is
      unchanged by swapping the component labels: the labeled parameters
      are not identifiable (fix by ordering the means).
   Moderate 3 (neural networks): permuting hidden units, or scaling
      weights up in one layer and down in the next with ReLU, gives the
      same function: parameters are not identifiable, yet predictions
      are fine (identifiability matters for interpretation, less for
      prediction).
   Moderate 4: Y ~ Poisson(alpha beta): only the product alpha beta is
      identified.
H. CONSEQUENCES: non-unique MLE; infinite or huge standard errors;
   numerical instability; coefficients that cannot be interpreted.
   PRACTICAL (weak) NON-IDENTIFIABILITY: near-collinear features make
   parameters barely distinguishable and estimates unstable.
   FIXES: reparameterize; add constraints (sum-to-zero, drop a dummy);
   add regularization (ridge shifts eigenvalues, Module 9); collect more
   informative data (vary the inputs); use priors.
I. MISTAKES: reading individual coefficients of a non-identified model;
   assuming optimization failure when the likelihood is genuinely flat.
J. RELATED: rank and nullity (Module 8), Hessian singularity (Module 12),
   Fisher information being singular.

BAYESIAN ESTIMATION [SUPPLEMENTARY]: treat theta as random with a PRIOR
p(theta); POSTERIOR p(theta | data) is proportional to L(theta) p(theta)
(Bayes, F34.5). MAP estimate = argmax of the posterior. Example
(Beta-Bernoulli): prior Beta(2, 2); data 7 successes in 20: posterior
Beta(9, 15); posterior mean 9/24 = 0.375; MAP (a + k - 1)/(a + b + n - 2)
= 8/22 = 0.364; MLE 0.35: the prior pulls the estimate toward 0.5
(shrinkage). Gaussian prior on regression coefficients gives ridge
regression; Laplace prior gives lasso.

PYTHON: NUMERICAL MLE
   from scipy import stats, optimize
   x = np.array([1.2, 0.8, 2.5, 0.4, 1.6])
   nll = lambda lam: -np.sum(stats.expon.logpdf(x, scale=1/lam))
   optimize.minimize_scalar(nll, bounds=(1e-3, 10), method="bounded").x
   -> 0.7692 (matches 1/x_bar). Library fits: stats.norm.fit(x),
   stats.expon.fit(x, floc=0).

DATA SCIENCE LINK (Modules 37-38): model training = estimating parameters
(MLE or penalized MLE); loss functions are negative log-likelihoods;
standard errors/confidence intervals of coefficients; the Fisher
information matrix underlies natural-gradient methods and Laplace
approximations; identifiability explains unstable coefficients under
multicollinearity.


========================================================================
MODULE 39: HYPOTHESIS TESTING
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
A hypothesis test is a structured way to ask: "Is what I observed in my
sample surprising enough, if nothing special were going on, that I
should doubt that 'nothing special' explanation?" It is proof by
probabilistic contradiction: assume the boring explanation (the null),
see how unlikely the data would be under it, and reject it if the data
would be too surprising.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.1 NULL AND ALTERNATIVE HYPOTHESES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   NULL HYPOTHESIS H0: the default claim to be tested: no effect, no
      difference, parameter equals a specified value (mu = 100,
      p1 = p2, beta = 0). It always contains the "equals" sign.
   ALTERNATIVE HYPOTHESIS H1 (or Ha): what we conclude if the data
      contradict H0: the research claim.
   TWO-SIDED:   H0: mu = mu0   vs   H1: mu != mu0   (any difference)
   ONE-SIDED:   H0: mu = mu0 (or <=) vs H1: mu > mu0 (right-tailed), or
                H1: mu < mu0 (left-tailed).
   The direction of H1 must be chosen BEFORE seeing the data, from the
   question being asked. Choosing a one-sided test after seeing which way
   the data point is cheating (it halves the p-value).
LOGIC OF TESTING
   - We never "prove" H0. The result is either REJECT H0 (evidence against
     it) or FAIL TO REJECT H0 (insufficient evidence). Failing to reject is
     not the same as "H0 is true": the sample may simply be too small.
   - Analogy: a court. H0: "innocent". Evidence insufficient: "not guilty"
     (not "proved innocent").
EXAMPLES
   A machine should fill bottles with 500 ml: H0: mu = 500, H1: mu != 500.
   A new website design should raise the conversion rate above the current
      4%: H0: p = 0.04, H1: p > 0.04.
   A model's mean error is claimed to be at most 10: H0: mu <= 10, H1: mu > 10.
COMMON MISTAKES: putting the claim you hope to prove in H0; writing
hypotheses about SAMPLE statistics (x_bar) instead of PARAMETERS (mu);
choosing the direction after seeing the data.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.2 SIGNIFICANCE LEVEL
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F39.1  SIGNIFICANCE LEVEL ALPHA
A. NAME: Significance level.
B. DEFINITION: alpha = P(reject H0 | H0 is true) = P(Type I error). It is
   chosen BEFORE the analysis; conventional values 0.05, 0.01, 0.10.
   Decision rule: reject H0 if the test statistic falls in the REJECTION
   (critical) REGION, equivalently if p-value <= alpha.
C. SYMBOLS: alpha the significance level; H0 the null hypothesis;
   'reject' means the test statistic falls in the rejection region.
D. CONCEPT: alpha is the long-run false-alarm rate we are willing to
   accept when H0 is actually true. Smaller alpha means stricter evidence
   but more Type II errors (missed effects).
E. CONDITIONS: alpha is a threshold set by the analyst based on the cost of
   false positives (drug approval: small; exploratory screening: larger).
   0.05 is a convention, not a law of nature.
G. EXAMPLE: alpha = 0.05, two-sided z-test: reject when |z| > 1.96;
   across 100 true null hypotheses about 5 will be rejected by chance.
H. INTERPRETATION: alpha is the false-alarm rate accepted in advance.
I. MISTAKES: choosing alpha after seeing the p-value; reading alpha as the
   probability that H0 is true; treating 0.05 as a magic boundary.
J. RELATED: p-value (F39.5), power (F39.6), confidence level 1 - alpha
   (F39.7).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.3 TEST STATISTICS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F39.2  GENERAL FORM OF A TEST STATISTIC
B. test statistic = (estimate - value claimed under H0) / (standard error)
C. SYMBOLS: 'estimate' the sample statistic; 'value under H0' the
   hypothesized value; SE its standard error.
D. CONCEPT: measures how many standard errors the observed estimate lies from
   what H0 predicts. Its sampling distribution when H0 is true is known
   (normal, t, chi-square, F), which lets us compute probabilities.
E. CONDITIONS: the null distribution of the statistic must be known
   (normal, t, chi-square, F), which requires the test's assumptions.
G. EXAMPLE: estimate x_bar = 104, H0: mu = 100, SE = 2.5: statistic =
   (104 - 100)/2.5 = 1.6.
H. INTERPRETATION: the statistic counts how many standard errors the
   data are from H0.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using the sd instead of the SE in the denominator;
   using the wrong null distribution.
J. RELATED: z-test (F39.3), t-statistic (F39.4), regression t-tests
   (F40.4).
F39.3  z-TEST FOR A MEAN (sigma known, or large n)
B. Z = (x_bar - mu0) / (sigma / sqrt(n))   ~  N(0, 1) when H0: mu = mu0 holds.
C. SYMBOLS: x_bar sample mean; mu0 hypothesized mean; sigma population sd
   (known); n sample size.
D. CONCEPT: how many standard errors the sample mean lies from the
   hypothesized mean, assuming the population sd is known.
E. CONDITIONS: random sample; population normal OR n large (CLT); sigma known
   (rare in practice).
G. EXAMPLE: H0: mu = 100 vs H1: mu != 100; n = 36, x_bar = 104, sigma = 15.
   SE = 15/6 = 2.5; z = (104 - 100)/2.5 = 1.6. Two-sided p = 2 P(Z >= 1.6)
   = 0.1096 > 0.05: fail to reject. (One-sided p = 0.0548: still above
   0.05.)
H. INTERPRETATION: z = 1.6 is below 1.96, so the evidence is not strong
   enough at the 5% level.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using z when sigma is estimated from a small sample
   (use t); confusing one-sided and two-sided p-values.
J. RELATED: t-test (F39.4), confidence interval (F39.7), CLT (F37.4).
F39.4  t-STATISTIC (sigma unknown)
B. T = (x_bar - mu0) / (s / sqrt(n))  ~  t with n - 1 degrees of freedom
   under H0 (population normal).
C. SYMBOLS: s sample sd; n - 1 the degrees of freedom (df).
D. CONCEPT: replacing the unknown sigma by s adds extra randomness; the
   Student t distribution has HEAVIER TAILS than the normal to account for
   it. As df grows t approaches N(0, 1).
   Critical values for a two-sided test with alpha = 0.05 (t_(0.975, df)):
        df = 5: 2.571;  9: 2.262;  14: 2.145;  24: 2.064;  29: 2.045;
        100: 1.984;  infinity: 1.960.
   Two-sided 99%: df = 9: 3.250; infinity: 2.576.
E. CONDITIONS: independent observations; population approximately normal (or n
   large; t is fairly robust except with strong skew or outliers in
   small samples).
G. EXAMPLE: n = 10, x_bar = 12.23, s = 0.359, H0: mu = 12: T =
   0.23/0.1136 = 2.025, df = 9, p = 0.0735 (section 39.7).
H. INTERPRETATION: T = 2.025 is below the 5% critical value 2.262: not
   significant.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using n instead of n - 1 for the degrees of freedom;
   using z critical values with small samples.
J. RELATED: z-statistic (F39.3), t-tests (F39.8), regression (F40.4).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.4 p-VALUES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F39.5  p-VALUE
A. NAME: p-value.
B. DEFINITION: the probability, ASSUMING H0 IS TRUE, of obtaining a test
   statistic at least as extreme as the one observed (in the direction(s) of
   H1).
     right-tailed: p = P(T >= t_obs | H0)
     left-tailed:  p = P(T <= t_obs | H0)
     two-sided:    p = P(|T| >= |t_obs| | H0) = 2 P(T >= |t_obs|) for
                   symmetric distributions.
   Decision: reject H0 if p <= alpha.
C. SYMBOLS: t_obs the value computed from the data; T the random statistic
   under H0.
D. CONCEPT: "How surprising are data like these if nothing is going on?" A small
   p means such data would be rare under H0, which is evidence against H0.
E. CONDITIONS: p-values are valid only if the model/assumptions behind the
   null distribution hold and the test was specified in advance. If H0 is
   true and the test statistic is continuous, p is UNIFORMLY distributed on
   (0, 1): a p-value below 0.05 happens 5% of the time by design
   (simulation with 5,000 t-tests on null data gives 5.1%).
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: z = 1.6 (above): p = 2(1 - 0.9452) = 0.1096.
   Moderate: t = 2.025 with df = 9: two-sided p = 0.0735 (software). Since
      2.025 < the critical value 2.262, we fail to reject at 5%; the
      p-value and the critical-value method always agree.
H. INTERPRETATION: a small p means the data would be unusual if H0 were
   true; it is not a probability about H0.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: see the list 'What a p-value is not' in this
   section.
J. RELATED: alpha (F39.1), duality with confidence intervals (F39.7),
   multiple testing (section 39.5).
WHAT A p-VALUE IS NOT (the most important list in this module)
   - It is NOT the probability that H0 is true. (p = 0.03 does not mean a 3%
     chance that H0 holds; that needs a prior: Bayes, F34.5.)
   - It is NOT the probability that the result occurred "by chance".
   - It is NOT the probability that a repeat experiment would agree.
   - It is NOT the size or importance of the effect.
   - 1 - p is NOT the probability that H1 is true.
   - A large p does NOT show that H0 is true ("absence of evidence is not
     evidence of absence"; the test may lack power).
   - p-values depend on sample size: with huge n trivial effects get tiny
     p-values.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: the dichotomy p = 0.049 "significant" vs p = 0.051 "not";
   reporting only "p < 0.05" without estimate and interval; p-hacking
   (trying many analyses and reporting the one with p < 0.05);
   one-sided p after peeking.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.5 TYPE I AND TYPE II ERRORS; POWER
------------------------------------------------------------------------
THE FOUR OUTCOMES
                       Truth: H0 true          Truth: H0 false
   Reject H0           TYPE I ERROR            Correct (power = 1 - beta)
                       (false positive), prob. alpha
   Fail to reject H0   Correct (prob. 1-alpha) TYPE II ERROR
                                               (false negative), prob. beta
F39.6  ERROR RATES AND POWER
B. alpha = P(reject H0 | H0 true)
   beta  = P(fail to reject H0 | H1 true: a specific alternative)
   POWER = 1 - beta = P(reject H0 | H1 true).
   Power depends on: effect size (true difference delta), population
   variability sigma, sample size n, significance level alpha, and
   one- vs two-sided test. For the one-sided z-test with true mean
   mu1 = mu0 + delta:
       power = P( Z > z_alpha - delta sqrt(n)/sigma ),
   and the sample size for target power 1 - beta is
       n = ( (z_alpha + z_beta) sigma / delta )^2   (one-sided)
       n = ( (z_(alpha/2) + z_beta) sigma / delta )^2   (two-sided).
C. SYMBOLS: z_alpha the upper-alpha point of N(0,1) (1.645 for 0.05; 1.960 for
   alpha/2 = 0.025); z_beta the point for the Type II error (0.8416 for 80%
   power; 1.2816 for 90%).
D. CONCEPT: alpha controls false alarms when nothing is going on; power is
   the chance to detect a real effect of a given size. Lowering alpha
   (stricter) lowers power unless n grows; more data, bigger effects,
   less noise all raise power.
E. CONDITIONS: power is computed for a specific alternative; the
   formulas shown are for the z-test with known sigma (approximate for
   t-tests).
G. EXAMPLE (power): H0: mu = 100 vs H1: mu > 100; sigma = 15, n = 36, alpha =
   0.05 (one-sided). Reject when x_bar > 100 + 1.645 * 2.5 = 104.11.
   If the TRUE mean is 105 (delta = 5): power = P(x_bar > 104.11 | mu = 105)
   = P(Z > (104.11 - 105)/2.5 = -0.355) = 0.639. So there is only a 64%
   chance to detect a 5-point effect; beta = 0.361.
   SAMPLE SIZE for 80% power (delta = 5, sigma = 15): one-sided
   n = ((1.645 + 0.8416) * 15/5)^2 = (7.46)^2 = 55.6 -> 56 (two-sided,
   alpha = 0.05: ((1.96 + 0.8416) * 3)^2 = 70.6 -> 71).
H. INTERPRETATION: an underpowered study (low power) rarely finds real effects
   and, when it does find "significant" ones, tends to overestimate them.
   Plan sample size BEFORE collecting data (power analysis).
I. MISTAKES: calling "not significant" a proof of no effect in a low-power
   study; confusing alpha with beta or with power; computing "post-hoc
   power" from the observed p-value (uninformative).
J. RELATED: sample size and CI width (F39.7), Type I and II errors
   (table above).
MULTIPLE TESTING: testing m independent true nulls at alpha = 0.05 each gives
P(at least one false positive) = 1 - 0.95^m: m = 20 gives 64%.
   Bonferroni: use alpha/m per test (0.0025 for 20 tests); Benjamini-
   Hochberg controls the false discovery rate (less conservative). Data
   science: testing hundreds of features, many model variants or many
   A/B segments inflates false positives; use corrections or hold-out
   confirmation.
COMPARISON TABLE: TYPE I vs TYPE II ERROR
  Aspect         Type I                      Type II
  Meaning        reject a TRUE H0            fail to reject a FALSE H0
  Also called    false positive, false alarm false negative, miss
  Probability    alpha (set by analyst)      beta (depends on effect, n, alpha)
  Control        choose alpha                increase n, effect, reduce noise
  Trade-off      lowering alpha raises beta (for fixed n)
  Example        declaring a useless drug    missing a useful drug
                 effective                   (confirming it is "no different")
  ML analog      false positive rate         false negative rate (1 - recall)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.6 CONFIDENCE INTERVALS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F39.7  CONFIDENCE INTERVAL FOR A MEAN
A. NAME: Confidence interval (CI) for a population mean.
B. FORMULAS
     sigma known (or large n):   x_bar +/- z_(alpha/2) * sigma/sqrt(n)
     sigma unknown:              x_bar +/- t_(alpha/2, n-1) * s/sqrt(n)
     General:  estimate +/- critical value x standard error;
     the quantity "critical value x SE" is the MARGIN OF ERROR E.
     Proportion (large-sample, Wald): p_hat +/- z_(alpha/2) sqrt(p_hat (1 - p_hat)/n).
     Sample size for a margin E (mean): n = (z_(alpha/2) sigma / E)^2;
        (proportion, worst case p = 0.5): n = z^2 * 0.25/E^2.
C. SYMBOLS: confidence level 1 - alpha (e.g. 95%); t_(alpha/2, n-1) the upper
   alpha/2 point of t with n - 1 df; SE the standard error (F37.8).
D. CONCEPT: a range of plausible parameter values. The "95%" describes the
   PROCEDURE: if you repeated the sampling many times and built an interval
   each time, about 95% of those intervals would contain the true
   parameter.
E. CONDITIONS: same as the corresponding test (random sample, near-normal
   or large n). Wald proportion intervals need n p_hat >= 10 and
   n(1 - p_hat) >= 10; otherwise use Wilson or exact methods.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: n = 25, x_bar = 52, s = 10: SE = 2, t_(0.975, 24) = 2.064:
      CI = 52 +/- 4.13 = (47.87, 56.13).
   Moderate 1 (from data): sample 12.1, 11.8, 12.6, 12.3, 11.9, 12.8, 12.2,
      12.5, 11.7, 12.4 (n = 10): x_bar = 12.23, s = 0.359, SE = 0.1136,
      t_(0.975, 9) = 2.262: CI = 12.23 +/- 0.257 = (11.973, 12.487).
   Moderate 2 (proportion): p_hat = 0.40, n = 100: margin = 1.96 * 0.049 =
      0.096: CI = (0.304, 0.496).
   Moderate 3 (planning): to estimate a mean with sigma = 15 to within +/-3
      at 95%: n = (1.96 * 15/3)^2 = 96.04 -> 97.
H. INTERPRETATION: width shrinks like 1/sqrt(n), grows with s and with the
   confidence level (99% is wider than 95%). The interval conveys effect
   size AND uncertainty; it is more informative than a bare p-value.
   CORRECT: "We are 95% confident that mu lies between 47.87 and 56.13 (the
   method captures the truth in 95% of repetitions)."
   INCORRECT (frequentist): "there is a 95% probability that mu lies in this
   particular interval" (mu is fixed; the interval is the random object
   before data are collected). A Bayesian credible interval does have
   that probability interpretation, under a prior.
   DUALITY WITH TESTS: a two-sided test of H0: mu = mu0 at level alpha rejects
   exactly when mu0 lies OUTSIDE the (1 - alpha) CI. In the example above,
   H0: mu = 12 is NOT rejected because 12 lies inside (11.973, 12.487).
I. MISTAKES: reading 95% as the proportion of DATA inside the interval (that
   is a prediction interval, much wider); comparing two CIs for overlap as
   a substitute for testing the difference (overlap is not a reliable test);
   using z instead of t for small n; applying Wald proportions to tiny
   counts.
J. RELATED: duality with tests (this section), standard error (F37.8),
   prediction intervals (F40.5).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.7 t-TESTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F39.8  THE THREE t-TESTS
A. ONE-SAMPLE t-TEST: compares a mean with a fixed value.
     H0: mu = mu0.  T = (x_bar - mu0)/(s/sqrt(n)),  df = n - 1.
B. TWO-SAMPLE (INDEPENDENT-GROUPS) t-TEST: compares two means from separate,
   unrelated groups. H0: mu_1 = mu_2.
     WELCH (unequal variances allowed; the safe default):
        T = (x_bar_1 - x_bar_2) / sqrt( s_1^2/n_1 + s_2^2/n_2 ),
        df from the Welch-Satterthwaite formula (non-integer).
     POOLED (assumes equal variances):
        T = (x_bar_1 - x_bar_2) / ( s_p sqrt(1/n_1 + 1/n_2) ),
        s_p^2 = ((n_1 - 1) s_1^2 + (n_2 - 1) s_2^2)/(n_1 + n_2 - 2),
        df = n_1 + n_2 - 2.
C. PAIRED t-TEST: compares two measurements on the SAME units (before/after,
   matched pairs). Compute differences d_i = after_i - before_i, then do a
   one-sample t-test on the d_i with H0: mu_d = 0:
        T = d_bar / (s_d/sqrt(n)),  df = n - 1.
D. CONCEPT: all three divide an estimated difference by its standard error.
   Pairing removes between-subject variability and is usually far more
   powerful when the pairs are correlated.
E. CONDITIONS: see 39.8. CHOOSE the test by the DESIGN: same units measured
   twice -> paired; two separate groups -> two-sample.
G. WORKED EXAMPLES
   (1) ONE-SAMPLE: data in 39.6 (n = 10, x_bar = 12.23, s = 0.359).
       H0: mu = 12 vs H1: mu != 12, alpha = 0.05.
       T = (12.23 - 12)/0.1136 = 2.025, df = 9, p = 0.0735. Since p > 0.05
       (and |T| < 2.262) we FAIL TO REJECT H0: no convincing evidence that
       the mean differs from 12 (the 95% CI (11.97, 12.49) contains 12).
   (2) WELCH TWO-SAMPLE: group A (n = 8): 23, 25, 21, 27, 24, 26, 22, 25 ->
       mean 24.125, s^2 = 4.125. Group B (n = 8): 20, 22, 19, 24, 21, 23, 20,
       22 -> mean 21.375, s^2 = 2.839.
       Difference = 2.75. SE = sqrt(4.125/8 + 2.839/8) = sqrt(0.8705) = 0.933.
       T = 2.75/0.933 = 2.947; Welch df = 13.5; p = 0.0109 -> REJECT H0 at 5%:
       evidence that the means differ.
       (Pooled version: same T because n_1 = n_2; df = 14; p = 0.0106.)
       Effect size: Cohen's d = 2.75/s_p = 2.75/1.866 = 1.47 (large).
       95% CI for the difference: 2.75 +/- t_(0.975, 13.5) * 0.933 = 2.75 +/- 2.0
       -> about (0.75, 4.75).
   (3) PAIRED: 6 subjects' scores before: 80, 85, 90, 78, 92, 88; after: 75,
       83, 86, 79, 87, 84. Differences (after - before): -5, -2, -4, 1, -5,
       -4. d_bar = -3.167, s_d = 2.317, SE = 2.317/sqrt(6) = 0.946.
       T = -3.167/0.946 = -3.348, df = 5, p = 0.0204 < 0.05: REJECT H0: the
       scores dropped by about 3.2 points on average (95% CI: -3.167 +/-
       2.571 * 0.946 = (-5.60, -0.73)).
       (Treating these as independent groups would ignore the pairing and
       give a much larger SE: the paired test is the right one here.)
H. INTERPRETATION: report the estimate, CI, effect size and p-value together
   ("scores fell by 3.2 points, 95% CI 0.7 to 5.6, p = 0.02").
I. COMMON MISTAKES: using an independent test on paired data (or vice
   versa); using the pooled test with very unequal variances and unequal
   sample sizes; dropping the sign when interpreting direction; deciding
   the test after seeing the results; running t-tests on many features
   without correction.
J. RELATED: z-test, ANOVA (more than two groups), chi-square tests for
   categorical data [SUPPLEMENTARY], nonparametric alternatives: Wilcoxon
   signed-rank (paired/one-sample), Mann-Whitney U (two independent
   groups), permutation tests.
PYTHON
   from scipy import stats
   stats.ttest_1samp(x, popmean=12)
   stats.ttest_ind(a, b, equal_var=False)       # Welch
   stats.ttest_rel(after, before)               # paired
   stats.sem(x)                                  # standard error
   stats.t.interval(0.95, df, loc=xbar, scale=se)

COMPARISON: z-TEST vs t-TEST
  Aspect              z-test                          t-test
  Population sd       known                           unknown, estimated by s
  Reference dist.     N(0, 1)                         t with df (heavier tails)
  Sample size         any if sigma known/large n      any (esp. small n)
  Critical value 5%   1.960 two-sided                 larger for small df (2.262 at df 9)
  Converges           -                               to z as df -> infinity
  Typical use         large samples, proportions      means in real data

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.8 ASSUMPTIONS OF STATISTICAL TESTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
Every test is valid only under assumptions. Know them, check them, and
know which violations are serious.
  ASSUMPTION                     WHY IT MATTERS / HOW TO CHECK / REMEDY
  Random sampling or randomized  Inference to a population needs it; bias
  assignment                     cannot be fixed by n. Check design.
  INDEPENDENCE of observations   MOST critical. Repeated measures on the same
                                 person, clusters, time series, or duplicated
                                 rows make SEs too small and p-values too
                                 low (pseudo-replication; Type I inflated).
                                 Remedy: paired/mixed models, clustered SEs,
                                 aggregate to the independent unit.
  Normality (t-tests)            Of the population for small n; for large n
                                 the CLT makes the mean nearly normal. Check
                                 histogram/Q-Q plot, never rely only on a
                                 Shapiro-Wilk p-value (with large n it flags
                                 trivial deviations; with small n has no
                                 power). Remedy: transform, nonparametric
                                 test, bootstrap, larger n.
  Equal variances (pooled t,     Check ratio of sds (> 2 suspicious); remedy:
  ANOVA)                         Welch.
  Measurement scale              Means need interval/ratio data; ordinal data
                                 (Likert) often use rank tests.
  No extreme outliers            Inflate s and distort x_bar; investigate, use
                                 robust methods.
  Adequate sample size           Large-sample z and Wald methods need n large;
                                 small samples need exact methods.
  Pre-specified analysis         Choose hypotheses, test and alpha before
                                 looking; avoid p-hacking, optional stopping.
ROBUSTNESS SUMMARY: t-tests tolerate mild non-normality (especially with
equal n and symmetric data); they are NOT robust to non-independence,
and moderately sensitive to outliers/heavy skew with small n; Welch is
robust to unequal variances.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
39.9 INTERPRETATION OF STATISTICAL SIGNIFICANCE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
KEY PRINCIPLES
   1. STATISTICAL SIGNIFICANCE != PRACTICAL IMPORTANCE.
      Significance says the effect is unlikely to be exactly zero; it says
      nothing about whether it is large or useful.
      Example: two groups each of 1,000,000 observations; true difference of
      0.01 standard deviations (d = 0.01). The test statistic is about
      z = d sqrt(n/2) = 0.01 * 707 = 7.07, p approx 1.5 x 10^-12: "highly
      significant" yet practically nil. With n = 20 per group a real,
      important effect of d = 0.5 may not reach significance (power about
      34%).
   2. NOT SIGNIFICANT != NO EFFECT. Report the CI: a wide interval
      containing both zero and important values means "inconclusive".
   3. EFFECT SIZES give magnitude: raw difference in natural units;
      Cohen's d = (x_bar_1 - x_bar_2)/s_p (rough benchmarks 0.2 small,
      0.5 medium, 0.8 large; context matters); r or R^2; odds ratios.
   4. The difference between "significant" and "not significant" is not
      itself statistically significant (comparing two p-values is not a
      test of the difference).
   5. ALPHA = 0.05 is a convention; consider costs of errors; avoid
      treating p = 0.049 and p = 0.051 differently.
   6. MULTIPLE COMPARISONS and the garden of forking paths inflate false
      positives; pre-register or hold out data; adjust.
   7. REPLICATION and prior plausibility matter: a surprising result with p =
      0.03 has a high chance of being a false positive (Bayes: low prior
      probability of a true effect).
   8. Report: estimate, CI, effect size, test, df, exact p-value, n, and
      assumption checks. The American Statistical Association (2016)
      statement: a p-value does not measure the probability that the
      studied hypothesis is true, and scientific conclusions should not
      be based only on whether p passes a threshold.
STEP-BY-STEP CHECKLIST FOR A TEST
   1. State H0 and H1 (parameters, direction) and alpha before the data.
   2. Choose the test from the design and data type.
   3. Check assumptions (independence first).
   4. Compute the statistic, df, p-value, and the CI/effect size.
   5. Decide (reject / fail to reject) and state it in context, in words.
   6. Discuss limitations (power, bias, multiplicity).
DATA SCIENCE LINKS
   - A/B testing: two-proportion z-test or Welch t-test on a metric;
     pre-compute sample size from power analysis; do not stop the moment
     p < 0.05 (optional stopping inflates Type I error); use corrections
     for many metrics/segments.
   - Comparing models: cross-validation folds are NOT independent (they share
     training data); use corrected paired t-tests, repeated CV with
     corrections, or McNemar's test on the same test set.
   - Feature screening with hundreds of tests: control the false discovery
     rate.
   - A statistically significant improvement of 0.001 in AUC may be
     practically irrelevant.
   - Regression coefficients: t-test of H0: beta_j = 0 (Module 40).
   - Drift detection: KS test or chi-square tests on feature distributions;
     with huge data everything is "significant": use effect sizes.


========================================================================
MODULE 40: LINEAR REGRESSION
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Regression models how the AVERAGE value of an outcome y changes with
one or more predictors x. Linear regression assumes the average changes
in a straight-line (linear) way and finds the line (or plane) that
fits the data best in the least-squares sense. It is the workhorse of
statistics and the foundation of many machine learning methods.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
40.1 SIMPLE LINEAR REGRESSION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F40.1  THE SIMPLE LINEAR REGRESSION MODEL
A. NAME: Simple linear regression model (one predictor).
B. FORMULA
     Population model:   y_i = beta_0 + beta_1 x_i + eps_i,   i = 1..n
        with errors eps_i independent, E[eps_i] = 0, Var(eps_i) = sigma^2
        (and, for exact small-sample inference, eps_i ~ N(0, sigma^2)).
     Fitted (regression) line:  y_hat = b_0 + b_1 x
C. SYMBOLS
   y_i      response (dependent variable, outcome, target).
   x_i      predictor (independent variable, feature, regressor).
   beta_0   INTERCEPT (population): mean of y when x = 0.
   beta_1   SLOPE (population): change in the mean of y per 1-unit increase
            in x.
   eps_i    error term: everything else affecting y (unmodeled factors,
            noise).
   b_0, b_1 ESTIMATES of beta_0, beta_1 from data (also written beta_hat).
   y_hat_i  fitted value (prediction) for observation i.
D. CONCEPT: the model says E[y | x] = beta_0 + beta_1 x: the conditional mean is a
   straight line; individual y values scatter around it with constant
   spread sigma.
E. CONDITIONS: see 40.7 (assumptions). Regression describes the average
   relationship; it does not by itself say that x causes y (40.9).
G. EXAMPLE: the study-hours data are modeled as score = beta_0 + beta_1
   hours + eps; the fit gives 46.86 + 4.476 hours (next formula).
H. INTERPRETATION: the line gives the mean of y for each x; the residual
   scatter is sigma.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: treating the error eps and the residual e as the
   same thing; forgetting that the model is about the conditional mean.
J. RELATED: OLS estimates (F40.2), multiple regression (F40.6), MLE with
   Gaussian errors (F38.3).

F40.2  LEAST SQUARES ESTIMATES FOR SIMPLE REGRESSION
A. NAME: Ordinary least squares (OLS) estimates.
B. FORMULAS
     Choose b_0, b_1 to minimize  SSE = sum (y_i - b_0 - b_1 x_i)^2.
     b_1 = sum (x_i - x_bar)(y_i - y_bar) / sum (x_i - x_bar)^2 = S_xy/S_xx
         = r * s_y / s_x
     b_0 = y_bar - b_1 x_bar
     Residual standard error (estimate of sigma):
        sigma_hat = s_e = sqrt( SSE/(n - 2) ),   MSE = SSE/(n - 2).
C. SYMBOLS: S_xx = sum (x_i - x_bar)^2; S_xy = sum (x_i - x_bar)(y_i - y_bar);
   r the correlation (Module 32); s_x, s_y sample SDs; n - 2 degrees of
   freedom (two parameters estimated).
D. CONCEPT: the line that makes the total squared vertical distance from the
   points as small as possible. Slope = correlation rescaled by the ratio
   of spreads, so a positive correlation gives a positive slope. The
   line always passes through the center of the data (x_bar, y_bar).
E. CONDITIONS: needs variation in x (S_xx > 0); outliers and leverage points
   strongly affect it.
F. DERIVATION (calculus, Module 12): SSE(b_0, b_1) is a quadratic bowl.
     dSSE/db_0 = -2 sum (y_i - b_0 - b_1 x_i) = 0  ->  sum e_i = 0
        ->  b_0 = y_bar - b_1 x_bar.
     dSSE/db_1 = -2 sum x_i (y_i - b_0 - b_1 x_i) = 0  ->  sum x_i e_i = 0.
   Substituting b_0 into the second equation and solving gives b_1 = S_xy/S_xx.
   These are the NORMAL EQUATIONS (matrix form in 40.2).
G. WORKED EXAMPLE (hours studied vs exam score)
   x (hours): 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8
   y (score): 52, 55, 61, 64, 70, 72, 79, 83
   x_bar = 4.5, y_bar = 67. S_xx = 42, S_xy = 188.
   b_1 = 188/42 = 4.476;  b_0 = 67 - 4.476 * 4.5 = 46.857.
   Fitted line: score = 46.86 + 4.476 * hours.
   Fitted values: 51.33, 55.81, 60.29, 64.76, 69.24, 73.71, 78.19, 82.67.
   Residuals (y - y_hat): 0.667, -0.810, 0.714, -0.762, 0.762, -1.714, 0.810,
   0.333. They sum to 0 and are orthogonal to x (sum x e = 0).
   SSE = 6.476; SST = sum (y - 67)^2 = 848; SSR = SST - SSE = 841.52.
   Residual standard error = sqrt(6.476/6) = 1.039 points.
H. INTERPRETATION
   Slope: each additional hour of study is associated with an average
   increase of about 4.5 points in the score (within the observed range 1-8
   hours).
   Intercept: predicted score at 0 hours is 46.9. (Meaningful only if
   x = 0 is within or near the data; otherwise it is just a positioning
   constant: e.g. regressing weight on height gives an "intercept" for
   height 0 which is nonsense. Centering x makes the intercept the
   prediction at the mean.)
   Residual sd 1.04: typical prediction errors are about 1 point.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: interpreting the slope as causal; extrapolating beyond
   the data (see 40.2); interpreting the intercept literally; mixing up y
   on x with x on y (the two regression lines are different unless r = +/-
   1: b_1(y on x) = r s_y/s_x but b_1(x on y) = r s_x/s_y); reversing the
   order of x and y in S_xy/S_xx.
J. RELATED: correlation r (F32.2): r^2 = R^2 here; least squares (F10.5).

F40.3  GOODNESS OF FIT: SST, SSR, SSE AND R^2
B. SST = sum (y_i - y_bar)^2   (total variation)
   SSR = sum (y_hat_i - y_bar)^2 (variation explained by the regression)
   SSE = sum (y_i - y_hat_i)^2   (unexplained variation)
   SST = SSR + SSE   (holds for OLS with an intercept)
   R^2 = SSR/SST = 1 - SSE/SST  (coefficient of determination)
   Simple regression: R^2 = r^2.
   Multiple regression: ADJUSTED R^2 = 1 - (1 - R^2)(n - 1)/(n - p - 1),
      with p predictors.
C. SYMBOLS: y_i the observations; y_bar their mean; y_hat_i the fitted
   values; p the number of predictors.
D. CONCEPT: R^2 is the proportion of the variance of y accounted for by the
   model (relative to predicting y_bar always). Adding any predictor never
   decreases R^2, so adjusted R^2 penalizes needless predictors.
E. CONDITIONS: SST = SSR + SSE requires OLS with an intercept; R^2 can
   be negative for predictions on new data.
G. EXAMPLE: R^2 = 841.52/848 = 0.9924: the model explains 99.2% of the
   variation in scores (r = 0.9962).
H. INTERPRETATION: R^2 = 0.9924 means that 99.2% of the variance of y is
   explained by the model.
I. MISTAKES: "high R^2 means a correct/causal model" (no); "low R^2 means
   the model is useless" (in noisy social data R^2 = 0.1 can still be
   informative; the slope may be well determined); comparing R^2 across
   different response transformations; using training R^2 as evidence of
   predictive performance (use held-out data, Module 46); forgetting that
   R^2 can be negative on test data.
J. RELATED: correlation (F32.2), adjusted R^2, F-test, test-set R^2
   (F46.6).

F40.4  INFERENCE FOR THE SLOPE AND INTERCEPT
B. SE(b_1) = sigma_hat / sqrt(S_xx);
   SE(b_0) = sigma_hat sqrt( 1/n + x_bar^2/S_xx );
   t-statistic for H0: beta_j = 0:  t = b_j / SE(b_j) ~ t_(n - 2) under H0;
   (1 - alpha) CI: b_j +/- t_(alpha/2, n - 2) SE(b_j).
   Overall F-test (simple regression): F = t^2 = MSR/MSE with (1, n - 2) df.
C. SYMBOLS: sigma_hat the residual standard error; SE the standard error of
   the estimate (F37.8).
D. CONCEPT: the slope estimate varies from sample to sample; its
   standard error shrinks when there is more data, more spread in x
