   MOMENTUM: v <- mu v + grad; theta <- theta - eta v: accumulates a running
      direction, damping zig-zags. ADAPTIVE METHODS (AdaGrad, RMSProp,
      Adam): per-parameter step sizes from gradient statistics.
   LEARNING-RATE SCHEDULES: step decay, exponential decay eta_0 e^(-k t),
      1/t decay, cosine, warm-up; BACKTRACKING LINE SEARCH (Armijo): shrink
      eta until f decreases enough.
CONVERGENCE SUMMARY
   Strongly convex, smooth f, suitable eta: GD converges LINEARLY
   (geometrically) with rate depending on kappa. Convex, not strongly:
   sublinear O(1/t). Non-convex: converges to stationary points only.
   SGD with constant eta converges to a NEIGHBOURHOOD of the optimum.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
43.4 NEWTON-RAPHSON METHOD
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F43.3  NEWTON-RAPHSON FOR ROOT FINDING (ONE VARIABLE)
A. NAME: Newton-Raphson method.
B. FORMULA:   x_(n+1) = x_n - f(x_n)/f'(x_n)    (solves f(x) = 0)
C. SYMBOLS: x_n current guess; f'(x_n) the slope there.
D. CONCEPT: replace f by its tangent line at x_n (F3.2) and jump to where the
   tangent crosses the x-axis.
E. CONDITIONS: f'(x_n) != 0; start reasonably close to the root; convergence
   is QUADRATIC near a simple root (the number of correct digits roughly
   doubles each step). Can diverge, cycle, or jump to another root
   from a poor start.
G. EXAMPLE: solve x^2 - 2 = 0 (sqrt 2): x_(n+1) = x_n - (x_n^2 - 2)/(2 x_n)
   = (x_n + 2/x_n)/2. From x_0 = 1: 1.5, 1.416667, 1.4142157, 1.41421356
   (true sqrt 2 = 1.41421356237): errors 0.086, 0.0025, 2 x 10^-6, 2 x
   10^-12: digits double. (Same recursion as the sequence in F5.1.)
H. INTERPRETATION: the tangent-line jump converges very fast near the
   root.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: starting near a point with f' = 0; expecting
   convergence from any start.
J. RELATED: bisection and IVT (F2.7), Newton for optimization (F43.4),
   the sequence in F5.1.

F43.4  NEWTON'S METHOD FOR OPTIMIZATION (MULTIVARIATE)
A. NAME: Newton's method (Newton-Raphson applied to the gradient).
B. FORMULA
     theta_(t+1) = theta_t - H(theta_t)^(-1) grad f(theta_t)
   implemented by SOLVING  H d = -grad f  for the step d (never by forming
   the inverse), then theta <- theta + d. In one variable:
   x_(n+1) = x_n - f'(x_n)/f''(x_n) (root finding applied to f' = 0).
C. SYMBOLS: H the Hessian matrix of second partial derivatives (F12.6).
D. CONCEPT: approximate f by its SECOND-order Taylor model
   q(d) = f + g^T d + (1/2) d^T H d and jump to that quadratic's minimizer
   d = -H^(-1) g. The Hessian supplies curvature: flat directions take
   LONG steps, steep directions SHORT steps, so the step size adapts
   automatically (no learning rate; well-conditioned in the right
   coordinates).
E. CONDITIONS: needs the Hessian (expensive: d^2 entries, O(d^3) to solve);
   the Hessian must be positive definite near the solution, otherwise
   the step may point uphill or toward a saddle (modify H: add lambda I,
   trust regions, line search). Pure Newton may diverge far from the
   optimum. Local convergence is quadratic.
   Practical variants: QUASI-NEWTON (BFGS, L-BFGS build a Hessian
   approximation from gradients), Gauss-Newton and Levenberg-Marquardt
   (nonlinear least squares), IRLS (logistic regression).
G. EXAMPLES
   Quadratic in two variables: f = x^2 + 3 y^2 from (2, 1): g = (4, 6);
      H = diag(2, 6); step d = -H^(-1) g = -(2, 1): new point (0, 0)
      exactly in ONE step (Newton solves quadratics in one step; GD needed
      many).
   One variable: minimize f(x) = x - ln x (x > 0): f' = 1 - 1/x, f'' = 1/x^2.
      Newton: x_(n+1) = x - (1 - 1/x) x^2 = 2x - x^2. From x_0 = 0.5:
      0.75, 0.9375, 0.99609, 0.9999847, 0.99999999977. The distance to the
      minimizer x = 1 is 0.25, 0.0625, 0.0039, 1.5 x 10^-5, 2.3 x 10^-10:
      each error is the SQUARE of the previous (quadratic convergence).
   Logistic regression: Newton/IRLS typically converges in 5-10 iterations.
H. INTERPRETATION: Newton uses curvature; GD uses only slope.
I. MISTAKES: inverting H explicitly; applying Newton where H is not
   positive definite (a maximum or saddle attracts it); divide-by-zero
   when f'' = 0; using it for huge parameter counts (neural nets).
J. RELATED: gradient descent (F43.2), BFGS and L-BFGS, IRLS for logistic
   regression.

COMPARISON TABLE: GRADIENT DESCENT vs NEWTON'S METHOD
  Aspect                Gradient descent               Newton's method
  Information used      gradient (first order)         gradient + Hessian (second order)
  Update                theta - eta g                  theta - H^(-1) g
  Step size             needs tuned eta                determined by curvature
  Cost per iteration    O(d) (one gradient)            O(d^2) memory, O(d^3) solve
  Iterations needed     many (linear rate, depends     few (quadratic rate near solution)
                        on kappa)
  Convergence           global for small eta (convex)  local; may diverge from poor start
  Sensitive to scale    yes (condition number)         invariant to linear rescaling
  Non-convex behavior   moves downhill always          may move toward saddles/maxima
  Large-scale use       standard (SGD, Adam)           limited; use L-BFGS / quasi-Newton
  Example               training neural nets           logistic regression (IRLS),
                                                       small/medium smooth problems

------------------------------------------------------------------------
43.5 NELDER-MEAD METHOD
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F43.5  NELDER-MEAD (DOWNHILL SIMPLEX)
A. NAME: Nelder-Mead simplex method.
B. IDEA AND ALGORITHM: a DERIVATIVE-FREE method. In n dimensions keep a SIMPLEX
   of n + 1 points (a triangle in 2D, tetrahedron in 3D). Each iteration:
     1. ORDER the vertices by f: best x_1, ..., worst x_(n+1).
     2. Compute the CENTROID c of all vertices except the worst.
     3. REFLECT the worst through the centroid: x_r = c + alpha (c - x_worst)
        (standard alpha = 1).
     4. If x_r is better than the best: try EXPANSION x_e = c + gamma (x_r - c)
        (gamma = 2); keep the better of x_e and x_r.
        If x_r is between the best and the second-worst: ACCEPT it.
        If x_r is worse than the second-worst: CONTRACT toward the centroid
        (x_c = c + rho (x_worst - c) or c + rho (x_r - c), rho = 0.5); accept
        if better than the worst.
     5. If contraction fails: SHRINK all vertices toward the best:
        x_i <- x_1 + sigma (x_i - x_1) (sigma = 0.5).
     6. Stop when the simplex is small and f values are close.
C. SYMBOLS: alpha, gamma, rho, sigma the reflection, expansion, contraction and
   shrink coefficients.
D. CONCEPT: the simplex crawls and reshapes itself downhill like an amoeba,
   using only function VALUES (rankings), never derivatives.
E. CONDITIONS: use for noisy, non-differentiable or "black-box" objectives
   with few parameters (about 2-10). It may stall at non-stationary points,
   has no general convergence guarantee, and becomes very slow as the
   dimension grows. Sensitive to the initial simplex and to scaling.
G. EXAMPLE (one iteration): f(x, y) = x^2 + y^2, simplex A = (1, 1) with f = 2,
   B = (2, 1) with f = 5, C = (1, 2) with f = 5. Worst = C (tie with B;
   take C). Centroid of A and B: c = (1.5, 1). Reflection:
   x_r = c + (c - C) = (2, 0), f = 4. Since 2 <= 4 < 5 (between the best and
   the second-worst), ACCEPT: new simplex A (f = 2), (2, 0) (f = 4),
   B (f = 5): the worst value dropped from 5 to 4.
   In practice: scipy.optimize.minimize(f, x0, method="Nelder-Mead"). For
   Rosenbrock's function (1 - x)^2 + 100 (y - x^2)^2 from (-1.2, 1) it
   reaches (1.00002, 1.00004) with f about 8e-10 in 85 iterations (159
   function evaluations); BFGS (with gradients by finite differences) reaches
   (0.99999, 0.99999) with f about 5e-11 in 31 iterations (114
   evaluations).
H. INTERPRETATION: tuning model hyperparameters with a black-box objective
   (validation score) is a derivative-free problem (Module 47), though
   specialized methods are used there.
I. MISTAKES: using it for hundreds of parameters; trusting convergence
   without restarts; poor initial simplex scale; applying it where
   gradients are cheap (use gradient methods).
J. RELATED: gradient methods (F43.2), hyperparameter search (section
   47.2).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
43.6 LOCAL vs GLOBAL MINIMA
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   GLOBAL minimum: the lowest value of f over the whole feasible set.
   LOCAL minimum: lowest in a neighbourhood only.
   SADDLE POINT: gradient zero, but the Hessian is indefinite (a minimum in
   some directions, a maximum in others). PLATEAU: a flat region with
   tiny gradient (slow progress).
   Gradient-based methods can only guarantee reaching a STATIONARY point.
WORKED EXAMPLE (non-convex): f(x) = x^4 - 3x^2 + x, f'(x) = 4x^3 - 6x + 1.
   Stationary points (roots of f'): x = -1.3008 (f = -3.514, GLOBAL minimum),
   x = 0.1699 (f = 0.084, a local MAXIMUM) and x = 1.1309 (f = -1.070,
   a LOCAL minimum, not global).
   Gradient descent with eta = 0.01 (500 iterations): from x_0 = 2 it converges to
   1.1309 (stuck in the local minimum, f = -1.07); from x_0 = -2 it converges
   to -1.3008 (the global minimum, f = -3.51). THE ANSWER DEPENDS ON THE
   START.
SADDLE EXAMPLE: f(x, y) = x^2 - y^2: gradient (2x, -2y) vanishes at the
   origin; Hessian diag(2, -2) indefinite. GD (eta = 0.1) starting at
   (1, 0) converges to (0, 0) (the saddle) because y stays exactly 0; a tiny
   perturbation (1, 10^-6) escapes: y grows by 1.2 per step (and f is
   unbounded below). In high dimensions saddles are far more common than
   local minima; SGD noise and momentum help escape them.
STRATEGIES FOR NON-CONVEX PROBLEMS: multiple random restarts and keep the
   best; sensible initialization; stochastic noise (SGD); momentum/adaptive
   methods; simulated annealing; evolutionary/genetic algorithms;
   convex relaxations; regularization to smooth the landscape. For
   neural networks many local minima give similarly good test
   performance, so exact global optimality is rarely needed.
GUARANTEES: convex + differentiable => any stationary point is a global
   minimum; strictly convex => unique. Linear regression and logistic
   regression objectives are convex; k-means, mixture models and deep
   networks are not.
CHECKS: plot loss versus iteration; try several initializations; compare
the final value across runs.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
43.7 NUMERICAL OPTIMIZATION IN PRACTICE; CONVERGENCE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
CONVERGENCE CRITERIA: |grad f| < tol_g; |theta_(t+1) - theta_t| < tol_x;
   |f_t - f_(t+1)| < tol_f (absolute or relative); maximum iterations.
   Always inspect WHY the optimizer stopped (result.message, result.success).
PRACTICAL ISSUES: scaling/conditioning (standardize features, Module 42);
   floating-point error; ill-posed problems (collinearity: flat valleys,
   Module 40.6); choice of starting point; noisy gradients; constraints
   (bounds, equality/inequality): use projected GD, penalty/barrier
   methods, Lagrange multipliers (F12.8), or solvers such as L-BFGS-B, SLSQP.
SCIPY QUICK REFERENCE (verified on Rosenbrock)
   from scipy import optimize
   rosen = lambda p: (1 - p[0])**2 + 100*(p[1] - p[0]**2)**2
   res = optimize.minimize(rosen, x0=[-1.2, 1], method="BFGS")
   res.x -> [0.99999328, 0.99998655]; res.fun -> 4.5e-11; res.nit -> 31;
   res.success, res.message
   optimize.minimize(..., jac=grad_function)   # supply exact gradients
   methods: "BFGS", "L-BFGS-B" (large, bounded), "CG", "Newton-CG",
            "Nelder-Mead", "Powell" (derivative-free), "SLSQP"/"trust-constr"
            (constraints).
   optimize.minimize_scalar(f, bounds=(a, b), method="bounded")  # 1D
   optimize.root(f, x0) / brentq (root finding); optimize.curve_fit and
   least_squares (nonlinear regression).
SIMPLE GD IN PYTHON
   def gradient_descent(grad, theta0, eta=0.1, tol=1e-8, max_iter=10000):
       theta = np.array(theta0, dtype=float)
       for t in range(max_iter):
           g = grad(theta)
           if np.linalg.norm(g) < tol: break
           theta = theta - eta * g
       return theta, t
   X = np.array([[1, 0], [1, 1], [1, 2.]]); y = np.array([1, 2, 2.])
   grad = lambda b: -(X.T @ (y - X @ b)) / len(y)
   gradient_descent(grad, [0, 0])  ->  beta about [1.16667, 0.5]

------------------------------------------------------------------------
43.8 OPTIMIZATION IN REGRESSION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
LINEAR REGRESSION (convex quadratic objective)
   Objective: L(beta) = (1/(2n)) |y - X beta|^2; gradient -(1/n) X^T (y - X
   beta); Hessian (1/n) X^T X (PSD).
   Solutions: (1) closed form via normal equations beta = (X^T X)^(-1) X^T y;
   computed in practice by QR or SVD (Module 10), cost O(n p^2); (2) GD/SGD,
   cost O(n p) per full-gradient step, preferable when p or n is huge or
   data arrive in streams; (3) Newton converges in ONE step (quadratic
   objective).
RIDGE REGRESSION: minimize |y - X beta|^2 + lambda |beta|^2: gradient adds
   2 lambda beta; closed form (X^T X + lambda I)^(-1) X^T y (Modules 9, 47): the
   added lambda I removes singularity and improves conditioning (every
   eigenvalue increases by lambda, so kappa shrinks).
LASSO: adds lambda |beta|_1: not differentiable at 0; solved by coordinate
   descent or proximal/sub-gradient methods; yields exact zeros (feature
   selection).
LOGISTIC REGRESSION: minimize the average negative Bernoulli log-likelihood
   L(beta) = -(1/n) sum [y_i ln p_i + (1 - y_i) ln(1 - p_i)], p_i =
   sigma(x_i^T beta), sigma(z) = 1/(1 + e^(-z)).
   Gradient: (1/n) X^T (p - y); Hessian: (1/n) X^T W X with W = diag(p_i (1 -
   p_i)) (PSD: a CONVEX problem, no local minima). No closed form:
   GD/Newton (IRLS)/L-BFGS.
   WORKED EXAMPLE: x = 0, 1, 2, 3 with y = 0, 0, 1, 1; X = [1 x]. At beta =
   (0, 0) every p_i = 0.5; residuals p - y = (0.5, 0.5, -0.5, -0.5);
   gradient = (1/4) X^T (p - y) = (1/4)(0, 0.5 - 1 - 1.5) = (0, -0.5); loss
   = ln 2 = 0.6931. One GD step with eta = 0.1: beta = (0, 0.05): the slope
   becomes positive (larger x -> more likely class 1). 
   !! NOTE !! This particular data set is PERFECTLY SEPARABLE (x <= 1 are all
   class 0; x >= 2 all class 1), so the unpenalized MLE does not exist: the
   coefficients grow without bound while the loss tends to 0. Regularization
   (an L2 penalty) or early stopping fixes this (Module 47). Separation is a
   frequent cause of "non-convergence" warnings.
NEURAL NETWORKS: objectives are non-convex; trained by mini-batch SGD with
   gradients from back-propagation (the chain rule, F12.5).
K-MEANS: alternating minimization of within-cluster squared distance
   (converges to a local minimum; use several random starts).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: nearly every training procedure is "choose an objective,
choose an optimizer, choose a learning-rate schedule, monitor
convergence". Understanding curvature (Hessian eigenvalues, condition
number) explains why feature scaling and regularization speed
training.


========================================================================
D3. DECISION TREES
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 44: TREE-BASED LEARNING
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
A decision tree plays "twenty questions" with the data. It asks a series
of simple yes/no questions about the features ("Is x <= 4.5?"), each
chosen to separate the outcomes as cleanly as possible, until the
groups at the bottom are (nearly) pure. To predict for a new case, you
answer the questions about it and read off the answer at the end of the
path. The mathematical heart of tree learning is the measurement of
how "mixed" a group is (impurity) and how much a question reduces it.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
44.1 STRUCTURE: ROOT, NODES, LEAVES, DECISION RULES
------------------------------------------------------------------------
TERMINOLOGY (trees, Module 18/19 applied to learning)
   ROOT NODE: the top node; contains ALL training data.
   INTERNAL (DECISION) NODE: a test on one feature, e.g. x_j <= t (numeric)
      or x_j in {A, B} (categorical); sends each case to the left or right
      child (binary splits are standard in CART).
   BRANCH: outcome of a test. LEAF (TERMINAL) NODE: no further split; holds the
      prediction. DEPTH: number of edges from the root to the deepest leaf.
   SPLIT: the chosen (feature, threshold) pair.
GEOMETRY: each split cuts the feature space with an axis-parallel boundary
   (x_j = t). A tree therefore partitions the space into rectangles
   ("boxes"), one per leaf, and predicts a CONSTANT in each: a
   piecewise-constant function.
DECISION RULES: each root-to-leaf path is an IF-THEN rule: a conjunction (AND,
   Module 23) of the tests along the path.
   Example: IF x <= 4.5 THEN class = 1 (4 of 4 training cases positive);
            IF x > 4.5 THEN class = 0 (4 negative, 2 positive).
   Tree = a set of mutually exclusive, exhaustive rules, hence its
   interpretability.
CLASSIFICATION TREE: leaf predicts a class; REGRESSION TREE: leaf predicts a
number. Same structure, different leaf rule and splitting criterion.
   COMPARISON: classification vs regression
     Target                   categorical          numeric
     Leaf prediction          majority class       mean of the y values in the leaf
     Leaf output (soft)       class proportions    (mean, optionally the variance)
     Impurity measure         Gini / entropy       variance (SSE/MSE)
     Evaluation               accuracy, log-loss   RMSE, MAE

------------------------------------------------------------------------
44.2 CLASS PROPORTIONS AND TREE PREDICTIONS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F44.1  CLASS PROPORTIONS AND PREDICTION
B. For a node containing n observations of which n_k belong to class k
   (k = 1..K):
        p_k = n_k / n          (class proportion, a probability estimate)
   Classification prediction: the MAJORITY class, argmax_k p_k.
   Predicted probabilities: P(class k | x in this leaf) = p_k.
   Regression prediction: y_hat = mean of the y_i in the leaf.
C. SYMBOLS: n_k count of class k in the node; p_k = n_k/n; sum_k p_k = 1.
D. CONCEPT: the tree treats every case that falls in the same leaf alike, and
   the best constant guess for that group is the group's typical value.
E. CONDITIONS: leaves with few cases give unreliable proportions (a leaf
   with 1 observation says "probability 1.0"): regularize (min samples
   per leaf) or smooth (Laplace correction (n_k + 1)/(n + K)). Predicted
   probabilities from a single deep tree are poorly calibrated.
G. EXAMPLE (the toy data used below: x = 1..10; labels 1,1,1,1,0,1,0,0,0,1;
   6 positives, 4 negatives): after the split x <= 4.5, the left leaf has 4
   positives, 0 negatives: p(1) = 1.0 -> predict 1. The right leaf has 2
   positives and 4 negatives: p(1) = 1/3 -> predict class 0 with
   probability 2/3 for class 0.
   Prediction for x = 3: path root -> left leaf -> class 1 (p = 1.0). For
   x = 7: path root -> right leaf -> class 0 (p(class 0) = 0.667).
H. INTERPRETATION: the leaf's majority class is the best guess; the
   proportions express the uncertainty.
I. MISTAKES: reading leaf proportions as true probabilities when leaves are
   tiny; forgetting that regression trees cannot extrapolate beyond the
   range of leaf means (the prediction never exceeds the training y-range).
J. RELATED: Gini and entropy (F44.2, F44.3), probability calibration
   (F46.5).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
44.3 SPLITTING CRITERIA: GINI IMPURITY, ENTROPY, INFORMATION GAIN
------------------------------------------------------------------------
BASIC IDEA: a node is PURE if all its cases share one class. An impurity
measure is 0 for a pure node and largest for a perfectly mixed node. The
best split is the one that produces the purest children (on average).

F44.2  GINI IMPURITY
A. NAME: Gini impurity (Gini index).
B. FORMULA:   G = 1 - sum[k=1..K] p_k^2 = sum[k] p_k (1 - p_k)
   Range: 0 (pure) to 1 - 1/K (all classes equally likely).
   Two classes: G = 2 p (1 - p), maximum 0.5 at p = 0.5.
C. SYMBOLS: p_k class proportions in the node (F44.1); K number of classes.
D. CONCEPT: the probability that two cases drawn at random (with replacement)
   from the node belong to DIFFERENT classes; equivalently, the error rate
   if each case were labeled randomly according to the node's class
   distribution. Lower = purer.
E. CONDITIONS: needs class proportions; for K classes the maximum is 1 - 1/K.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: node with 6 positives and 4 negatives: p = (0.6, 0.4):
      G = 1 - (0.36 + 0.16) = 0.48 (= 2 * 0.6 * 0.4).
   Pure node (4, 0): G = 1 - 1 = 0. Evenly mixed (5, 5): G = 0.5.
   Three classes (0.5, 0.3, 0.2): G = 1 - (0.25 + 0.09 + 0.04) = 0.62
      (maximum 2/3 = 0.667).
H. INTERPRETATION: G = 0.48 for a 60/40 node says there is a 48% chance
   that two randomly drawn cases differ in class.
I. MISTAKES: forgetting the "1 -"; using counts instead of proportions.
J. RELATED: entropy (F44.3), information gain (F44.4), misclassification
   error.

F44.3  ENTROPY
A. NAME: Shannon entropy of a node.
B. FORMULA:   H = - sum[k=1..K] p_k log2(p_k),   with the convention 0 log 0 = 0.
   Units: BITS with log base 2 (natural log gives nats; the choice only
   rescales all values by a constant and does not change the best split).
   Range: 0 (pure) to log2 K. Two classes: H = -p log2 p - (1 - p) log2(1 - p),
   maximum 1 bit at p = 0.5.
C. SYMBOLS: p_k class proportions; log2 the base-2 logarithm (F1.12).
D. CONCEPT: the average number of yes/no questions (bits) needed to identify
   the class of a randomly drawn case, i.e. the expected "surprise" or
   uncertainty. Highly uncertain nodes have high entropy. (Information
   theory; cross-entropy loss in Module 48 is a relative.)
E. CONDITIONS: p_k = 0 terms contribute 0.
G. EXAMPLES
   Simple: (0.6, 0.4): H = -0.6 log2 0.6 - 0.4 log2 0.4 = 0.6(0.737) + 0.4(1.322)
      = 0.442 + 0.529 = 0.971 bits.
   (0.8, 0.2): H = 0.722 bits. (1, 0): H = 0. (0.5, 0.5): H = 1.
   Three classes (0.5, 0.3, 0.2): H = 1.485 bits (maximum log2 3 = 1.585).
   Table (two classes): p = 0.1: Gini 0.18, entropy 0.469; p = 0.2: 0.32,
      0.722; p = 0.3: 0.42, 0.881; p = 0.4: 0.48, 0.971; p = 0.5: 0.5, 1.0.
      Both rise and fall together; entropy is a bit more "curved".
H. INTERPRETATION: 0.971 bits means that almost one full yes/no question
   of uncertainty remains.
I. MISTAKES: using log base 10 inconsistently; forgetting that log2 of a
   fraction is negative (hence the leading minus); computing 0 * log 0
   as NaN.
J. RELATED: Gini (F44.2), cross-entropy loss (F46.5, Appendix D).

F44.4  INFORMATION GAIN (IMPURITY DECREASE)
A. NAME: Information gain (for entropy) / impurity decrease (for Gini).
B. FORMULA: for a split of a parent node with n cases into children j = 1..m
   with n_j cases each:
        Gain = Impurity(parent) - sum[j] (n_j/n) Impurity(child_j)
   (impurity = entropy H gives information gain; impurity = Gini gives the
   Gini decrease). The second term is the WEIGHTED average impurity of the
   children.
C. SYMBOLS: n_j/n the share of cases going to child j (the weight).
D. CONCEPT: how much uncertainty about the class is removed by learning the
   answer to the question. The weights prevent a tiny pure child from
   making a split look better than it is.
E. CONDITIONS: Gain >= 0 for concave impurities. Gain favours splits
   producing many tiny children (e.g. an ID column): use binary splits,
   minimum leaf sizes, or the gain ratio (C4.5) to counter this.
G. WORKED EXAMPLE (the same data used throughout): x = 1..10, labels
   1,1,1,1,0,1,0,0,0,1 (6 positive, 4 negative):
     Parent: Gini = 0.48; entropy = 0.9710.
   Candidate threshold x <= 4.5 (left = x 1-4: 4 pos, 0 neg; right = x 5-10: 2 pos,
   4 neg):
     Gini left 0; Gini right = 1 - (1/9 + 4/9) = 0.4444.
     Weighted Gini = 0.4 * 0 + 0.6 * 0.4444 = 0.2667; Gini gain =
        0.48 - 0.2667 = 0.2133.
     Entropy right = -(1/3) log2(1/3) - (2/3) log2(2/3) = 0.9183;
     weighted = 0.6 * 0.9183 = 0.5510; Information gain = 0.9710 - 0.5510 =
        0.4200 bits.
   Candidate threshold x <= 5.5 (left 5 cases: 4 pos, 1 neg; right 5 cases: 2 pos,
   3 neg):
     Gini left = 1 - (0.64 + 0.04) = 0.32; right = 1 - (0.16 + 0.36) = 0.48;
     weighted 0.4; Gini gain 0.08.
     Entropy left 0.7219, right 0.9710; weighted 0.8464; information gain
     0.1245 bits.
   All candidate thresholds (midpoints between consecutive x values):
     threshold   left (pos/neg)  right (pos/neg)  weighted Gini  Gini gain  Info. gain
     1.5         1 (1/0)         9 (5/4)          0.4444         0.0356     0.0790
     2.5         2 (2/0)         8 (4/4)          0.4000         0.0800     0.1710
     3.5         3 (3/0)         7 (3/4)          0.3429         0.1371     0.2813
     4.5         4 (4/0)         6 (2/4)          0.2667         0.2133     0.4200
     5.5         5 (4/1)         5 (2/3)          0.4000         0.0800     0.1245
     6.5         6 (5/1)         4 (1/3)          0.3167         0.1633     0.2564
     7.5         7 (5/2)         3 (1/2)          0.4190         0.0610     0.0913
     8.5         8 (5/3)         2 (1/1)          0.4750         0.0050     0.0074
     9.5         9 (5/4)         1 (1/0)          0.4444         0.0356     0.0790
   Best split under BOTH criteria: x <= 4.5. (scikit-learn's
   DecisionTreeClassifier with max_depth=1 returns exactly this tree:
   threshold 4.50, leaves with class counts [0, 4] and [4, 2].)
H. INTERPRETATION: the first question removes 0.42 of the 0.97 bits of
   uncertainty (43%). Gains are used to rank splits; the sum of gains over
   all splits using a feature gives "impurity-based feature importance".
I. COMMON MISTAKES: unweighted averaging of child impurities; thinking
   Gini and entropy give very different trees (they usually agree);
   computing gain relative to the wrong parent; considering thresholds
   at data values instead of midpoints (equivalent in effect but check
   the convention).
J. RELATED: entropy and KL divergence (Appendix D); log-loss (Module 48).

COMPARISON TABLE: GINI vs ENTROPY
  Aspect            Gini impurity               Entropy / information gain
  Formula           1 - sum p_k^2               -sum p_k log2 p_k
  Maximum (K = 2)   0.5                         1 bit
  Computation       cheaper (no logs)           slightly slower
  Behavior          similar; tends to isolate   tends to produce slightly more
                    the most frequent class     balanced splits
  Default           scikit-learn "gini"         criterion="entropy" or "log_loss"
  Practical effect  usually negligible difference in accuracy
  Misclassification error 1 - max p_k is less sensitive: used for pruning, not for growing.

F44.5  SPLITTING CRITERION FOR REGRESSION TREES
B. For a node with responses y_i (i in node), impurity = sum of squared
   deviations from the node mean: SSE = sum (y_i - y_bar)^2 (or MSE =
   SSE/n, the variance). Choose the split (j, t) minimizing
        SSE_left + SSE_right,   equivalently maximizing the variance (SSE)
        reduction  SSE_parent - SSE_left - SSE_right.
   Leaf prediction: y_bar of the leaf.
C. SYMBOLS: y_i the responses in the node; y_bar the node mean; SSE the
   sum of squared deviations.
D. CONCEPT: the best constant for a group (squared error) is its mean (F29.1);
   splits are chosen to make each group's responses as similar as possible.
E. CONDITIONS: numeric target; outliers dominate squared error (use
   absolute-error criteria for robustness).
G. EXAMPLE: x = 1..6, y = 1.0, 1.2, 0.9, 3.1, 3.0, 2.9. Parent mean 2.0167,
   SST = 5.868.
     threshold 1.5: SSE = 4.628   2.5: 3.348   3.5: 0.0667   4.5: 3.255   5.5:
     4.932.
   Best split x <= 3.5: left mean 1.0333, right mean 3.0, total SSE 0.0667
   (reduction 5.80 of 5.87: 99%). Tree: IF x <= 3.5 THEN 1.03 ELSE 3.00 (the
   scikit-learn tree matches).
H. INTERPRETATION: the best split separates low and high responses so
   that each leaf is homogeneous.
I. MISTAKES: expecting a smooth fit (steps only); extrapolation.
J. RELATED: variance (F31.3), classification criteria (F44.2 to F44.4),
   OLS (F40.2).

THE GREEDY ALGORITHM (CART: Classification And Regression Trees)
   1. Start with all data in the root.
   2. For each feature j and each candidate threshold t (midpoints between
      consecutive sorted distinct values; for categorical features subsets
      or one-vs-rest), compute the weighted child impurity.
   3. Choose the split with the largest gain; create two child nodes.
   4. Recurse on each child until a STOPPING RULE holds: node is pure; node
      has fewer than min_samples_split cases; maximum depth; no split with
      enough gain; minimum leaf size would be violated.
   5. Assign leaf predictions (majority class or mean).
   GREEDY: each split is the best LOCAL choice, with no look-ahead (finding
   the globally optimal tree is NP-hard); an XOR-like pattern (Module 48) may
   show no gain at the first split although two splits would classify
   perfectly.
   COST: sorting each feature at each node gives roughly O(p n log n) work per
   tree level, so growing a tree costs about O(depth x p n log n) (balanced
   depth about log n); prediction is O(depth) per case: very fast.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
44.4 OVERFITTING AND PRUNING
------------------------------------------------------------------------
THE PROBLEM: with no limits a tree keeps splitting until every leaf is pure,
so it memorizes the training data (training accuracy 100%), including the
noise: HIGH VARIANCE, poor generalization (Module 46).
   Toy data fully grown: the tree needs 5 leaves and depth 4 to reach
   training accuracy 1.0, isolating individual points:
      x <= 4.5 -> class 1 [0, 4];
      x > 4.5: x <= 9.5 -> (x <= 6.5 -> (x <= 5.5 -> class 0, else class 1),
      else class 0), x > 9.5 -> class 1.
   The splits at 5.5, 6.5 and 9.5 fit single points (x = 6 and x = 10
   labeled positive among negatives): likely noise.
EXPERIMENT (verified; synthetic data, scikit-learn 1.9):
   make_classification(n_samples = 600, n_features = 10, n_informative = 4,
   n_redundant = 2, flip_y = 0.1, class_sep = 1.0, random_state = 0), stratified
   70/30 split (random_state = 0), DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth = d):
     max_depth   train accuracy   test accuracy   leaves
        1           0.707            0.756            2
        2           0.781            0.739            4
        3           0.874            0.811            8
        4           0.931            0.861           12
        5           0.952            0.867           16
        8           0.979            0.856           28
       12 (full)    1.000            0.872           36
     Reading: depth 1-2 UNDERFIT (low training and test accuracy: high
     bias). Training accuracy keeps rising to 1.0 while the test accuracy
     levels off near 0.86-0.87: the gap (0.128 for the full tree) measures
     overfitting. Here the full tree is not catastrophically worse on test
     data, but it is far more complex (36 leaves) for no gain; with noisier
     data the test accuracy would fall.
CONTROLLING COMPLEXITY
   PRE-PRUNING (early stopping; hyperparameters): max_depth, min_samples_split,
      min_samples_leaf, max_leaf_nodes, min_impurity_decrease. Simple and
      fast, but may stop too early (a poor first split can hide a good
      second split).
   POST-PRUNING: grow a large tree, then cut back. COST-COMPLEXITY (weakest-
      link) PRUNING:
F44.6  COST-COMPLEXITY PRUNING
B. R_alpha(T) = R(T) + alpha |T|
   R(T): training error (misclassification rate or impurity) of tree T;
   |T|: number of leaves; alpha >= 0: the complexity penalty per leaf.
   For each alpha there is a unique smallest subtree minimizing R_alpha; as
   alpha increases the tree shrinks (alpha = 0: the full tree; large alpha:
   the root only). Choose alpha by CROSS-VALIDATION (Module 46/47).
C. SYMBOLS: R(T) the training error; |T| the number of leaves; alpha the
   penalty; T_alpha the best subtree for alpha.
D. CONCEPT: trade fit against size (Occam's razor); like L1 regularization on
   the number of leaves.
E. CONDITIONS: alpha is chosen by cross-validation; the nested sequence
   of subtrees comes from weakest-link pruning.
G. EXAMPLE (same experiment, 5-fold CV on the training set, ccp_alpha):
     alpha     CV accuracy   leaves   test accuracy
     0.00000     0.888        36        0.872
     0.00397     0.890        22        0.878
     0.00449     0.886        14        0.889
     0.01161     0.893         8        0.878
     0.02473     0.881         6        0.867
     0.04403     0.838         4        0.817
     0.10290     0.643         2        0.756
   The CV accuracy is essentially flat from 36 down to 8 leaves (0.888 to
   0.893): a much smaller tree does just as well, and is easier to
   interpret; cutting too far (2-4 leaves) hurts. (Differences of a few
   hundredths are within noise on 600 observations.)
H. INTERPRETATION: alpha is the price per leaf: higher alpha gives a
   smaller tree.
I. MISTAKES: choosing the tree size by training error (always favours the full
   tree); tuning ccp_alpha on the test set; ignoring that a pruned tree can
   still be unstable.
J. RELATED: regularization (section 43.8, F48.7), cross-validation
   (F46.2).
BIAS-VARIANCE VIEW: shallow tree = high bias, low variance; deep tree = low
bias, high variance; pruning trades them. A tiny change in the training
data can change the top split and thus the whole tree (instability): the
motivation for ensembles (Module 45).

ADVANTAGES AND LIMITATIONS OF DECISION TREES
   + Easy to interpret and visualize (rules); handle numeric and categorical
     features and (in some implementations) missing values; need no feature
     scaling; invariant to monotone transformations of features; capture
     nonlinearities and INTERACTIONS automatically (a split on x_2 inside a
     branch of x_1 = an x_1 by x_2 interaction); fast prediction.
   - High variance/unstable; greedy; axis-parallel boundaries (a diagonal
     boundary needs a staircase of many splits); piecewise-constant
     (no extrapolation, coarse for smooth relationships); biased toward
     features with many possible splits (high cardinality); poor
     probability calibration; class imbalance needs weights.
FEATURE IMPORTANCE (impurity-based): for feature j, sum the (weighted)
   impurity decreases of all splits on j, normalized to sum to 1. Fast but
   biased toward high-cardinality/continuous features and computed on the
   training data; prefer PERMUTATION importance on held-out data (shuffle a
   feature, measure the drop in score).
DATA SCIENCE LINK: trees are the base learner of random forests and gradient
boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM), which dominate tabular-data competitions.
Decision-tree rules also explain model behavior (surrogate models).
PYTHON
   from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier, export_text
   tree = DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=3, min_samples_leaf=5, ccp_alpha=0.0,
                                 random_state=0).fit(X_train, y_train)
   print(export_text(tree, feature_names=names, show_weights=True))
   tree.predict_proba(X_new); tree.feature_importances_; tree.get_depth()
   tree.cost_complexity_pruning_path(X_train, y_train)  # candidate alphas


========================================================================
D4. ENSEMBLE LEARNING
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 45: RANDOM FORESTS
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
One decision tree is unstable: a small change in the data can change it a
lot. But if you grow MANY different trees and let them vote, the
individual mistakes tend to cancel out: the "wisdom of crowds". A random
forest makes the trees different by giving each its own random resample of
the data and by letting each split see only a random subset of the
features.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
45.1 ENSEMBLE LEARNING
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITION: an ENSEMBLE combines several base models (weak/unstable
learners) to obtain a better prediction than any single one.
MAIN FAMILIES
   BAGGING (parallel, reduces VARIANCE): train independent models on
      different bootstrap samples; average/vote. Example: random forest.
   BOOSTING (sequential, reduces BIAS): each new model focuses on the previous
      models' errors; weighted sum. Example: AdaBoost, gradient boosting.
   STACKING: a meta-model learns how to combine different models' outputs.
WHY IT WORKS: averaging reduces random error provided the base models make
DIFFERENT errors (diversity) and are each better than chance.
   COMPARISON TABLE: BAGGING vs BOOSTING
     Aspect              Bagging / random forest          Boosting
     Training            independent trees (parallel)     sequential, each corrects the last
     Data per model      bootstrap sample                 re-weighted or residuals
     Base learners       deep, low-bias, high-variance    shallow, high-bias trees
     Main effect         reduces variance                 reduces bias (and some variance)
     Overfitting risk    low; more trees do not overfit   can overfit with too many rounds
     Tuning              few hyperparameters, robust      learning rate, rounds, depth matter
     Parallelism         easy (n_jobs)                    limited
     Examples            RandomForest, ExtraTrees         AdaBoost, GradientBoosting, XGBoost

------------------------------------------------------------------------
45.2 BOOTSTRAP SAMPLING AND BAGGING
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F45.1  BOOTSTRAP SAMPLE
A. NAME: Bootstrap sample.
B. DEFINITION: from a training set of n observations, draw n observations
   WITH replacement. Some observations appear several times, some not at
   all.
   Probability that a particular observation is NOT in a bootstrap sample:
        (1 - 1/n)^n  ->  e^(-1) = 0.3679   as n -> infinity.
   So about 63.2% of the distinct observations appear (the "in-bag"
   fraction), and about 36.8% are left out ("out-of-bag", OOB).
C. SYMBOLS: n sample size; each draw misses a given observation with
   probability 1 - 1/n; there are n independent draws.
D. CONCEPT: each bootstrap sample is a plausible alternative dataset from
   the same population (F37 bootstrap); training on many of them
   exposes how much a model depends on the particular sample.
E. CONDITIONS: the 36.8% is a large-n limit; for small n the exact value
   is (1 - 1/n)^n.
F. DERIVATION: P(observation i not chosen in one draw) = (n - 1)/n; in n
   independent draws, ((n - 1)/n)^n = (1 - 1/n)^n, and the limit is e^(-1)
   (F1.13, since (1 + x/n)^n -> e^x with x = -1).
G. EXAMPLE: n = 10: (0.9)^10 = 0.3487; n = 100: 0.3660; n = 1000: 0.3677; n = 10^6:
   0.36788; limit 0.36788. Expected number of distinct observations in a
   bootstrap sample of size 10: 10 (1 - 0.3487) = 6.51.
H. INTERPRETATION: about two thirds of the distinct cases are in each
   bootstrap sample; the rest are free test data (OOB).
I. MISTAKES: forgetting that bootstrap samples contain duplicates; thinking
   the bootstrap sample is smaller than n (it has n draws but only about
   0.632 n distinct cases).
J. RELATED: bagging (F45.2), OOB error (F45.4), bootstrap standard
   errors (section 37.6).

F45.2  BAGGING (BOOTSTRAP AGGREGATING)
A. NAME: Bagging.
B. PROCEDURE: for b = 1..B: draw a bootstrap sample D_b; fit a model T_b on
   D_b. Predict by AGGREGATING:
        regression:      y_hat(x) = (1/B) sum_b T_b(x)       (average)
        classification:  majority vote of the B predicted classes, or the
                         average of the predicted class probabilities
                         ("soft voting", usually better).
C. SYMBOLS: B the number of bootstrap models; T_b the b-th model; D_b
   its bootstrap sample.
D. CONCEPT: averaging B noisy but roughly unbiased predictors reduces
   variance.
   (The size of this variance reduction is given in F45.3 below.)
E. CONDITIONS: helps unstable (high-variance) learners such as deep
   trees, little for stable ones.
G. EXAMPLE: B = 5 regression trees predict 3.1, 2.9, 3.4, 3.0, 3.2 for a
   case: the bagged prediction is 15.6/5 = 3.12.
H. INTERPRETATION: averaging smooths the step functions of single trees.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: expecting bagging to reduce bias; using too few
   bootstrap models.
J. RELATED: random forests (section 45.3), variance of averages (F45.3),
   boosting (section 45.1).

F45.3  VARIANCE OF AN AVERAGE OF CORRELATED PREDICTORS
A. NAME: Why bagging reduces variance, and why correlation limits it.
B. FORMULA: if each tree prediction has variance sigma^2 and each PAIR of
   trees has correlation rho, then the average of B trees has
        Var( (1/B) sum_b T_b ) = rho sigma^2 + (1 - rho) sigma^2 / B.
C. SYMBOLS: sigma^2 variance of a single tree's prediction (at a fixed x, over
   random training sets/bootstrap draws); rho correlation between two
   trees; B number of trees.
D. CONCEPT: the second term vanishes as B grows (independent noise averages
   out); the first term, rho sigma^2, remains: trees trained on overlapping
   data are correlated, so you cannot average away their shared error.
   To improve further, DECORRELATE the trees (smaller rho): random feature
   selection.
E. CONDITIONS: assumes equal variance and a common pairwise correlation
   across trees (an idealization); rho is typically positive.
F. DERIVATION: Var(sum T_b) = sum Var(T_b) + sum_(b != c) Cov(T_b, T_c) = B sigma^2
   + B (B - 1) rho sigma^2. Divide by B^2: sigma^2/B + ((B - 1)/B) rho sigma^2 =
   rho sigma^2 + (1 - rho) sigma^2/B.
G. EXAMPLES (sigma^2 = 1): B = 1: 1.
      rho = 0:   B = 10: 0.10;  B = 100: 0.01  (independent: 1/B).
      rho = 0.3: B = 10: 0.37;  B = 100: 0.307; B = 1000: 0.3007 (floor 0.3).
      rho = 0.7: B = 10: 0.73;  B = 100: 0.703 (floor 0.7).
   Halving the correlation from 0.7 to 0.3 lowers the achievable variance
   floor from 0.7 to 0.3.
H. INTERPRETATION: more trees help only until the correlation floor; reducing
   rho (diverse trees) is worth more than adding trees beyond a few
   hundred.
I. MISTAKES: expecting variance to fall like 1/B for correlated trees;
   thinking bagging reduces bias (it keeps bias about the same;
   averaging does not fix systematic error).
J. RELATED: variance of sums (F36.3), random feature selection (section
   45.3).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
45.3 RANDOM FORESTS: RANDOM FEATURE SELECTION, VOTING, AGGREGATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
THE RANDOM FOREST ALGORITHM
   For b = 1..B:
     1. Draw a bootstrap sample of the training data.
     2. Grow a decision tree on it, DEEP (little or no pruning; low bias).
        At EACH split, consider only a random subset of m of the p features
        (chosen anew at every node) and pick the best split among those.
   Predict: classification by majority vote (or averaged probabilities),
   regression by averaging the trees' predictions.
RANDOM FEATURE SELECTION: the extra randomness (m < p) prevents a few strong
   predictors from dominating the top splits of every tree, so trees become
   less correlated (smaller rho in F45.3).
   DEFAULTS: m = sqrt(p) for classification, m = p/3 for regression (commonly;
   sklearn uses max_features = "sqrt" for classifiers and 1.0, i.e. all
   features, for regressors in recent versions: check the documentation);
   m = p gives plain bagging. Small m: more diversity but each tree weaker
   (higher bias); large m: stronger but correlated trees. Tune m.
CLASSIFICATION VOTING EXAMPLE: five trees predict classes 1, 0, 1, 1, 0 for a new
   case: majority vote -> class 1; estimated P(class 1) = 3/5 = 0.6 (the
   proportion of votes). Soft voting averages the trees' leaf proportions,
   e.g. leaf probabilities 0.9, 0.2, 0.7, 0.6, 0.4 -> mean 0.56.
REGRESSION AGGREGATION EXAMPLE: three trees predict 3.1, 2.9, 3.4: forest
   prediction (3.1 + 2.9 + 3.4)/3 = 3.133. The forest is a smoother
   function than any single tree (average of step functions).
BIAS AND VARIANCE
   Trees are grown deep: LOW BIAS, HIGH VARIANCE individually. Bagging/
   random forest: bias about the same as a single tree (slightly higher
   because each tree sees about 63% distinct data and restricted features),
   variance much lower (F45.3). Net effect: lower expected error for
   unstable learners. For stable learners (linear regression, k-NN with
   large k) bagging helps little. A forest cannot extrapolate (like its trees).
INTERPRETABILITY: much lower than one tree; use permutation importance,
   partial dependence plots, SHAP.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
45.4 OUT-OF-BAG (OOB) EVALUATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F45.4  OUT-OF-BAG ERROR
A. NAME: Out-of-bag (OOB) estimate of generalization error.
B. PROCEDURE: for each training observation i, collect the trees whose
   bootstrap sample did NOT contain i (about 36.8% of the B trees);
   predict y_i using ONLY those trees (vote/average); compare with the
   true y_i. The OOB error = average loss over all observations (OOB
   accuracy = fraction correct).
C. SYMBOLS: i an observation; the OOB trees are those whose bootstrap
   sample excluded i; y_hat_i^OOB its OOB prediction.
D. CONCEPT: each observation is "test data" for the trees that never saw it,
   so the OOB prediction is an honest out-of-sample prediction obtained
   for free, without a separate validation set or repeated training. It
   behaves much like leave-one-out / k-fold cross-validation (but with
   forests of about 0.37 B trees per prediction instead of B).
E. CONDITIONS: needs enough trees (B large) so every observation is OOB for
   several trees; slightly pessimistic for small B (each OOB prediction
   uses fewer trees); unbiased only if the model/hyperparameters were
   NOT tuned on the same OOB score (tuning on it makes it optimistic;
   Module 47). It is not valid for dependent data without care (grouped/time
   series).
G. EXAMPLE (verified; the synthetic data of Module 44: 420 training and 180
   test cases, RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators = 300,
   oob_score = True, random_state = 0)):
     training accuracy 1.000 (the forest memorizes the bootstrap samples,
        a useless estimate of performance);
     OOB accuracy 0.921;
     test accuracy 0.894;
     a single fully grown tree: test accuracy 0.872.
   OOB and test accuracy agree to within about 0.03 (the difference is
   sampling noise on 180 test cases: SE about 0.023).
H. INTERPRETATION: report OOB (or CV) accuracy, never training accuracy, for a
   random forest.
I. MISTAKES: using forest training accuracy as performance; using OOB after
   tuning hyperparameters on it and reporting it as final; too few trees.
J. RELATED: cross-validation (F46.2), bootstrap (F45.1), tuning bias
   (section 47.4).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
45.5 NUMBER OF TREES; COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY
------------------------------------------------------------------------
NUMBER OF TREES (n_estimators)
   More trees do NOT cause overfitting: the variance term (1 - rho) sigma^2/B
   decreases and then plateaus (F45.3); the forest converges to a limiting
   predictor. The cost is time and memory (linear in B). Choose B where the
   OOB error stabilizes (often 100-500; 1000+ for stable importance
   estimates).
   VERIFIED (same data, RandomForestClassifier, bootstrap = True):
       B        OOB accuracy   test accuracy
        5          0.776          0.833
       10          0.845          0.856
       25          0.883          0.878
       50          0.902          0.889
      100          0.912          0.900
      300          0.921          0.894
   The OOB accuracy rises steadily and flattens (with few trees many
   observations have no or few OOB trees, which also depresses the early
   values); test accuracy fluctuates by about +/-0.01 around 0.89-0.90.
   MAX_FEATURES (m), 300 trees: m = 1: OOB 0.907, test 0.894; m = 3 (= sqrt(10)):
   0.921, 0.894; m = 10 (= bagging): 0.919, 0.883. Moderate m worked
   best; all features (plain bagging) gave slightly lower accuracy,
   consistent with the decorrelation argument. Bagging of full trees: test
   0.883, OOB 0.919.
COMPUTATIONAL COMPLEXITY (n observations, p features, B trees, m features per
   split, tree depth d about log n for balanced trees)
   TRAINING: B x O(m n log n x d) roughly (each node sorts only m features);
      TREES ARE INDEPENDENT, so training is EMBARRASSINGLY PARALLEL across
      trees (n_jobs = -1; Module 28). Boosting is sequential.
   PREDICTION: B x O(d) per observation (also parallelisable).
   MEMORY: B x (number of nodes); fully grown trees have O(n) nodes each,
      so the forest has O(B n) nodes: large for big n.
   Speed-ups: restrict max_depth/min_samples_leaf, max_samples (subsample
   size), fewer trees, histogram binning, early OOB monitoring.
   Practical hyperparameters: n_estimators, max_features, max_depth,
   min_samples_leaf (reduces variance/size), max_samples, class_weight
   (imbalance), bootstrap, criterion, random_state (reproducibility).
ADVANTAGES: strong default performance with little tuning; robust to
   outliers and irrelevant features; handles mixed feature types and
   interactions; built-in OOB validation and importances; easy to
   parallelize. LIMITATIONS: less interpretable; large models; cannot
   extrapolate; biased MDI importances (use permutation importance:
   in the experiment above impurity importances were 0.221, 0.185,
   0.149, 0.117, 0.108, 0.099, 0.035, 0.029, 0.028, 0.028 for the ten
   features, i.e. concentrated on about six); slower than a single tree;
   probability estimates need calibration; may be beaten by gradient
   boosting.
COMPARISON TABLE: SINGLE TREE vs RANDOM FOREST
  Aspect             Single decision tree        Random forest
  Variance           high (unstable)             low (averaged)
  Bias               low if deep                 about the same as one deep tree
  Interpretability   high (rules)                low (use importances)
  Training cost      one tree                    B trees (parallel)
  Prediction         one path                    B paths + vote/average
  Overfitting        severe if unpruned          resistant; more trees safe
  Validation         needs held-out/CV           OOB available free
  Typical accuracy   baseline                    usually clearly better
DATA SCIENCE LINK: forests are a strong baseline for tabular data; use OOB
and cross-validation (Module 46), tune max_features/min_samples_leaf
(Module 47); the same idea (diversity through resampling) appears in
dropout, snapshot ensembles and bootstrap confidence intervals.
PYTHON
   from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
   rf = RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=300, max_features="sqrt",
                               oob_score=True, n_jobs=-1, random_state=0)
   rf.fit(X_train, y_train)
   rf.oob_score_; rf.score(X_test, y_test); rf.feature_importances_
   from sklearn.inspection import permutation_importance


========================================================================
D5. MODEL VALIDATION AND SELECTION
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 46: MODEL EVALUATION
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
A model is only useful if it works on NEW data it has never seen. A model
can look perfect on its training data simply by memorizing it. Model
evaluation is the discipline of measuring honestly how well a model will
perform in the future, and of choosing between models without fooling
ourselves.
NOTE: the experiments quoted in Modules 46 and 47 were actually run
(Python 3.12, scikit-learn 1.9, NumPy 2.x, fixed random seeds); exact
numbers vary with versions and seeds, but the qualitative patterns are
robust.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.1 TRAINING ERROR, TEST ERROR AND GENERALIZATION ERROR
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   LOSS L(y, y_hat): the penalty for predicting y_hat when the truth is y
      (squared error, 0-1 loss, log-loss).
   TRAINING ERROR: average loss on the data used to fit the model:
        Err_train = (1/n) sum[i in train] L(y_i, f_hat(x_i)).
   TEST ERROR: average loss on a held-out set never used for fitting or
      tuning: an ESTIMATE of the generalization error.
   GENERALIZATION ERROR (true/expected error): the expected loss on a NEW
        observation from the same population:  Err = E[ L(Y, f_hat(X)) ].
        It cannot be computed exactly; we estimate it from held-out data.
   VALIDATION ERROR: error on held-out data used for MODEL SELECTION/tuning
      (it becomes optimistic once used for decisions).
   GENERALIZATION GAP = test error - training error (usually positive).
COMPARISON TABLE: TRAINING ERROR vs TEST ERROR
  Aspect              Training error               Test error
  Data                data used to fit the model   fresh data, untouched by fitting/tuning
  Tends to be         optimistic (too low)         honest (unbiased) estimate
  As complexity rises always decreases (memorizes) U-shaped: falls, then rises
  Use                 diagnose underfitting;       report performance; compare
                      monitor optimization         final models
  Danger              mistaking it for quality     reusing test set repeatedly

F46.1  BIAS-VARIANCE DECOMPOSITION OF EXPECTED TEST ERROR
A. NAME: Bias-variance decomposition (squared-error loss).
B. FORMULA: for y = f(x) + eps with E[eps] = 0, Var(eps) = sigma^2, and a model
   f_hat trained on a random training set, at a fixed point x:
        E[ (y - f_hat(x))^2 ] = sigma^2 + Bias[f_hat(x)]^2 + Var[f_hat(x)]
   Bias[f_hat(x)] = E[f_hat(x)] - f(x);  Var[f_hat(x)] = E[ (f_hat(x) - E f_hat(x))^2 ],
   the expectations taken over random training sets.
C. SYMBOLS: sigma^2 IRREDUCIBLE noise (cannot be removed by any model);
   Bias^2 systematic error from a model too simple to capture f; Variance
   sensitivity of the fit to the particular training sample.
D. CONCEPT: complex (flexible) models have low bias but high variance;
   simple models have high bias but low variance. Total test error is
   minimized at an intermediate complexity: the BIAS-VARIANCE TRADE-OFF.
E. CONDITIONS: holds for squared-error loss with additive noise
   independent of the model; analogous decompositions exist for other
   losses.
F. DERIVATION (sketch): add and subtract E[f_hat] and f inside the square; cross
   terms vanish because eps is independent of f_hat and E[f_hat - E f_hat] =
   0 (same device as MSE = Var + Bias^2, F37.6).
G. EXAMPLE (verified simulation): y = sin(2 pi x) + noise (sigma = 0.3, so
   sigma^2 = 0.09), polynomial regression, n = 30 training points, 500
   independent training sets, evaluated at x = 0.3:
        degree 1: Bias^2 = 0.315, Var = 0.016  (underfit: huge bias)
        degree 3: Bias^2 = 0.004, Var = 0.011  (balanced; total 0.104 with noise)
        degree 9: Bias^2 = 0.000, Var = 0.026  (more variance; total 0.116)
   Each total includes sigma^2 = 0.09: the noise floor that no model can beat.
H. INTERPRETATION: underfitting = high bias; overfitting = high variance.
   Bagging reduces variance (Module 45); boosting reduces bias; regularization
   trades bias for variance (Module 47).
I. MISTAKES: forgetting the irreducible term; thinking low training error
   means low bias AND low variance; trying to get test MSE below sigma^2.
J. RELATED: MSE of estimators (F37.6), decision-tree depth (Module 44).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.2 UNDERFITTING AND OVERFITTING
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITIONS
   UNDERFITTING: the model is too simple to capture the real pattern; BOTH
      training and test error are high (high bias).
   OVERFITTING: the model fits noise/idiosyncrasies of the training set;
      training error is very low but test error is much higher (high
      variance; large generalization gap).
   GOOD FIT: training and test error both low and close.
VERIFIED EXAMPLE (polynomial regression; the same data-generating process,
   n = 30 training points, 2,000 fresh test points; noise variance 0.09):
        degree   train MSE   test MSE
          1        0.370       0.327     underfit (both high)
          2        0.356       0.356     underfit
          3        0.099       0.107     good fit (test about noise + small error)
          5        0.041       0.141     overfit: train falls, test rises
          9        0.040       0.143     overfit
         15        0.040       0.143     overfit
   Training MSE decreases monotonically with complexity (0.370 to 0.040)
   while test MSE is U-shaped with its minimum at degree 3. The model with
   the lowest TRAINING error (degree 15) is far from the best on new data.
   (Training MSE below the noise variance 0.09 is a red flag: the model
   is fitting the noise.)
DIAGNOSIS AND REMEDIES
   Underfitting: add features/interactions, increase model capacity, reduce
      regularization, train longer.
   Overfitting: more data, simpler model, regularization (ridge/lasso,
      pruning, dropout), early stopping, feature selection, bagging, cross-
      validation to choose complexity.
   LEARNING CURVES (error vs training-set size): a large persistent gap
      between train and validation error means variance (get more data /
      simplify); both curves high and converged means bias (richer model).
SIGNS TO WATCH: training accuracy 100% with much lower test accuracy; test
   error that worsens as a hyperparameter (depth, degree, epochs) grows.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.3 TRAIN/TEST SPLIT
------------------------------------------------------------------------
PROCEDURE: randomly split the data once, e.g. 70-80% training, 20-30% test
(sklearn train_test_split(X, y, test_size = 0.2, random_state = 0,
stratify = y)). Fit everything (preprocessing, feature selection, model)
on the TRAINING part; evaluate once on the TEST part.
   THREE-WAY SPLIT: training (fit parameters) / validation (choose
   hyperparameters, early stopping) / test (final, untouched estimate). Or use
   cross-validation instead of a fixed validation set.
RULES: the test set must represent the future deployment population;
   split BEFORE any learning step (imputation, scaling, encoding, feature
   selection); never tune on the test set; stratify for classification;
   for time series split by time (train on past, test on future);
   for grouped data (several rows per patient/customer) keep each group
   entirely in one side (GroupShuffleSplit), otherwise the model sees the
   same entity in training and test (leakage).
LIMITATIONS: one split gives a noisy estimate (depends on which rows land
   in test); with a test set of n_t cases and accuracy p the SE is about
   sqrt(p(1 - p)/n_t): for p = 0.9, n_t = 100 SE = 0.03 (a 95% interval of
   about +/-6 points); wasting data for small n.
MISTAKES: reporting results from a split that was chosen because it looked
good; random split of time-ordered data; duplicates present in both
sets; evaluating many models on the same test set and picking the best
(it becomes a validation set).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.4 K-FOLD CROSS-VALIDATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F46.2  K-FOLD CROSS-VALIDATION
A. NAME: k-fold cross-validation (CV).
B. PROCEDURE AND FORMULA
     1. Shuffle the data and split it into k folds of (about) equal size.
     2. For each fold j = 1..k: train on the other k - 1 folds, compute the
        error E_j on fold j.
     3. CV estimate:  CV_k = (1/k) sum[j=1..k] E_j.
     Fold-to-fold spread (SD of E_j) indicates the uncertainty; the standard
     error of the CV estimate is roughly SD/sqrt(k) but UNDERESTIMATES the
     true uncertainty because folds share training data (the E_j are
     positively correlated).
     Cost: k model fits. Common choices k = 5 or 10; k = n is
     LEAVE-ONE-OUT (LOOCV: almost unbiased but expensive and high variance);
     REPEATED k-fold (several random partitions) reduces variance due to the
     particular partition.
C. SYMBOLS: k number of folds; E_j error (or accuracy) on held-out fold j.
D. CONCEPT: every observation is used for testing exactly once and for
   training k - 1 times, giving a more stable estimate than a single split
   and using the data efficiently. All data contribute to the estimate.
E. CONDITIONS: observations should be (approximately) exchangeable; for
   dependent data use GROUP k-fold or TIME-SERIES splits. The model is
   RETRAINED within each fold (all preprocessing inside the loop; see
   leakage). The final model is refit on ALL data after selection.
   BIAS-VARIANCE OF THE ESTIMATOR: small k (2-3) trains on much less data
   than the final model (pessimistic bias); large k (LOOCV) has low bias
   but higher variance and cost; k = 5-10 is a good compromise.
G. EXAMPLE (verified): synthetic classification data (300 observations, 10
   features, logistic regression), 5-fold CV:
        fold accuracies 0.917, 0.867, 0.883, 0.867, 0.850;
        mean 0.877, SD 0.025 (naive SE = 0.025/sqrt 5 = 0.011).
        10-fold CV: mean 0.880. Repeated 5-fold (10 repeats = 50 fits): mean
        0.888 (SD of the 50 fold scores 0.037).
   The individual fold scores vary by +/-0.03 even though the model is the
   same: a single 60-case test fold is a noisy measurement.
H. INTERPRETATION: report mean and spread of the fold scores (e.g. "0.877 +/-
   0.025"); do not over-interpret differences smaller than the spread
   between competing models.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: fitting scalers/imputers/feature selectors on the whole
   dataset before CV (leakage); using the CV score of the BEST configuration
   as a performance estimate (selection bias, Module 47); forgetting to
   shuffle sorted data (see below); reporting the SE as SD/sqrt(k) as if
   folds were independent; using random k-fold on time series or grouped
   data.
J. RELATED: bootstrap and OOB (Modules 45, 47); nested CV (Module 47).

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.5 STRATIFIED CROSS-VALIDATION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
IDEA: in each fold keep the same class proportions as the full dataset. It
reduces the variance of the estimates for classification, and is essential
for imbalanced classes or small data, where a plain split can produce folds
with very few (or zero) minority cases.
EXAMPLE (verified): 100 cases, 10 positives (10%), 5 folds, number of
positives in each TEST fold:
   KFold without shuffling (data sorted by class):  [10, 0, 0, 0, 0]
   KFold with shuffling:                            [3, 2, 4, 0, 1]
   StratifiedKFold:                                 [2, 2, 2, 2, 2]
   The first scheme is useless (one test fold contains all positives, the
   training folds for that fold none); even random folds can contain zero
   positives (precision/recall undefined); stratification guarantees 2 per
   fold.
USE: StratifiedKFold(n_splits = 5, shuffle = True, random_state = 0); for
   regression stratify on binned targets (optional); for grouped data
   StratifiedGroupKFold.
LIMIT: stratification fixes class proportions only; it does not remove
   dependence between observations from the same group.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.6 DATA LEAKAGE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
DEFINITION: DATA LEAKAGE occurs when information that would NOT be available
at prediction time (or information from the test data) influences model
training or selection. It produces evaluation results that are too
optimistic and models that fail in deployment.
WHY IT PRODUCES MISLEADING RESULTS: the evaluation no longer measures
performance on truly unseen data. The model is "told the answer", directly
(target leakage) or indirectly (test statistics seeping into
preprocessing).
TYPES AND EXAMPLES
   1. PREPROCESSING LEAKAGE (train-test contamination): computing the mean/sd
      for scaling, imputation values, PCA components, or encodings on the
      whole dataset before splitting; test-set information enters
      training. Fix: fit on train, transform test; use Pipelines.
   2. FEATURE-SELECTION LEAKAGE (verified, striking): data of 100 cases and 2,000
      features of PURE NOISE with random labels (true accuracy = 50%).
        WRONG: select the 20 features most associated with the label using
        ALL the data, then run 5-fold CV on those 20 features:
        accuracy = 0.81 (!).
        RIGHT: put feature selection INSIDE the CV pipeline (make_pipeline
        (SelectKBest(k = 20), LogisticRegression())): accuracy = 0.52, the
        truth.
      The selection step saw the labels of the "test" folds, so noise
      features that happened to correlate with those labels were chosen.
   3. TARGET LEAKAGE: a feature that is a consequence of the target or is
      recorded after the event: predicting loan default using "number of
      collection calls"; predicting hospital readmission using the discharge
      note written after the outcome; "total_refund" in a churn model;
      an ID/time stamp correlated with the label.
   4. TEMPORAL LEAKAGE: using future information (random split of time series;
      rolling features computed with future values; resampling/target
      encoding with future data).
   5. DUPLICATE/GROUP LEAKAGE: the same patient, customer, image or sentence
      (or near-duplicates) appearing in both training and test folds.
   6. TUNING ON THE TEST SET (repeated peeking); using the OOB or CV score of
      the tuned model as the final estimate (Module 47).
   7. LABEL-DERIVED FEATURES: target encoding or class-based imputation done
      without proper cross-fitting.
SYMPTOMS: suspiciously high performance (accuracy 99%, AUC near 1.0), a
   single feature with unreasonable importance, performance collapse in
   production, results that depend on the order/IDs.
PREVENTION CHECKLIST
   [ ] Split first (respecting time and groups), then do everything else.
   [ ] Put ALL data-dependent steps inside a Pipeline evaluated by CV.
   [ ] For each feature ask: "Would I know this value at prediction time?"
   [ ] Audit top features for implausible predictive power.
   [ ] Keep a final test set untouched until the very end.
   [ ] Check for duplicates across splits.
I. MISTAKE: assuming leakage only means "using the target as a feature".

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.7 MODEL COMPARISON AND GENERALIZATION PERFORMANCE
------------------------------------------------------------------------
HOW TO COMPARE MODELS HONESTLY
   1. Same data splits for all candidates (paired comparison; repeated CV).
   2. Same preprocessing rules inside CV; same evaluation metric chosen for
      the problem.
   3. Include a BASELINE: majority class / mean predictor, a simple linear
      or logistic model. A complex model must beat it meaningfully.
   4. Consider variability: differences smaller than the fold-to-fold SD (or
      the CV SE) are not evidence. Use repeated CV with a paired test, with
      caution: CV folds share data, so naive paired t-tests are too liberal;
      use the corrected resampled t-test (Nadeau-Bengio), McNemar's test
      (same test set, paired classification outcomes), or report
      intervals/bootstrap.
   5. Prefer the SIMPLEST model within one standard error of the best (the
      "one-standard-error rule").
   6. Consider cost, latency, interpretability, fairness and robustness, not
      only accuracy.
GENERALIZATION PERFORMANCE: the model's accuracy on the population it will
actually face; estimated by test error / CV / OOB provided no leakage,
a representative split and no tuning on the evaluation data. Expect
degradation under DISTRIBUTION SHIFT (covariate shift, label shift,
concept drift): monitor in production.
BOOTSTRAP ESTIMATE OF PERFORMANCE (preview of Module 47): train on a bootstrap
sample, test on the out-of-bag cases (about 36.8%), repeat many times.
(Verified: for 200 training cases, resubstitution accuracy 1.00; 10-fold CV
0.895; bootstrap-OOB 0.888; accuracy on 19,800 held-out cases 0.891.)

------------------------------------------------------------------------
46.8 EVALUATION METRICS FOR CLASSIFICATION AND REGRESSION
------------------------------------------------------------------------
(Emphasis topic of the study plan; complements the syllabus.)
F46.3  CONFUSION MATRIX AND DERIVED CLASSIFICATION METRICS
A. NAME: Confusion matrix; accuracy, precision, recall, specificity, F1.
B. DEFINITIONS (positive class = the event of interest)
                          Predicted positive   Predicted negative
     Actual positive           TP                   FN
     Actual negative           FP                   TN
     Accuracy      = (TP + TN)/(TP + TN + FP + FN)
     Precision (PPV) = TP/(TP + FP)           of the cases flagged, how many are real
     Recall (sensitivity, TPR) = TP/(TP + FN) of the real positives, how many are found
     Specificity (TNR) = TN/(TN + FP);   FPR = FP/(FP + TN) = 1 - specificity
     F1 = 2 * precision * recall/(precision + recall) (harmonic mean);
        F_beta weights recall beta times as much as precision.
     Balanced accuracy = (recall + specificity)/2.
     Matthews correlation MCC = (TP TN - FP FN)/sqrt((TP + FP)(TP + FN)(TN + FP)(TN + FN)).
C. SYMBOLS: TP true positives; FP false positives; FN false negatives; TN true
   negatives.
D. CONCEPT: different mistakes have different costs. Precision cares about
   false alarms; recall about misses; there is a trade-off controlled by
   the decision threshold.
E. CONDITIONS: choose the metric from the business cost; accuracy is
   misleading when classes are imbalanced.
G. WORKED EXAMPLE: 200 cases: TP = 40, FP = 10, FN = 20, TN = 130.
      accuracy = 170/200 = 0.850
      precision = 40/50 = 0.800
      recall = 40/60 = 0.667
      specificity = 130/140 = 0.929
      F1 = 2 (0.8)(0.667)/(1.467) = 0.727
      balanced accuracy = (0.667 + 0.929)/2 = 0.798
      MCC = (40*130 - 10*20)/sqrt(50*60*140*150) = 5000/7937 = 0.630.
   ACCURACY PARADOX: a fraud dataset with 1% fraud; "predict never fraud" gets
   99% accuracy, recall 0, F1 0: useless. Report precision/recall/PR curve
   for rare events. (Link: PPV and prevalence, F34.6: a classifier with
   recall 0.9 and specificity 0.99 at prevalence 0.1% has precision only
   8.3%.)
H. INTERPRETATION: precision is the reliability of positive calls;
   recall is the coverage of the true positives.
I. MISTAKES: using accuracy on imbalanced data; averaging F1 across classes
   without stating the average (macro/micro/weighted); comparing precision
   across datasets with different prevalence; computing metrics on training
   data.
J. RELATED: PPV (F34.6), ROC and AUC (F46.4), threshold choice.
F46.4  THRESHOLDS, ROC CURVE AND AUC
B. A probabilistic classifier outputs scores s(x); predict positive if s >=
   threshold t. Sweeping t from 1 to 0 traces:
     ROC curve: (FPR(t), TPR(t)); PR curve: (recall(t), precision(t)).
   AUC (area under the ROC curve) = P( score of a random positive > score of a
   random negative ) (ties count 1/2). 0.5 = random, 1 = perfect.
C. SYMBOLS: s(x) the score; t the threshold; TPR, FPR the true and false
   positive rates; AUC the area under the ROC curve.
D. CONCEPT: AUC measures ranking quality independent of the threshold. For
   rare positives the PR curve/average precision is more informative
   (ROC is insensitive to prevalence).
E. CONDITIONS: needs scores that rank cases; AUC ignores calibration and
   class prevalence.
G. EXAMPLE: positive scores 0.9, 0.8, 0.4; negative scores 0.7, 0.3, 0.2, 0.1 (3 x 4 =
   12 pairs). 0.9 beats all 4; 0.8 beats all 4; 0.4 beats 3 (0.3, 0.2, 0.1)
   and loses to 0.7: AUC = 11/12 = 0.917.
   Threshold example: 10 cases with scores 0.95, 0.85, 0.80, 0.70, 0.60, 0.55,
   0.40, 0.30, 0.20, 0.10 and labels 1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0, 0, 0:
     t = 0.5: TP 4, FP 2, FN 0, TN 4: precision 0.667, recall 1.0;
     t = 0.75: TP 2, FP 1, FN 2, TN 5: precision 0.667, recall 0.5.
     (Raising the threshold lowers recall; AUC of this ranking is 0.875.)
H. INTERPRETATION: AUC = 0.9 means a random positive outranks a random
   negative 90% of the time.
I. MISTAKES: choosing the threshold 0.5 by default (tune it to the cost and
   prevalence, on validation data); reporting AUC when calibrated
   probabilities are needed (AUC ignores calibration).
J. RELATED: confusion matrix (F46.3), Mann-Whitney U statistic,
   precision-recall curves.
F46.5  LOG-LOSS (CROSS-ENTROPY) AND CALIBRATION
B. log-loss = -(1/n) sum [ y_i ln p_i + (1 - y_i) ln(1 - p_i) ]; punishes
   confident wrong predictions severely. Example: y = (1, 0, 1), p =
   (0.9, 0.2, 0.6): -(ln 0.9 + ln 0.8 + ln 0.6)/3 = (0.1054 + 0.2231 + 0.5108)/3
   = 0.2798. Predicting p = 0.5 always gives ln 2 = 0.693.
   Calibration: among cases with predicted probability about 0.7, about 70%
   should be positive (reliability diagram; Platt/isotonic scaling).
C. SYMBOLS: y_i in {0, 1} the true label; p_i the predicted probability
   of class 1; n the number of cases.
D. CONCEPT: the average negative log-probability assigned to the true
   class; confident wrong predictions cost heavily.
E. CONDITIONS: probabilities must lie in (0, 1) (clip to avoid ln 0);
   the multi-class version uses -ln p(true class).
H. INTERPRETATION: a log-loss of 0.693 equals random guessing at p =
   0.5.
I. COMMON MISTAKES: feeding class labels instead of probabilities;
   ignoring calibration.
J. RELATED: MLE (F38.3), cross-entropy (Appendix D), logistic regression
   (F48.1).
F46.6  REGRESSION METRICS
B. MSE = (1/n) sum (y_i - y_hat_i)^2;  RMSE = sqrt(MSE) (same units as y);
   MAE = (1/n) sum |y_i - y_hat_i| (robust);  R^2 = 1 - SSE/SST (relative to
   predicting the mean; can be negative on test data); MAPE = mean |error/y|
   (undefined at y = 0).
C. SYMBOLS: y_i the true values; y_hat_i the predictions; n the number
   of cases; y_bar the mean of y.
D. CONCEPT: MSE and RMSE penalize large errors heavily; MAE treats all
   errors proportionally; R^2 compares the model with the mean
   predictor.
E. CONDITIONS: R^2 on new data compares with the training-mean baseline
   and can be negative; MAPE needs y != 0.
G. EXAMPLE: y = (3, 5, 2), y_hat = (2.5, 5.5, 1): errors -0.5, +0.5, -1:
   MSE = (0.25 + 0.25 + 1)/3 = 0.500; RMSE = 0.707; MAE = 0.667;
   SST = (3 - 3.333)^2 + (5 - 3.333)^2 + (2 - 3.333)^2 = 4.667; R^2 = 1 - 1.5/4.667
   = 0.679.
H. INTERPRETATION: RMSE = 0.71 means typical errors are about 0.7 units.
I. MISTAKES: comparing RMSE across different target scales; using R^2 on
   training data; MSE dominated by outliers (use MAE/Huber when needed).
J. RELATED: OLS (F40.2), R^2 (F40.3), loss functions (F43.1).


========================================================================
D6. HYPERPARAMETER OPTIMIZATION
========================================================================

========================================================================
MODULE 47: MODEL TUNING
========================================================================
BASIC IDEA (plain English)
Most models have "knobs" that you must set BEFORE training: tree depth, the
strength of regularization, the number of neighbors. Model tuning is the
search for good knob settings. The danger is that, by trying many
settings and keeping the best score, you fool yourself about how good the
model really is. The whole module is about searching efficiently and
reporting honestly.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
47.1 MODEL PARAMETERS vs HYPERPARAMETERS
------------------------------------------------------------------------
COMPARISON TABLE: PARAMETERS vs HYPERPARAMETERS
  Aspect            Model parameters                 Hyperparameters
  Meaning           numbers LEARNED from data        settings chosen BEFORE/around training
  Set by            the training algorithm           the analyst or a tuning procedure
                    (OLS, gradient descent)          (grid/random search, CV)
  Examples          regression coefficients,         learning rate, tree depth, number of
                    tree split thresholds, neural    trees, k in k-NN, ridge lambda, SVM C
                    network weights, class priors    and gamma, batch size, epochs
  Optimized on      training loss                    validation (CV) score
  How many          from a few to billions           typically 2-10
  Stored in         fitted model (coef_, tree_)      constructor arguments (get_params)
  Effect            define the fitted function       control complexity/behavior
                                                     (bias-variance trade-off)
  Example (ridge)   beta (learned)                   lambda (the penalty weight)
  Example (forest)  split features/thresholds        n_estimators, max_features, max_depth
  KEY RULE: hyperparameters must NOT be chosen by minimizing TRAINING error
  (it always prefers the most flexible model); choose by validation/CV
  performance.
THE TWO-LEVEL VIEW: inner problem: for fixed hyperparameters h, find parameters
theta(h) that minimize the training loss; outer problem: find h that
minimizes the validation loss of theta(h). Tuning is optimization of a
noisy black-box function of h (no gradient available; expensive: every
evaluation trains a model).
SEARCH SPACES: the set of values to explore for each hyperparameter:
   - SCALE: use a LOG scale for hyperparameters spanning orders of
     magnitude (learning rate 1e-5 to 1; ridge lambda; SVM C 1e-2 to 1e3;
     gamma): sample log-uniformly (scipy loguniform) so that 0.001 and
     0.1 are equally likely to be explored.
   - TYPE: integers (depth 1-20), categorical (kernel linear/rbf),
     continuous (fraction), conditional (gamma only matters for the RBF
     kernel).
   - RANGE: wide enough to bracket the optimum (if the best value is at the
     boundary of the grid, extend the grid).
   - Start coarse and refine around good regions.

------------------------------------------------------------------------
47.2 GRID SEARCH
------------------------------------------------------------------------
F47.1  GRID SEARCH
A. NAME: Grid search (exhaustive search).
